Instantons on ALE spaces for classical groups, involutions on quiver varieties, and quantum symmetric pairs
Hiraku Nakajima

Abstract
. Moduli spaces of instantons on ALE spaces for classical groups are examples of fixed point sets of involutions on quiver varieties, i.e., σ \sigma -quiver varieties. In 2018 Yiqiang Li considered their equivariant cohomology, and by stable envelope of Maulik-Okounkov, constructed representations of coideal subalgebras of Maulik-Okounkov Yangian, called twisted Yangian. We calculate K K -matrices as matrices in examples, identified the twisted Yangians with ones studied in other literature, and clarify conditions which we should impose to make them well-defined.



1.
Introduction

In 1992, the author
[
Nak94b
]
discovered a relation between homology of moduli spaces of
instantons on ALE spaces and representations of affine Kac-Moody Lie algebras.
1
1

1

Announced in
[
Nak92
]
.

There have been many developments since then, extensions both in geometry and representation theory sides.
One of applications of these developments was an explicit character formula
of arbitrary simple modules of quantum loop algebras
[
Nak04
]
.
See
[
NW23
, Appendix B]
for reduction of non-simply laced cases to simply laced cases.
The formula itself, like in the case of Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials, involves
only combinatorial inputs, but its proof used geometry.
No alternative proof is known up to this day.

Most of developments are restricted to the case when the gauge group is unitary,
or moduli spaces of coherent sheaves, quiver varieties on geometric side,
and quantum loop algebras or Yangians on representation theory side.
The focus of this paper is to study examples when the gauge group is a classical group,
i.e., a special orthogonal or symplectic group.
Let us recall sporadic earlier results in this direction.

When the underlying ALE space is
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}
,
Braverman, Finkelberg and the author equipped
the equivariant intersection cohomology of the moduli space of

G
-instantons
with a structure of a representation of the affine
\mathscr{W}
-algebra
associated with
{\mathfrak{g}}=\operatorname{Lie}G

[
BFN16
]
.
Here the moduli space is, more precisely, Uhlenbeck partial compactification of the

genuine
moduli space, which has singularities. And
G
could
be even an exceptional group, but assumed to be of type ADE.
This construction was motivated by the extension of the Alday-Gaiotto-Tachikawa correspondence

[
AGT10
]
to arbitrary
G

[
KMST12
]
.
When the underlying ALE space is of type A, it is expected that the
same result holds where the affine
\mathscr{W}
-algebra is replaced by
the coset vertex algebra
[
NT11
]
.
But it is not clear
what is the corresponding algebra for ALE spaces of type D, E.

In 2018, Yiqiang Li
[
Li19
]
considered an involution on
a quiver variety of finite type, which is a moduli space of
unitary instantons on ALE spaces by
[
KN90
]
. He called
the fixed point set a
\sigma
-quiver variety
, and equipped
its equivariant cohomology with a structure of a representation of a coideal subalgebra
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
of the Maulik-Okounkov Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
of the corresponding quiver variety.
(We will show that Maulik-Okounkov Yangian is isomorphic to the extended Yangian
[
Wen18
]
associated with
the corresponding simple Lie algebra.)
An example of involutions was given earlier in
[
Nak03
]
, and
the fixed points set is the moduli space of orthogonal or symplectic instantons on ALE spaces,
but Li considered more general involutions.
We will work in his generality in this paper, hence study
\sigma
-quiver varieties.
The type of a
\sigma
-quiver variety is classified by (i) the type of the underlying Dynkin graph,
(ii) a choice of a diagram involution
\sigma^{\prime}
, possibly trivial, and (iii) a choice of the type
(+)
or
(-)
.
Moduli spaces of
\operatorname{\rm SO}
and
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons correspond to the case when
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
, and
(+)
or
(-)
is
\operatorname{\rm SO}
and
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
respectively.

However, one should keep in mind that we assume
\sigma
-quiver varieties are
smooth
,
hence the classification above is not complete.
We exclude examples in
[
BFN16
]
, as they are singular.
Therefore, generalization of this paper to singular
\sigma
-quiver varieties is an important future problem.
For instantons on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, the deformed affine
\mathscr{W}
-algebras of classical type
are related to a coideal subalgebra of the quantum toroidal algebra
type
\mathbf{U}_{q}(\mathbf{L}\hat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})

[
FJMV21
]
.
This result suggests that our construction should have
K
-theoretic analogue,
even though we need to deal with singular moduli spaces.

Li’s construction is based on the stable envelope of Maulik-Okounkov
[
MO19
]
.
The stable envelope gives a geometric construction of the
R
-matrix on
the equivariant cohomology of quiver varieties, and the Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
was
constructed by the
RTT
construction
[
RTF89
]
.
Similarly, Li constructed
K
-matrices using stable envelope,
and the coideal subalgebra
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
is given by matrix coefficients of
K
-matrices.
This construction is similar to one for twisted Yangian by Olshanski, Molev-Ragoucy, MacKay and others.
(See the textbook by Molev
[
Mol07
]
for the construction.)
Note that coideal subalgebras of quantum groups have been studied
in the context of quantum symmetric pairs, which are quantizations of symmetric pairs, i.e.,
pairs of Lie algebras and their fixed point subalgebras under an involution.
Twisted Yangians are quantization of the fixed point subalgebras of the current algebras

{\mathfrak{g}}[z]
of complex simple Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The involution is in the form
xu^{k}\mapsto\sigma(x)(-u)^{k}
for
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
k\geq 0
,
where
\sigma
is an involution on
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
See
[
BR17
]
and references therein.

Li’s construction is
abstract
, in the sense that
K
-matrices are defined as linear operators
on the localized equivariant cohomology of
\sigma
-quiver varieties.
In a sense, the
K
-matrices should be called
K
-
linear maps
.
See
2.13
.
In order to compare his twisted Yangian with the one in other literature,
we need to calculate
K
-matrices explicitly as
matrices

after choosing bases of the localized equivariant cohomology.
This is the main purpose of this paper.
We will find that Li’s construction indeed give twisted Yangians in the literature,
Olshanski twisted Yangian and Molev-Ragoucy reflection equation algebra.
See
6.21
and
7.4
.
The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is determined by (i), and the involution
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{g}}

is the composite of the Chevalley involution of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}
. Both are
independent of (iii). See
5.17
.
The embedding
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}\to{\mathsf{X}}
depends on the data (iii) in general.

In the course of our study, we found that an assumption in Li’s paper,

a(W^{2})=W^{2}
in
[
Li19
, §5.2, the first paragraph]
,
is unnecessary.
This is important for our purpose, as moduli spaces of orthogonal and symplectic instantons
do not satisfy this condition in general. See
section

6
.

We also find that we need to impose a compatibility condition on the polarization, which is
a choice of a square root of the Euler class of the normal bundle of the fixed point set
of a torus action, in order to make
K
-matrices well-defined. See
sections

4(v)
and
6(iii)

and Assumption
5.4
.
This is a
new
phenomenon, not seen in the case of
R
-matrices from quiver varieties.
It is an interesting question to understand the homology group when this condition is
not
satisfied.

We find a polarization satisfying the compatibility condition in
type A, D or
\mathrm{E}_{6}
with
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
,
(+)
type, and

\mathrm{A}_{1}
with
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
,
(-)
type.
We also find a polarization in type A with
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\operatorname{id}
for both
(+)
and
(-)
types.
For type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
(
\ell>2
),
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
and
(-)
type, we show that there is no choice of polarization
satisfying the compatibility condition.
In the remaining case, we do not know whether a compatible polarization exists or not.

Except Li’s assumption mentioned above, and the compatibility condition on the polarization,
the only new part in this paper is calculation of
K
-matrices in examples.

There are earlier works on geometric realization of twisted Yangians and
coideal subalgebras of quantum groups

[
Li21
,
DM24
,
SW24
]
,
and references therein.
Their approaches are different from Li’s one, hence from ours.
They are based on explicit presentations of coideal subalgebras, given
under
\iota
quantum group program
[
Wan23
]
. In particular,
the larger algebra, containing the coideal subalgebra,
does
not
play any role in their approaches.
2
2

2

In
[
DM24
]
it was shown that equivariant cohomology in these cases
are representations of the pre-twisted Yangian, defined by generators and relations.
We show that the same cohomology groups are representations of
the reflection equation algebra
[
Mol07
, §2.16 Example 4]
. But,
relation between pre-twisted Yangian and reflection equation algebra
is not known to the author.

Moreover, all these papers use cotangent bundles of partial
flag varieties, which are special cases of
\sigma
-quiver varieties,
studied in
section

7
.
However, they were studied in the spirit of
\iota
quantum group program,
namely relation between
\sigma
-quiver varieties and quiver varieties
played no direct role in their works.
Also, our approach can be applied to more examples, even though we still impose the strong assumption
that
\sigma
-quiver varieties are smooth.

There is also another work
[
DL25
]
.
It is an orthosymplectic analogue of the cohomological Hall algebra approach to the upper half subalgebra
of the Yangian
[
SV13
,
KS11
]
. For the Yangian, cohomological Hall algebra has a
representation on the equivariant cohomology of moduli spaces of unitary instantons or quiver varieties

[
YZ18
,
SV20
]
.
One of key properties of relation between two constructions was the claim that a quiver variety is an open
subset of the moduli
stack
of representations of the preprojective algebra.
This claim has no obvious analogue for
\sigma
-quiver varieties.
On representation theory side, it is not clear what is the upper half subalgebra of the twisted Yangian.
Hence the relation between two constructions is not clear.

Another, more technical motivation of the author to study moduli spaces of orthogonal and symplectic instantons
is an issue in the definition of de Campos Affonso’s symmetric bow varieties
[
dCA18
]
.
They were introduced as candidates of Coulomb branches of quiver gauge theories
of type B, C, D, but do not have expected properties in general.
Our study of moduli spaces resolves the issue.
We will mention relation to Coulomb branches at several places in the main text.

As we mentioned above, geometric realization of Yangian and quantum loop algebras
has applications to representation theory. We expect that our result will have similar applications to representation theory of
twisted Yangians. Many ingredients used in
[
Nak04
]
have analogues in our setting.
For example,
t
-analogue of Knight’s spectral characters (aka
q
-characters) of standard modules

[
Nak01a
, §13.5]
have analogue as generating functions of
Poincaré polynomials of the fixed point sets of a
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on

\sigma
-quiver varieties.
One of important tools, not discussed in this paper, is the Grassmann bundle
in
[
Nak98
, §4]
,
[
Nak01a
, §5.4]
.
It is an interesting direction to pursue, as the Grassmann bundle
is an essential ingredient of geometric realization of Kashiwara crystals.
See e.g.,
[
Nak01b
, §4]
for a derivation of the tensor product rule.

We focus on the twisted Yangian in this paper, and leave modification to quantum coideal
subalgebras to future works. We need to replace equivariant cohomology by equivariant
K
-theory.
If we restrict ourselves to smooth
\sigma
-quiver varieties as in this paper, the only issue
is to clarify the condition on the polarization. If we want to deal with more examples of
quantum symmetric pairs, we need to consider singular
\sigma
-quiver varieties.
Then the definition of
K
-theoretic stable envelope should be more involved.

The paper is organized as follows.
In
section

2
we introduce the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
following

[
Wen18
]
.
In
section

3
we introduce an involution on a quiver variety
following
[
Li19
]
. We consider the involution for both generic
stability parameter and zero stability parameter, although the latter
one plays no significant role in this paper. They give rise examples
of gauge theories, whose Coulomb branches are expected to be interesting.
See
section

3(ix)
.
In
section

4
we introduce the stable envelope
[
MO19
]

and show that
{\mathsf{X}}
is isomorphic to one given by the stable envelope.
Contrary to other parts of this paper, we also discuss the case of affine quivers.
See
section

4(ix)
.
In
section

5
we introduce
K
-matrices and define
the twisted Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
following
[
Li19
]
.
In
section

6
we study the
K
-matrices for moduli spaces of
\operatorname{\rm SO}
and
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons
on ALE spaces in type A in detail.
We observe that there is
no polarization satisfying the condition
5.4

for
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
-instantons. On the other hand, the twisted Yangian is
Olshanski twisted Yangian for
\operatorname{\rm SO}
-instantons.
In
section

7
we study the
K
-matrices for cotangent bundles of partial flag varieties
of type BCD.
This is the case when
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\operatorname{id}
.
We show that the twisted Yangian is Molev-Ragoucy reflection equation algebra.
In
appendix

A
we summarize the ADHM description of classical group instantons
on ALE spaces.

Convention

Transpose

Suppose
C\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
is a linear map from a finite dimensional
complex vector space
V_{1}
to another space
V_{2}
. Let
V_{1}^{*}
,

V_{2}^{*}
denote the dual spaces of
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
. The transpose

\prescript{t}{}{C}\colon V_{2}^{*}\to V_{1}^{*}
of
C
is defined by

\langle Ce,f\rangle=\langle e,\prescript{t}{}{C}f\rangle
for

e\in V_{1}
,
f\in V_{2}^{*}
. We have
\prescript{t}{}{(}\prescript{t}{}{C})=C
under
the natural isomorphism
V_{1}^{**}\cong V_{1}
,
V_{2}^{**}\cong V_{2}
.

Adjoint

Let
\varepsilon
be
1
or
-1
. An
\varepsilon
-form
on a finite dimensional
complex vector space
V
is a non-degenerate form
(\ ,\ )
such that

(x,y)=\varepsilon(y,x)
for
x
,
y\in V
. Namely it is an orthogonal form
if
\varepsilon=1
, and a symplectic form if
\varepsilon=-1
. Throughout this
paper, an
orthogonal
form means a non-degenerate symmetric
form.

Let
G_{\varepsilon}(V)
denote the subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}(V)
preserving the

\varepsilon
-form. Namely
G_{\varepsilon}(V)=\operatorname{\rm O}(V)
if
\varepsilon=1
,
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(V)
if
\varepsilon=-1
.
We denote it simply by
G(V)
if the
\varepsilon
-form on
V
is clear
from the context.
We denote the connected component of
G(V)
containing
\operatorname{id}
by

G^{0}(V)
.
The corresponding Lie algebra is denoted by
\mathfrak{g}_{\varepsilon}(V)
or

\mathfrak{g}(V)
. It is
\operatorname{\mathfrak{so}}(V_{2})
(
\varepsilon=1
) or
\operatorname{\mathfrak{sp}}(V_{2})
(
\varepsilon=-1
).

Suppose that
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
are equipped with
\varepsilon
-forms

(\ ,\ )_{V_{1}}
,
(\ ,\ )_{V_{2}}
respectively. We define the

adjoint

C^{*}
of a linear map
C\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
by

(Cv_{1},v_{2})_{V_{2}}=(v_{1},C^{*}v_{2})_{V_{1}}\qquad v_{1}\in V_{1},v_{2}\in V_{2}.

We then have
(C^{*})^{*}=C
.

Suppose
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
are equipped with
\varepsilon
-form and
(-\varepsilon)
-form
respectively. We define
C^{*}
by the same formula as above. We then have

(C^{*})^{*}=-C
.

If we identify
(\ ,\ )_{V_{1}}
with
\varphi_{1}\colon V_{1}\to V_{1}^{*}
by

\langle v_{1},\varphi_{1}(v_{1}^{\prime})\rangle=(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime})_{V_{1}}
, and similarly for
V_{2}

with
\varphi_{2}
, we have
C^{*}=\varphi_{1}^{-1}\,\prescript{t}{}{C}\,\varphi_{2}
.

2.
Extended Yangian

In this section, we introduce the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
of
Drinfeld Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}

[
Dri85
]
.
The definition was given in
[
Wen18
]
, but it appears in
earlier literature in various forms. For example, in
[
Mol07
]
,

{\mathsf{Y}}
appears as
\mathrm{Y}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
, while
{\mathsf{X}}
appears as

\mathrm{Y}(\mathfrak{gl}_{N})
.
We introduce
{\mathsf{X}}
as we will prove that the Maulik-Okounkov Yangian
[
MO19
]
in
type ADE is isomorphic to
{\mathsf{X}}
in
section

4(vii)
.

2(i).
Yangian

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a finite-dimensional complex simple Lie algebra.
We will assume that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of type ADE later, but we do not need this assumption at the moment.
We fix an invariant orthogonal form
(\ ,\ )
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
We normalize it so that
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})=2
for
long
simple roots
\alpha_{i}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
This is
the
normalized invariant form used for untwisted affine Lie algebras
in
[
Kac90
, §6]
.
Since our primary interest is in the case when
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of type ADE, all
roots have the same length. Hence we do not need to pay much attention to our
convention.

Let
\Bbbk={\mathbb{C}}[\hbar]
. The Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
is a
\Bbbk
-algebra defined by
generators
X
and
J(X)
for
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
with the certain defining relations, including

XY-YX=[X,Y],J([X,Y])=[X,J(Y)],\text{$J$ is linear in $X$},\dots

as in
[
Dri85
, Theorem 2]
.
It contains the universal enveloping algebra
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}})
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
as a subalgebra, generated
by
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.
For later convenience, we recall the Casimir elements

(2.1)

\Omega=\sum_{\lambda}X_{\lambda}\otimes X_{\lambda},\quad\omega=\sum_{\lambda}X_{\lambda}X_{\lambda},

where
\{X_{\lambda}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
{\mathfrak{g}}
with respect to
(\ ,\ )
.
It appears in the formula for the coproduct, e.g.,

\Delta J(h_{i})=J(h_{i})\otimes 1+1\otimes J(h_{i})+\frac{\hbar}{2}[h_{i,0},\Omega].

See
[
GNW18
, (4.11)]
.

Let
c_{\mathfrak{g}}
denote the eigenvalue of
\omega
on the adjoint representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
In other words, it is the ratio of the Killing form and
(\ ,\ )
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Under our normarlization of
(\ ,\ )
,
it is equal to twice of the dual Coxeter number of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. See
[
Kac90
, Ex. 6.2]
. It appears in the definition of the antipode:

(2.2)

S(X)=-X,\quad S(J(X))=-J(X)+\frac{\hbar}{4}c_{\mathfrak{g}}X.

See
[
Wen18
, (3.5)]
.

There is another presentation of
{\mathsf{Y}}
, called
a new realization
,
given in
[
Dri87
, Theorem 1]
. It is generated by
x^{\pm}_{i,r}
,
h_{i,r}
for

i\in I
,
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, where
I
is the index set of vertices of the Dynkin diagram corresponding to
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
We will use the convention in
[
GNW18
, Def. 2.1]
, but
the details of the defining relations are not important in this paper.
The subalgebra
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}})
is generated by
x_{i,0}^{\pm}
,
h_{i,0}
. They basically coincide
with standard generators of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, but rescaled according to
(\ ,\ )
, say

h_{i}=\frac{(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
for a simple coroot
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
.
The equivalence of the two presentations is explicit, and is due to Drinfeld, and the
details are available in
[
GRW19
]
. One of formulas in the equivalence is

h_{i,1}=J(h_{i})-\hbar v_{i}\quad\text{ where }v_{i}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}(\alpha,\alpha_{i})\{x_{\alpha}^{+},x_{\alpha}^{-}\}-\frac{1}{2}h_{i}^{2},

and
\{a,b\}=ab+ba
.

The Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
is equipped with an ascending filtration

{\mathsf{Y}}_{\leq 0}\subset{\mathsf{Y}}_{\leq 1}\subset{\mathsf{Y}}_{\leq 2}\subset\cdots

defined by

\deg X=0
,
\deg J(X)=1
for
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The associated graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}{\mathsf{Y}}={\mathsf{Y}}_{\leq 0}\oplus{\mathsf{Y}}_{\leq 1}/{\mathsf{Y}}_{\leq 0}\oplus\cdots

is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}[u])

of the current algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}[u]={\mathfrak{g}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Bbbk[u]
of

{\mathfrak{g}}
.
3
3

3

It is isomorphic to the algebra given by
\hbar=0
, and tensored with
\Bbbk
over
{\mathbb{C}}
.
In Maulik-Okounkov Yangian, which makes sense for arbitrary quiver
including Jordan quiver, this is
not
the case. Indeed,
Maulik-Okounkov Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
, in general,
is defined
a priori
only over
\Bbbk
.

The embedding
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}})\to{\mathsf{Y}}
specializes to the embedding
{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}[u]
at the associated graded.

Yangian is a Hopf algebra, in particular has a coproduct
\Delta
. Its
definition is given in the original presentation. The
details about the proof of the well-definedness of
\Delta
are available,
if
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
, and more generally affine Lie algebras
for
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq A^{(1)}_{1}
,
A^{(2)}_{2}
, in
[
GNW18
, Th. 4.9]
.

It is known that
{\mathsf{Y}}
has a one parameter family of Hopf algebra
automorphism
\tau_{\zeta}
for
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}
. See
[
GNW18
, §3.2]
.
It gives a translation
u\mapsto u+\zeta
on the associated graded algebra
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}[u])
.
From (
2.2
), we have

(2.3)

S^{2}=\tau_{-\frac{\hbar}{2}c_{\mathfrak{g}}}.

See
[
Wen18
, Cor. 3.3]
. This will play an important role later in comparison
with the extended Yangian and Drinfeld Yangian.

Yangian has a distinguished family of irreducible representations
\{F_{i}\}

indexed by
i\in I
.
Following
[
Nak01b
, §1.4]
, we call them
\ell
-fundamental representations
.
They are called
fundamental representations
in
[
CP91
]
.
It should be noted that their restriction to
{\mathfrak{g}}
are not irreducible in general,
hence different from the fundamental representations of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
To avoid confusion, we introduce the
new
terminology, which is almost the same as the original one.
Let us denote it by
\rho_{F_{i}}
:

\rho_{F_{i}}\colon{\mathsf{Y}}\to\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk}(F_{i}).

One way to define them
is to specify the Drinfeld polynomials as

P_{i}(u)=u
,
P_{j}(u)=1
for
j\neq i
. See
[
CP91
, §2.13]
.
It means that
F_{i}
has a distinguished vector
v_{i}
, called the
\ell
-highest weight vector
, such that

\begin{split}&{\mathsf{Y}}v_{i}=F_{i},\qquad x^{+}_{j,r}v_{i}=0\quad\text{for $j\in I$, $r\geq 0$},\\
&\left(1+\hbar\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}h_{j,r}u^{-r-1}\right)v_{i}=\left(\frac{P_{j}(u+\frac{\hbar}{2}\frac{(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2})}{P_{j}(u-\frac{\hbar}{2}\frac{(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2})}\right)^{-}v_{i}\quad\text{for $j\in I$},\end{split}

where
(\ )^{-}
means the expansion at
u=\infty
.
These conditions fixes
v_{i}
uniquely up to a scalar multiple. We choose and fix
v_{i}
hereafter.

In type ADE, they are constructed explicitly as the equivariant cohomology of the
lagrangian subvarieties in the quiver variety (
[
Var00
]
). In order to follow the convention in
[
MO19
]
, we define it
as the equivariant cohomology of the whole quiver variety:

(2.4)

F_{i}=H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i}))=\bigoplus_{{\mathbf{v}}}H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},\varpi_{i})).

The construction will be reviewed briefly later in
section

4(vii)
.
Strictly speaking, this is different from one given by the lagrangian subvarieties.
But they are isomorphic if we invert
\hbar
. It is also known that the above

F_{i}
is the dual module of one given by the lagrangian subvarieties, see
[
Nak01a
, Th. 7.3.5]
.

The component
{\mathfrak{M}}(0,\varpi_{i})
is a single point, hence we have a distinguished class

1\in H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}({\mathfrak{M}}(0,\varpi_{i}))
. We take it as the
\ell
-highest weight vector
v_{i}
of
F_{i}
,
in type ADE.

It is known that
F_{i}
is free of finite rank over
\Bbbk
.
See
[
Nak01a
, §7]
.
We fix a
\Bbbk
-base of
F_{i}
.

2(ii).
R
-matrix

Let us consider
F_{i}[u]=F_{i}\otimes\Bbbk[u]
, where

\otimes
is over
\Bbbk
hereafter.
We can make it a
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module through
\tau_{u}
:
\left.F_{i}[u]\right|_{u=a}
is
the
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module
F_{i}
pull-backed by
\tau_{a}
for
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

The tensor product
F_{i}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{j}[u_{2}]
has an
R
-matrix (
[
Dri85
, Th. 4]
and
[
GTLW21
, Th. 7.2]
for a published proof):

Fact 2.5
.

There exists
R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u_{1}-u_{2})\in\operatorname{End}(F_{i}\otimes F_{j})
depending
rationally on
u_{1}-u_{2}
with
R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(\infty)=\operatorname{id}
such that

(12)R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u_{1}-u_{2})\colon F_{i}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{j}[u_{2}]\to F_{j}[u_{2}]\otimes F_{i}[u_{1}]

intertwines the
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module structures on both sides. Here
(12)
is the
exchange of the first and second factors.
Moreover, it comes from the universal
R
-matrix
{\mathscr{R}}(u)\in 1+\frac{\hbar}{u}{\mathsf{Y}}\otimes{\mathsf{Y}}[[u^{-1}]]
by
evaluation up to rescaling by an element
f(u)\in 1+u^{-1}\Bbbk[[u^{-1}]]
.

We normalize the
R
-matrix so that the tensor product of the
\ell
-highest
weight vectors of
F_{i}[u_{1}]
,
F_{j}[u_{2}]
is preserved.
Since this normalized
R
-matrix is different from one from the universal
R
-matrix,
let us denote the universal
R
-matrix by
{\mathscr{R}}^{\mathrm{uni}}(u)
.
Both will play a role below.

The
R
-matrices satisfy the Yang-Baxter equation:

(2.6)

\begin{multlined}R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u_{1}-u_{2})R_{F_{1},F_{3}}(u_{1}-u_{3})R_{F_{2},F_{3}}(u_{2}-u_{3})\qquad\\
\qquad=R_{F_{2},F_{3}}(u_{2}-u_{3})R_{F_{1},F_{3}}(u_{1}-u_{3})R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u_{1}-u_{2})\end{multlined}

as rational operators on
F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes F_{3}[u_{3}]
,
where
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
,
F_{3}
are chosen among
\{F_{i}\}
.

2(iii).
Monodromy matrices

For a sequence of indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}
, we define a
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module by

(2.7)

{\mathscr{H}}=F_{i_{1}}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{i_{2}}[u_{2}]\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}}[u_{n}],

where the
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module structure
is given by the coproduct
\Delta
.

Choose
F
among
\{F_{i}\}
as an auxiliary space, we introduce a
monodromy
matrix:

(2.8)

\begin{split}{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}R_{F[u],{\mathscr{H}}}&=R_{F,F_{i_{n}}}(u-u_{n})R_{F,F_{i_{n-1}}}(u-u_{n-1})\cdots R_{F,F_{i_{1}}}(u-u_{1})\\
&\in\operatorname{id}+\frac{\hbar}{u}\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk[u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{n}]}(F\otimes{\mathscr{H}})[[u^{-1}]].\end{split}

In the view point in
[
MO19
, §4.2.4]
,
this operator moves from the chamber

\{u>u_{1}>u_{2}>\cdots>u_{n}\}
to the chamber

\{u_{1}>u_{2}>\cdots>u_{n}>u\}
in the space of cocharacters.
The first step goes from
\{u>u_{1}\}
to
\{u_{1}>u\}
, hence

R_{F,F_{i_{1}}}(u-u_{1})
. Then continue.
In terms of tensor product representations, it intertwines
between
F\otimes(F_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}})
and

(F_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}})\otimes F
after swapping the two factors.

By the base of
F
, we regard
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
as a matrix of size
=\dim F
whose entries are
elements of
\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk[u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{n}]}({\mathscr{H}})[[u^{-1}]]
.
We run all
{\mathscr{H}}
, hence consider
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)-\operatorname{id}
as elements of

\frac{\hbar}{u}\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk[u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{n}]}({\mathscr{H}})[[u^{-1}]]
.
So we do not include
{\mathscr{H}}
in the notation of
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
.
Note also that
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
, once
{\mathscr{H}}
is fixed, is a rational function in
u-u_{1}
, …,
u-u_{n}
by its construction.
It is regular at
u=\infty
, the expansion at
u=\infty
gives a formal power series in
u^{-1}
as above.

2(iv).
RTT
relation

Let us extend the action of
{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)

(resp.
{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{j}}(v)
) to

F_{i}[u]\otimes F_{j}[v]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}
by the identity on the
remaining factor
F_{j}[v]
(resp.
F_{i}[u]
).
From the Yang-Baxter equation, one derives the
RTT
relation:

(2.9)

R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{j}}(v)={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{j}}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u-v),

where
R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u-v)
acts by the identity on the factor
{\mathscr{H}}
.

2(v).
Extended Yangian

We choose a finite dimensional representation
V
of
{\mathsf{Y}}
as

\bigoplus_{i\in I}F_{i}
, and apply
the construction
[
Wen18
, §5]
of the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
.
The index set
\mathcal{I}
, appearing as
X_{\mathcal{I}}({\mathfrak{g}})
in the notation of the extended Yangian in
[
Wen18
]
,
is nothing but
I
in our choice of
V
:
By
[
Wen18
, §4.3]
,
\mathcal{I}
is the index set of a chosen base of
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathsf{Y}}}(V)
. In our case,
we choose
\{\operatorname{id}_{F_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
as a base of
\operatorname{End}_{{\mathsf{Y}}}(V)
.
We then formulate the construction as follows.

Definition 2.10
.

The
extended Yangian

{\mathsf{X}}
is the algebra generated by elements

\{t_{i;ab}^{(r)}\mid i\in I,a,b=1,\dots,\dim F_{i},r\geq 1\}
, subject to the
defining
RTT
relations (
2.9
) viewed
as an equality in
\operatorname{End}(F_{i}\otimes F_{j})\otimes{\mathsf{X}}[u,u^{-1}]][[v^{-1}]]
.
Here
{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)=\operatorname{id}_{F_{i}}+\left(\sum_{r}t_{i;ab}^{(r)}u^{-r-1}\right)_{a,b}

is regarded as an element of
\operatorname{End}(F_{i})\otimes{\mathsf{X}}[[u^{-1}]]

under the chosen base of
F_{i}
.

The extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
has a representation on
F
for one of
\{F_{i}\}
given by

(2.11)

\rho_{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}\colon{\mathsf{X}}\to\operatorname{End}(F);\qquad{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)\mapsto R_{F_{i},F}(u).

The
RTT
relation follows from the Yang-Baxter equation.

Definition 2.12
.

We define an ascending
filtration
on
{\mathsf{X}}
by the degree of
u
,
i.e.,
{\mathsf{X}}_{\leq N}
is generated by coefficients of
\hbar u^{-n-1}
with
n\leq N
.

Remark 2.13
.

In the conventional
RTT
approach to the extended Yangian, one starts
with
R
, given as an
explicit matrix
, rather than a
linear operator

on a vector space, which has no specific choice of a base. One can avoid
the use of Drinfeld’s definition of
{\mathsf{Y}}
, and define the extended Yangian
directly in this approach. See
[
Mol07
]
.
In this approach, people usually assume
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of classical type, and take

V
as a vector representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
In particular, this construction
explains
that the vector representation
is an example of
\ell
-fundamental representations, which
are also fundamental representations of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
See
2.21
below.

2(vi).
Hopf algebra structure

One has a Hopf algebra structure on
{\mathsf{X}}
with coproduct
\Delta
,
antipode
S
, and counit
\varepsilon
given by

\Delta({\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u))=({\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)\otimes 1)(1\otimes{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)),\quad S({\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u))={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)^{-1},\quad\varepsilon({\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u))=\operatorname{id}_{F_{i}}.

2(vii).
Translation automorphism

We have a family of automorphisms
\tau_{\zeta}\colon{\mathsf{X}}\to{\mathsf{X}}
for
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}
given by

{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)\mapsto{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u-\zeta).

By the coproduct and translation automorphisms, we equip
{\mathscr{H}}=F_{i_{1}}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{i_{2}}[u_{2}]\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}}[u_{n}]
in (
2.7
) with a
{\mathsf{X}}
-module
structure. Thus we have a homomorphism

\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\rho^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathscr{H}}\colon{\mathsf{X}}\to\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk[u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{n}]}({\mathscr{H}}).

In
[
MO19
]
the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
is defined as
a subalgebra of the right hand side of the above equation.
Since the above homomorphism
\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\rho^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathscr{H}}
is injective, say by

[
MO19
, §5.5.3]
, there is no difference in practice.

2(viii).
Homomorphism to the Yangian

We have a homomorphism
\tilde{\Phi}\colon{\mathsf{X}}\to{\mathsf{Y}}
given by

(2.14)

\tilde{\Phi}\colon{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)\mapsto(\rho_{F_{i}}\otimes 1)({\mathscr{R}}^{\mathrm{uni}}(u)).

Recall
\rho_{F_{i}}\colon{\mathsf{Y}}\to\operatorname{End}(F_{i})
is the representation of
{\mathsf{Y}}
on
F_{i}
. This is a surjective homomorphism
of Hopf algebras by
[
Wen18
, Lemma 6.1]
.
It is also a filtered homomorphism, as shown in the proof of
[
Wen18
, Th. 6.2]
.
It intertwines the translation automorphisms
\tau_{\zeta}
of
{\mathsf{X}}
and
{\mathsf{Y}}
, thanks
to a property of the universal
R
-matrix
[
Wen18
, (3.10)]
.

Remark that the definition above uses the universal
R
-matrix, not the normalized one.
This is necessary since we want to get a homomorphism to
{\mathsf{Y}}
.

Let us define

(2.15)

Z_{F_{i}}(u)=S^{2}({\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u+\frac{\hbar}{2}c_{\mathfrak{g}})^{-1}\in\operatorname{End}(F_{i})\otimes{\mathsf{X}}[[u^{-1}]].

By (
2.3
), it is killed by
\tilde{\Phi}
, i.e.,
\tilde{\Phi}(Z_{F_{i}}(u))=1
.

Since our normalized
R
-matrix differs from the universal one by a rescaling
by
f(u)
, we see that
Z_{F_{i}}(u)
is
f(u)f(u+\frac{\hbar}{2}c_{\mathfrak{g}})^{-1}
times
the identity operator in the representation
F_{i}
of
{\mathsf{X}}
defined by (
2.11
).
We write
Z_{F_{i}}(u)=\operatorname{id}_{F_{i}}\otimes z_{F_{i}}(u)
with
z_{F_{i}}(u)\in{\mathsf{X}}[[u^{-1}]]
.
It has the expansion
z_{F_{i}}(u)=1+z_{i}^{(2)}u^{-2}+z_{i}^{(3)}u^{-3}+\cdots
.
There is no term of
u^{-1}
by the definition. See
[
Wen18
, Lemma 7.5]
.
Since
z_{F_{i}}(u)
commutes with any elements of
{\mathsf{X}}
in
{\mathscr{H}}
,
it is contained in the center.
See also
[
Mol07
, Th. 1.9.9]
for
{\mathfrak{g}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{N}
.

Furthermore, we have

Theorem 2.16

(
[
Wen18
, Th. 6.2, Prop. 7.6, Th. 7.3]
)
.

(1)

Let
{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
be the quotient
{\mathsf{X}}/(z_{F_{i}}(u)-1)
.
Here
(z_{F_{i}}(u)-1)
is the ideal generated by matrix entries of coefficients
of
z_{F_{i}}(u)-1
for all
i\in I
.
Then
\tilde{\Phi}
induces an isomorphism

{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}\xrightarrow[\cong]{\Phi}{\mathsf{Y}}.

(2)
The center of
{\mathsf{X}}
is isomorphic to the polynomial
ring of generators
z_{i}^{(r)}
for
i\in I
,
r\geq 2
, appeared in the expansion
of
z_{F_{i}}(u)
.

(3)
The extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
is isomorphic to the tensor
product

{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}\,\otimes\,\Bbbk[z_{i}^{(r)}\mid i\in I,r\geq 2].

2(ix).
Extended Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
be the linear span of matrix entries of coefficients of
\hbar u^{-1}
of
{\mathsf{T}}(u)-\operatorname{id}
.
From the expansion of the
R
-matrix as

R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u)=\operatorname{id}+\frac{\hbar}{u}{\mathsf{r}}_{F_{i},F_{j}}+O(u^{-2}),

where
{\mathsf{r}}_{F_{i},F_{j}}\in\operatorname{End}(F_{i}\otimes F_{j})
is the
classical
R
-matrix
,
4
4

4

We follow
the
unconventional
terminology of the classical
R
-matrix,
taken in
[
MO19
, §4.8.4]
.

one can show that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
forms a Lie subalgebra.
See
[
Wen18
, §4]
,
[
MO19
, §5.3]
.
One of important ingredients in the proof is that the classical
R
for the
universal
{\mathscr{R}}^{\mathrm{uni}}(u)
is the Casimir operator
\Omega
in (
2.1
).

Indeed, the extended Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is

{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus\mathfrak{z}_{I}
where
\mathfrak{z}_{I}
is the
abelian Lie algebra of dimension
|I|
.
See
[
Wen18
, (4.25)]
.
Note that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is naturally equipped with a
representation on
V=\bigoplus_{i\in I}F_{i}
. We define
\delta_{i}\in\mathfrak{z}_{I}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
as the identity
operator on
F_{i}
, extended by
0
to the other
F_{j}
for
j\neq i
.
It means that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is the
maximal
realization
of the Lie algebra, used in the context of Kac-Moody algebras.
See
[
HK02
, Remark 2.1.2]
. (
d_{i}
there is

\delta_{i}+\sum{\mathbf{C}}^{ij}h_{j}
where
({\mathbf{C}}^{ij})
is the inverse of the
Cartan matrix
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}}_{ij})
.)
See also
[
MO19
, §5.3.4]
. The Cartan subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
has a basis

\{h_{i},\delta_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. It is convenient if we regard
the fundamental weights
\varpi_{i}
and simple roots
\alpha_{i}
as linearly independent
elements of
({\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathrm{ext}})^{*}
by
\langle\delta_{i},\varpi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
,

\langle\delta_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=0
, while

\langle h_{i},\varpi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
,
\langle h_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=a_{ij}
.

In this situation, contrary to more general cases in
[
MO19
]
,
the extended Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is defined over
{\mathbb{C}}
, instead of
\Bbbk
.

We have the following result, which has been known for many versions of Yangian.

Theorem 2.17

(
[
Wen18
, Th. 7.7]
)
.

The associated graded algebra of
{\mathsf{X}}
with respect to the filtration defined in

2.12
is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u])

of the current algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u]={\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Bbbk[u]
.

2(x).
Recovering the Yangian

Let
f_{i}(u)=1+f_{i}^{(1)}u^{-1}+f_{i}^{(2)}u^{-2}+\cdots\in 1+u^{-1}\Bbbk[[u^{-1}]]
be a formal power series in
u^{-1}

starting with
1
.
We consider a collection
{\mathbf{f}}(u)=(f_{i}(u))_{i\in I}
of such series.
It defines an automorphism of
{\mathsf{X}}
by

(2.18)

m_{\mathbf{f}}\colon{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)\mapsto f_{i}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)\quad\text{for $i\in I$}.

See
[
Wen18
, Lemma 5.3]
.

In our realization of
{\mathsf{X}}
in
\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}})[[u^{-1}]]
,
the action of
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
on
the summand
{\mathscr{H}}=F_{i_{1}}[u_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}}[u_{n}]
is multiplied by

f_{i_{1}}(u-u_{1})f_{i_{2}}(u-u_{2})\dots f_{i_{n}}(u-u_{n})
, as
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
acts
through the coproduct
\Delta
in
section

2(vi)
.

On
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, more precisely, the lowest part of the filtration
{\mathsf{X}}_{\leq 0}
,
it is given by

\text{elements in ${\mathfrak{g}}$ is fixed},\quad\delta_{i}\mapsto\delta_{i}+f_{i}^{(1)}\operatorname{id}.

In particular, the
m_{{\mathbf{f}}}
-fixed part of
{\mathsf{X}}_{\leq 0}

is the corresponding part in Drinfeld Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
.

In order to extend this observation to the whole
{\mathsf{X}}
, we prepare
rescaled operators of
{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)
as follows.

We introduce a formal power series
y_{F_{i}}(u)=1+y_{i}^{(1)}u^{-1}+y_{i}^{(2)}u^{-2}+\dots
, valued in the center of
{\mathsf{X}}
, for each
i\in I
,
by solving the equation

y_{F_{i}}(u)=z_{F_{i}}(u)y_{F_{i}}(u+\frac{\hbar}{2}c_{\mathfrak{g}}).

One see easily that
y_{i}^{(r)}=z_{i}^{(r+1)}
modulo terms with
z_{i}^{(s)}
for
s\leq r
,
and we have the unique solution. In
[
Wen18
, §7.1]
, it is defined as
a matrix
\mathcal{Y}(u)
, but it is a scalar matrix in our case, as
Z_{F_{i}}(u)
is so.

Under
m_{\mathbf{f}}
, the element
z_{F_{i}}(u)
is multiplied by
f_{i}(u)f_{i}(u+\frac{\hbar}{2}c_{\mathfrak{g}})^{-1}

from (
2.15
). Therefore
y_{F_{i}}(u)
is multiplied by
f_{i}(u)
.

Theorem 2.19

(
[
Wen18
, Th. 7.3, Th. 7.11]
)
.

(1)
Let
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
be the subalgebra of
{\mathsf{X}}
generated by matrix entries of coefficients of

y_{F_{i}}(u)^{-1}{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)
for
i\in I
. It is isomorphic to
{\mathsf{Y}}
under the restriction of
\tilde{\Phi}
.

(2)
The subalgebra
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
is equal to the
m_{{\mathbf{f}}}
-fixed subalgebra

\{x\in{\mathsf{X}}\mid m_{{\mathbf{f}}}(x)=x\}

of
{\mathsf{X}}
.

We thus recover the original Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
both as a quotient and subalgebra of the extended
Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
.

The following will be used later in the last part of
section

4(vii)
.

Lemma 2.20
.

(1)
Let
F
be one of
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.
The following diagram commutes:

\begin{CD}\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}@>{\rho_{F}^{\mathrm{ext}}|_{\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}}}>{}>\operatorname{End}(F)\\
@V{\tilde{\Phi}|_{\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}}}V{\cong}V\Big\|\\
{\mathsf{Y}}@>{}>{\rho_{F}}>\operatorname{End}(F)\end{CD}

Here
{}|_{\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}}
means the restriction of homomorphisms to
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
.
The same result holds if we replace
F
by
{\mathscr{H}}
in (
2.7
).

(2)
The image of
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
in
\prod_{\mathscr{H}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}})

under the injective homomorphism
\prod_{\mathscr{H}}\rho^{\mathrm{ext}}_{\mathscr{H}}

coincides with the image of
{\mathsf{Y}}
.

Proof.

By (
2.14
), the composition passing through the left bottom corner is
given by
(\rho_{F_{i}}\otimes\rho_{F})({\mathscr{R}}^{\mathrm{uni}}(u))
. On the other hand,
the composition passing through the right top corner is given by

R_{F_{i},F}(u)
by (
2.11
).
The two
R
-matrices differ by a rescaling by
f(u)
, but it can be absorbed
if we restrict to the
m_{{\mathbf{f}}}
-fixed subalgebra
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
.

The same is true for
{\mathscr{H}}
, as
\tilde{\Phi}
is a Hopf algebra isomorphism,
compatible with translation automorphisms
\tau_{\zeta}
.

(2) follows from (1).
∎

Remark 2.21
.

The definition of the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
depends on the choice of a finite
dimensional vector space
V=\bigoplus_{i\in I}F_{i}
.
We can replace it by a nontrivial irreducible representation, say a single
F_{i}
.
Let
{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
be the corresponding extended Yangian. We have a homomorphism
{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}\to{\mathsf{X}}

sending
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
to
{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)
. It is isomorphic to
{\mathsf{Y}}\otimes\Bbbk[z_{i}^{(r)}\mid r\geq 2]

as in
2.16
. (Here
i
is fixed.)
Moreover, we can recover
{\mathsf{Y}}
from
{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
either as a quotient or a subalgebra.

2(xi).
Unitarity

Consider
R_{F_{2},F_{1}}(u_{2}-u_{1})^{-1}(12)
as a rational operator

F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\to F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes F_{1}[u_{1}]
.
It intertwines the
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module structures, hence
must coincide with
R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u_{1}-u_{2})
as the uniqueness
of the
R
-matrix, and also the normalization condition on the tensor product of the
\ell
-highest weight vectors.
We have the
unitarity equation
:

(2.22)

R_{F_{2},F_{1}}(v-u)_{21}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}(12)R_{F_{2},F_{1}}(v-u)(12)=R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u-v)^{-1}.

See
[
MO19
, §4.5]
. The subscript
21
means the exchange
of the first and second factors, that is the conjugation by
(12)
.

3.
Involutions on quiver varieties

In this section, we will define an involution on a quiver variety.
This has been done already in
[
Li19
]
, but we will explain from
the scratch, as our main example was only briefly mentioned in
[
Li19
, Remark 9.2.4]
.

This section, as well as the appendix
appendix

A
, is an edit of an article
[
Nak18
]
,
written in response to
[
Li19
]
. The article was not submitted to
any journal.

3(i).
Quiver varieties

Let
(I,\Omega)
be a quiver of type ADE. Let
{\mathbf{v}}=({\mathbf{v}}_{i})
,

{\mathbf{w}}=({\mathbf{w}}_{i})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{I}
be dimension vectors for gauge
nodes and framing nodes respectively. Let
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
be the
corresponding quiver variety. When
{\mathbf{v}}
,
{\mathbf{w}}
are clear from the context, we simply denote it by
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
.

We will use the convention about quiver varieties in
[
Nak03
]
in
‘holomorphic’ description. In particular,

\zeta=(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}},\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})\in(\sqrt{-1}{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}})^{I}
. The
complex part
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
appears in the moment map equation

\mu=-\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
, while the real part
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
appears in the
stability condition.
We drop the subscript
{\mathbb{C}}
from the complex part of the moment map
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
,
as we only use the holomorphic description. We also denote
\prod_{i}\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
by

G_{{\mathbf{v}}}
instead of
G^{\mathbb{C}}_{{\mathbf{v}}}
.
We say
\zeta
is
generic
if it is not contained in any of

(\sqrt{-1}{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}})\otimes D_{\theta}
for a hyperplane
D_{\theta}

given by a positive root
\theta
with
\theta\leq{\mathbf{v}}
.
cf.
[
Nak03
, Prop. 2.6]
.
If
\zeta
is generic,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is smooth of dimension

{\mathbf{v}}\cdot(2{\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{v}})
, where
{\mathbf{C}}
is the Cartan matrix.

A standard stability condition used in
[
Nak98
, Def. 3.9]
is
one satisfying
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}>0
for all
i\in I
.

The symplectic vector space, used to define
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, is
denoted by
\mathbf{M}
. When we want to emphasize the underlying
I
-graded vector
spaces
V=\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}
,
W=\bigoplus_{i}W_{i}
, we denote it by
\mathbf{M}(V,W)
.

We associate weights
\lambda
,
\mu
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
to
{\mathbf{w}}
,
{\mathbf{v}}
by

(3.1)

\lambda=\sum_{i\in I}{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\varpi_{i},\quad\mu=\lambda-\sum_{i\in I}{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{i\in I}({\mathbf{w}}_{i}-\sum_{j}{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}{\mathbf{v}}_{j})\varpi_{i}

as usual. We can also regard
\lambda
,
\mu
as weights of the extended
Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
in
section

2(ix)
.

The assumption that
(I,\Omega)
is of type ADE is restrictive,
but it is necessary for the involution to be well-defined on
the quiver variety with
generic
parameter
\zeta
, as the involution
first sends
\zeta
to
-\zeta
, and then sends back to the original

\zeta
by the longest element of the Weyl group
w_{0}
plus a diagram automorphism.
It means that we need to deal with possibly singular quiver varieties with non-generic
\zeta
,
more general
\sigma
-quiver varieties. For example,
\operatorname{\rm SO}
or
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
-instanton moduli
spaces on
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
are
\sigma
-quiver varieties, but with parameter
\zeta=0
.
See
[
Cho16
]
. More general
\sigma
-quiver varieties will be focus of the future work.

We have a natural projective morphism

\pi\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{(0,\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, defined
in
[
Nak94b
, §4]
and
[
Nak98
, (3.18)]
.
Here
(0,\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})
is the parameter
obtained by replacing the real part
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
of
\zeta
by
0
, but
keeping the complex parameter.

We also use the notation
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{w}})
, which is the disjoint union of

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
for all
{\mathbf{v}}
with fixed
{\mathbf{w}}
.

Remark 3.2

(convention on
{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
-action)
.

Suppose
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
. Then
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
has a natural

{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
action, which scales all linear maps in
\mathbf{M}(V,W)
by weight
1
.
We denote this
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
by
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}
to distinguish it from the
other
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-actions.

Let
{\mathbf{q}}^{m}
denote the character of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
with weight
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We denote the Lie algebra element in the corresponding to
{\mathbf{q}}
by

\hbar/2
, i.e.,
{\mathbf{q}}^{m}=\exp(m\hbar/2)
. The division ‘
/2
’ is necessary
to make the geometric convention compatible with the standard defining relations
of Yangian. See the formula below
[
Var00
, (4.1)]
.

3(ii).
Involution defined by McKay correspondence

Let
\Gamma
be a finite subgroup of
\operatorname{\rm SU}(2)
. Recall that the McKay correspondence
associate an irreducible representation
\rho_{i}
of
\Gamma
to each vertex
i
of the Dynkin diagram of affine type ADE.

Definition 3.3
.

Let us define an involution
\star
5
5

5

This involution
\star
was denoted by
*
in
[
Nak03
, §9]
.

on the Dynkin diagram of affine type ADE by

\rho_{i}^{*}\cong\rho_{{i}^{\star}}
, where
\rho_{i}^{*}
is the dual representation of
\rho_{i}
.

It fixes the trivial representation
\rho_{0}
, and hence induces a
diagram involution on the Dynkin diagram of finite type ADE. It is the same
diagram involution given by the longest element
w_{0}
of the Weyl
group as
-w_{0}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{{i}^{\star}}
, where
\alpha_{i}
is the
simple root corresponding to the vertex
i
.
Explicitly, it is given as follows according to the type of the Dynkin diagram
as in
fig.

1
.
It is the identity for other types, not listed in
fig.

1
.

\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
A_{\mathrm{even}}

\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
A_{\mathrm{odd}}

\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
D_{\mathrm{odd}}

\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
E_{6}

Figure 1.
The involution induced by the longest element of the Weyl group

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
{i}^{\star}=i
. The irreducible representation
\rho_{i}

has either an orthogonal or a symplectic form, which is
\Gamma
-invariant.

Proof.

By the assumption we have an isomorphism

\rho_{i}\cong\rho_{i}^{*}
of
\Gamma
-modules. Let us fix it and denote it
by
\varphi
. We take

\prescript{t}{}{\varphi}\colon(\rho_{i}^{*})^{*}=\rho_{i}\to\rho_{i}^{*}
. Since

\rho_{i}
is irreducible, there is a nonzero constant
\varepsilon
with

\prescript{t}{}{\varphi}=\varepsilon\varphi
. Since

\prescript{t}{}{(\prescript{t}{}{\varphi})}=\varphi
,
\varepsilon^{2}=1
, hence
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.
If
\varepsilon=1
(resp.
-1
),
\varphi
gives an orthogonal (resp. symplectic) form on
\rho_{i}
.
∎

The sign
\varepsilon
above is
not
a choice from the proof above, and is
automatically determined by the McKay graph as follows:
The trivial representation, assigned to
i=0
, is orthogonal.
For type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell}
with odd
\ell
with
i={(\ell+1)/2}
, it is
orthogonal. This is because
\rho_{i}
is
1
-dimensional.
For other types,
\rho_{i_{0}}
for the vertex
i_{0}
adjacent to the vertex
i=0

in the affine Dynkin diagram is the
representation given by the inclusion
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{\rm SU}(2)
. Therefore

\rho_{i_{0}}\cong\rho_{i_{0}}^{*}
is symplectic. If
i
is adjacent to

i_{0}
and
{i}^{\star}=i
, then
\rho_{i}\cong\rho_{i}^{*}
is orthogonal.
This is because
\rho_{i}
is a direct summand of
\rho_{i_{0}}\otimes\rho_{i_{0}}

which has
\Gamma
-invariant orthogonal form.
If
j
is adjacent to such an
i
with
{j}^{\star}=j
, then
\rho_{j}\cong\rho_{j}^{*}
is
symplectic, and so on.
As a side remark, two vertices
i\neq{i}^{\star}
at the tail in type

D_{\mathrm{odd}}
satisfy this rule: If they were fixed by
\star
,
then they must be symplectic vertices. But
\rho_{i}
,

\rho_{{i}^{\star}}
are
1
-dimensional representations of

\Gamma
. Hence they cannot be fixed by
\star
.

We have an induced isomorphism

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{{\zeta}^{\star}}({{\mathbf{v}}}^{\star},{{\mathbf{w}}}^{\star})
, where

{\zeta}^{\star}_{i}=\zeta_{{i}^{\star}}
,
{{\mathbf{v}}}^{\star}_{i}={\mathbf{v}}_{{i}^{\star}}
,
{{\mathbf{w}}}^{\star}_{i}={\mathbf{w}}_{{i}^{\star}}
.
It is given by sending
B_{h}
,
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
to
B_{{h}^{\star}}
,
a_{{i}^{\star}}
,
b_{{i}^{\star}}
.
We also change the orientation
\Omega
to
{\Omega}^{\star}
, hence
\varepsilon
, by

\star
, i.e., we use
\varepsilon_{{\Omega}^{\star}}
instead of

\varepsilon
where

\varepsilon_{{\Omega}^{\star}}(h)=\varepsilon({h}^{\star})
.

3(iii).
Transpose

We replace vector spaces
V=\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}
,

\bigoplus_{i}W_{i}
by their dual spaces
V^{*}=\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}^{*}
,
W^{*}=\bigoplus_{i}W_{i}^{*}
,
and define the symplectic vector space
\mathbf{M}(V^{*},W^{*})
as in

[
Nak03
, §2(ii)]
.
We also replace the orientation
\Omega
by the opposite one
\bar{\Omega}
.
This is used for the definition of the symplectic form on
\mathbf{M}(V^{*},W^{*})
, and
hence the definition of the moment map
\mu
.

Definition 3.5
.

We define an isomorphism
t\colon\mathbf{M}(V,W)\to\mathbf{M}(V^{*},W^{*})
by
replacing linear maps
B_{h}
,
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
to

\prescript{t}{}{B}_{\overline{h}}\colon V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}^{*}\to V_{\operatorname{i}(h)}^{*},\quad-\prescript{t}{}{b}_{i}\colon W_{i}^{*}\to V_{i}^{*},\quad\prescript{t}{}{a}_{i}\colon V_{i}^{*}\to W_{i}^{*}.

The parameter
\zeta
for the hyperkähler moment map
equation is replaced by
-\zeta
.
Hence this construction gives an involutive isomorphism

t\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{-\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}).

A little more precisely, the space
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})

depends on the
I
-graded vector space
W
, not only on its dimension
vector
{\mathbf{w}}
. (On the other hand, it does not depend on
V
as we take
the quotient by
\prod\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
.)
Thus
{\mathfrak{M}}_{-\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is associated with
W^{*}
, instead of
W
.

Let us combine this isomorphism with one in

section

3(ii)
, i.e., consider

t\circ\star=\star\circ t
. Then we replace
a
\Gamma
-representation

\bigoplus W_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}
with its dual

\bigoplus W_{i}^{*}\otimes\rho_{i}^{*}=\bigoplus W_{i}^{*}\otimes\rho_{{i}^{\star}}
.
It is more natural in view of
[
KN90
]
.

3(iv).
Reflection functor

Let
S_{w_{0}}
be the reflection functor associated with the longest element
w_{0}
of the Weyl group.
(It was denoted by
\mathcal{F}
in
[
Nak03
, (9.1)]
.)
We use the definition given in
[
Nak92
,
Nak03
]
. See
[
Lus00
,
Maf02
]
for other definitions.

If
w_{0}=s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{\nu}}

(
i_{p}\in I
) is a reduced expression, we have

S_{w_{0}}=S_{i_{1}}\dots S_{i_{\nu}}
, where
S_{i_{p}}
is the reflection functor for a
simple reflection
s_{i_{p}}\equiv s_{i}
defined as follows. We assume

(\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}},\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{C}}})\neq(0,0)
, and
choose
z_{1}
,
z_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}
so that

z_{1}z_{2}=\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{C}}}
,

-|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}=2\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}
. They are unique up to

(tz_{1},t^{-1}z_{2})
for
t\in\operatorname{\rm U}(1)
. The ambiguity can be absorbed by
the action of the gauge group
\prod_{i}\operatorname{GL}(V^{\prime}_{i})
used in the definition of quiver varieties.

We consider a complex

(3.6)

V_{i}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{i}}\widetilde{V}_{i}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}V_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in H\\
\operatorname{i}(h)=i\end{subarray}}V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}\oplus W_{i}\xrightarrow{\beta_{i}}V_{i}

where

\alpha_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}z_{1}\operatorname{id}_{V_{i}}\\
\bigoplus B_{\overline{h}}\\
b_{i}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\beta_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}z_{2}\operatorname{id}_{V_{i}}&\sum\varepsilon(h)B_{h}&a_{i}\end{bmatrix}.

Then this is indeed a complex, moreover
\alpha_{i}
is injective, and
\beta_{i}

is surjective. We define
V^{\prime}_{i}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{i}/\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}
. We
further define
B^{\prime}_{h}
,
B^{\prime}_{\overline{h}}
for
\operatorname{i}(h)=i
as

\begin{gathered}B^{\prime}_{h}=\begin{bmatrix}B_{h}\\
-\varepsilon(h)z_{2}\iota_{h}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\colon V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}\to\widetilde{V}_{i},\quad B^{\prime}_{\overline{h}}=\begin{bmatrix}B_{\overline{h}}&-z_{1}\pi_{h}&0\end{bmatrix}\colon\widetilde{V}_{i}\to V_{\operatorname{o}(h)},\\
a^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{i}\\
0\\
-z_{2}\iota_{W_{i}}\end{bmatrix}\colon W_{i}\to\widetilde{V}_{i},\quad b^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{i}&0-z_{1}\pi_{W_{i}}\end{bmatrix}\colon\widetilde{V}_{i}\to W_{i},\end{gathered}

where
\iota_{h}
,
\iota_{W_{i}}
are the natural inclusions
of
V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
and
W_{i}
into
\widetilde{V}_{i}
, and

\pi_{h}
,
\pi_{W_{i}}
are the natural projections from
\widetilde{V}_{i}
to
V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
and
W_{i}
respectively.
One see that
\beta_{i}B^{\prime}_{h}=0
,
B^{\prime}_{\overline{h}}\alpha_{i}=0
,

\beta_{i}a^{\prime}_{i}=0
,
b^{\prime}_{i}\alpha_{i}=0
. Therefore they are well-defined linear maps
between
V^{\prime}_{i}
and
V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
,
W_{i}
.
Moreover they satisfy the moment map equation

\mu(B^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=-s_{i}(\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})
, as well as
the
s_{i}(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}})
-stability condition. See
[
Nak03
, Cor. 3.7]
.
This definition literally applies to the last operator
S_{i_{\nu}}
for

i=i_{\nu}
. For the next
S_{i_{\nu-1}}
we use the above definition
with the parameter
s_{i_{\nu}}\zeta
. And so on.

The composition
S_{w_{0}}=S_{i_{1}}\dots S_{i_{\nu}}
gives an isomorphism

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{-{\zeta}^{\star}}

as
\zeta=w_{0}(-{\zeta}^{\star})
. In general,
S_{w_{0}}
changes
dimensions of vector spaces:
S_{w_{0}}
does not change

{{\mathbf{w}}}
, but sends
{{\mathbf{v}}}
to

w_{0}\ast{{\mathbf{v}}}
given by

{{\mathbf{w}}}-\mathbf{C}(w_{0}\ast{{\mathbf{v}}})=w_{0}({{\mathbf{w}}}-\mathbf{C}{{\mathbf{v}}})
.
In terms of weights
\lambda
,
\mu
, they are changed as

\lambda\mapsto\lambda
,
\mu\mapsto w_{0}(\mu)=-{\mu}^{\star}
respectively.
Here
\star
on weights is defined in an obvious way.

When
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}=0
,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is an affine algebraic variety. The
definition of a reflection functor in
[
Lus00
]
is defined for
the coordinate ring of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
and
does
make sense even
when
\zeta
is not necessarily generic.

Suppose
\zeta
is generic again. Recall
(0,\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})
the parameter
obtained by replacing the real part
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
of
\zeta
by
0
, but
keeping the complex parameter. It may be generic or not. We have a
projective morphism
\pi\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{(0,\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}
. The
reflection functor in
[
Lus00
]
is defined for

{\mathfrak{M}}_{(0,\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}
even if
(0,\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})
is not generic, as we
remarked above. Moreover
\pi
commutes with reflection functors

[
Nak03
, §8]
.
Furthermore, the reflection functor in
[
Lus00
]
is
the identity when
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
.

Example 3.7
.

Consider a type
\mathrm{A}_{1}
quiver variety with dimension vector
{\mathbf{v}}
,

{\mathbf{w}}
with
{\mathbf{v}}\leq{\mathbf{w}}
. Suppose
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}\neq 0
. By

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\ni[(a,b)]\mapsto ba\in\operatorname{End}(W)
, we see that

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is isomorphic to the adjoint orbit of
semisimple matrices with eigenvalue
0
with multiplicity
{\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{v}}
,
and eigenvalue
-\zeta
with multiplicity
{\mathbf{v}}
. More concretely,
the eigenspace for the eigenvalue
0
(resp.
\zeta
) is
\operatorname{Ker}a

(resp.
\operatorname{Im}b
). By the reflection functor
S
, we get

\operatorname{End}(W)\ni b^{\prime}a^{\prime}=ba+\zeta,\quad V^{\prime}\cong W/\operatorname{Im}b\cong\operatorname{Ker}a.

Note
ba+\zeta
has eigenvalues
\zeta
and
0
, and their eigenspaces
are identified with
\operatorname{Ker}a
and
\operatorname{Im}b
respectively.

Next suppose
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
and
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}>0
. Then
b

is injective, and
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is isomorphic to the
cotangent bundle of the Grassmannian of
{\mathbf{v}}
-dimensional subspaces
in
W
. The isomorphism is given by
(ba,\operatorname{Im}b)
while
ba
is
viewed as
\operatorname{Hom}(W/\operatorname{Im}b,\operatorname{Im}b)
. Under the reflection functor

S
,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is sent to
{\mathfrak{M}}_{-\zeta}({\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
,
which is the cotangent bundle of the Grassmannian of

({\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{v}})
-dimensional
quotients
of
W
. Then
S
is given by

(ba,W/\operatorname{Im}a)
.

3(v).
Tautological bundles under the reflection functor

Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
be the tautological bundle on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})

associated with the vertex
i
. Similarly, let
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}
be the
tautological bundle associated with the framing node
i
.
Under the reflection
S_{i}
at
i
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is changed to
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}_{i}
.
By the definition of the reflection functor, we have the following
relation in the K-group of vector bundles on

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
:

{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}_{i}={\mathcal{W}}_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in H\\
\operatorname{i}(h)=i\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\operatorname{o}(h)}\ominus{\mathcal{V}}_{i},

where the left hand side is regarded as a class on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})

by the pull-back by
S_{i}
.
We use the Cartan matrix
\mathbf{C}=(a_{ij})
to define a
K-theory class

(3.8)

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}{\mathcal{W}}_{i}+\sum_{j}(-a_{ij}){\mathcal{V}}_{j}={\mathcal{W}}_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in H\\
\operatorname{i}(h)=i\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\operatorname{o}(h)}\ominus{\mathcal{V}}_{i}^{\oplus 2}.

Then we have
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{j}={\mathcal{U}}_{j}-\sum_{j}a_{ij}{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, i.e.,

{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
changes according to the usual formula for change of
weights under the simple reflection.
By the composition, we have

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{\prime}=-{\mathcal{U}}_{{i}^{\star}},

where
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{\prime}
is defined on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{-{\zeta}^{\star}}(w_{0}\ast{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
,
and pull-backed to
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
by
S_{w_{0}}
in this
equation.

We would like to consider
{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
-equivariant K-classes
when
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
(and generic
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
).
Recall that
{\mathbf{q}}^{m}
denote the character of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
with weight
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We consider it as an equivariant
K
-theory class.

Let us first note that
z_{1}=0
or
z_{2}=0
is
0
if
\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{C}}}=0

according to the sign of
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}
in the definition of
S_{i}
.
If
z_{2}=0
, we modify (
3.6
) as

{\mathbf{q}}^{-2}V_{i}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{i}}{\mathbf{q}}^{-2}V_{i}\oplus\bigoplus{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}\oplus{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}W_{i}\xrightarrow{\beta_{i}}V_{i}.

It becomes a
{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
-equivariant complex. If
z_{1}=0
,
the modification is almost the same, but put
V_{i}
instead of

{\mathbf{q}}^{-2}V_{i}
in the middle term.
The difference arises as we need to make
z_{1}\operatorname{id}_{V_{i}}
or
z_{2}\operatorname{id}_{V_{i}}

{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
-equivariant.
Then we define
V^{\prime}_{i}
as
{\mathbf{q}}^{2}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{i}/\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}

if
z_{2}=0
, and
\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{i}/\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}
if
z_{1}=0
.
This makes
S_{i}
a
{\mathbb{C}}_{\hbar}^{\times}
-equivariant isomorphism.

We modify (
3.8
) as

(3.9)

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{i}+\sum_{j}{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}[-a_{ij}]{\mathcal{V}}_{j},

where
[m]
is the
q
-integer defined by

[m]=\frac{{\mathbf{q}}^{m}-{\mathbf{q}}^{-m}}{{\mathbf{q}}-{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}}
.
In practice,
m=0
,
-1
, or
2
, hence
[0]=0
,
[-1]=-1
,

[2]={\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}
.
We have

(3.10)

{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{j}={\mathcal{U}}_{j}-\sum_{j}{\mathbf{q}}^{\pm}[a_{ij}]{\mathcal{U}}_{i},

where
\pm
is
+
if
z_{2}=0
,
-
if
z_{1}=0
.
This computation was given in
[
VV03
, (4.6.3)]
.

For
S_{w_{0}}
, we suppose
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
appearing in the
domain of
S_{w_{0}}
is one for
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}<0
for all
i
.
Then it follows from (
3.10
) that

(3.11)

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{\prime}=-{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathbf{c}}\,{\mathcal{U}}_{{i}^{\star}},

where
{\mathbf{c}}
is the Coxeter number of the Dynkin diagram.
See
[
VV03
, Lemma 4.6]
.
6
6

6

Note that
\omega
in
[
VV03
, Lemma 4.6]
is the composition of
S_{w_{0}}
and the transpose
t
. Therefore
the formula involves the dual of
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
.

Example 3.12
.

Consider type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
quiver variety with
{\mathbf{v}}=0
,
{\mathbf{w}}=\varpi_{1}
,
where the numbering of vertices is
1
,
2
, …,
\ell-1
from the left.
Then
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is a single point.
Suppose
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}<0
for all
i
as above. Then

{\mathfrak{M}}_{-{\zeta}^{\star}}(w_{0}\ast{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is given in the notation of
(
6.1
) by
D_{1/2}=D_{3/2}=\dots=D_{\ell-3/2}=1
,

C_{1/2}=C_{3/2}=\dots=C_{\ell-3/2}=0
,
where
W_{1}=V_{1}=\dots=V_{\ell-1}={\mathbb{C}}
,
W_{\ell-1}=0
.
We have

{\mathcal{U}}_{1}={\mathbf{q}}^{-1},\qquad{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}=-{\mathbf{q}}^{\ell-1}.

Thus (
3.11
) is checked. If we use the opposite stability parameter

\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}>0
for all
i
, we have
D_{1/2}=D_{3/2}=\dots=D_{\ell-3/2}=0
,

C_{1/2}=C_{3/2}=\dots=C_{\ell-3/2}=1
instead. Then

{\mathcal{U}}_{1}={\mathbf{q}}^{-1}
is unchanged, but
{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}=-{\mathbf{q}}^{-\ell-1}
.

3(vi).
Orthogonal/symplectic forms on framing vector spaces

Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be a diagram automorphism. Except the
D_{\mathrm{even}}
case, it is either
\operatorname{id}
or
\star
.
There is a nontrivial diagram automorphism for
D_{\mathrm{even}}
, which is
given by swap of the two tails as in the
D_{\mathrm{odd}}
case. Therefore

\sigma^{\prime}
is either
\operatorname{id}
or this nontrivial automorphism.
The diagram automorphism is a choice. A different choice will give a different
involution on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

Definition 3.13
.

We consider the composite

\sigma\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\sigma^{\prime}\circ S_{w_{0}}\circ\star\circ t
, which is an
isomorphism

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathfrak{M}}_{\sigma(\zeta)}(\sigma({\mathbf{v}}),\sigma({\mathbf{w}})),

where

\sigma(\zeta)=\sigma^{\prime}(\zeta),\quad\sigma({\mathbf{v}})=\sigma^{\prime}(w_{0}\ast{{\mathbf{v}}}^{\star}),\quad\sigma({\mathbf{w}})=\sigma^{\prime}({{\mathbf{w}}}^{\star}).

For the change of
\zeta
, we have used
w_{0}(-{\zeta}^{\star})=\zeta
.
Changes of
{\mathbf{v}}
,
{\mathbf{w}}
are obvious. In terms of weights,

(3.14)

\sigma(\lambda)=\sigma^{\prime}({\lambda}^{\star}),\qquad\sigma(\mu)=-\sigma^{\prime}(\mu).

In order to make
\sigma
an involution, we assume

\sigma(\zeta)=\zeta
,
\sigma({\mathbf{v}})={\mathbf{v}}
,
\sigma({\mathbf{w}})={\mathbf{w}}
hereafter.

Since the quiver varieties depend on the
I
-graded vector space
W
, the equality
\sigma({\mathbf{w}})={\mathbf{w}}

is not sufficient. We need to fix an identification between
W_{i}
and

W^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
. This will be done in the
remainder of this subsection.

We first choose an orthogonal
(+)
or symplectic
(-)
type, which determines
signs
\varepsilon_{i}
on vertices
i
by the rule described below.
If we exchange
(+)
and
(-)
, each
\varepsilon_{i}
changes the sign for
all
i
simultaneously. This is the consequence of the rule below.

Consider the composition
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
. Looking at

fig.

1
, we find that the non

(\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star)
-fixed edges forms a subdiagram of type

A_{\mathrm{odd}}
if
Q\neq A_{\mathrm{even}}
and

\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star\neq\operatorname{id}
. Let us first consider this case.

Consider a vertex
i
, which is fixed by
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
. Since
we assume
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star\neq\operatorname{id}
, we have
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
if
Q

is not of type
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{even}}
, and
\star=\operatorname{id}
if
Q
is of
type
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{even}}
. Therefore the vertex
i
is also fixed by

\star
.
Recall that we have an isomorphism
\rho_{i}\cong\rho_{i}^{*}
for

i={i}^{\star}
gives either an orthogonal or symplectic form on

\rho_{i}
. We put an
\varepsilon_{i}
-form on
W_{i}
so that the
tensor product form on
W_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}
is the given type
(+)
or

(-)
.
Forms on
W_{i}
and
W_{j}
are alternative when
i
and
j
are adjacent
and
{i}^{\star}=i
,
{j}^{\star}=j
.
We have an isomorphism
\varphi_{i}\colon W_{i}\to W_{i}^{*}

given by the form. It satisfies
\prescript{t}{}{\varphi}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\varphi_{i}
.

Let
i_{1}
be the middle of the subdiagram. It is fixed by

\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
, hence we have already assigned an
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}
-form
on
W_{i_{1}}
.
For other vertices
i
in the subdiagram of type
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, we
assign the form of the
same
type as
W_{i_{1}}
on

W_{i}\oplus W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
so that

W_{i}\perp W_{i}
,
W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}\perp W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
.
It means that we choose a pair of isomorphisms

\varphi_{i}\colon W_{i}\to W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}^{*}
,

\varphi_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}\colon W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}\to W_{i}^{*}
such that

\prescript{t}{}{\varphi}_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\varphi_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
. The sign
\varepsilon_{i}
is
equal to
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}
given by the form on
W_{i_{1}}
.

Next consider the case
Q=A_{\mathrm{even}}
and

\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star\neq\operatorname{id}
. There is no vertex fixed by

\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
, but the rule is similar to above: We assign an

\varepsilon_{i}
-form on
W_{i}\oplus W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
according to the
given type
(\pm)
.

The rest is
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star=\operatorname{id}
.
Suppose
Q
is not of type
\mathrm{A}
.
We put an
\varepsilon_{i}
-form on
W_{i}
so that
the tensor product form on
W_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}
is given type when

{i}^{\star}=i
.
We then continue to put
\varepsilon_{i}
-forms on
W_{i}
for the remaining
vertices alternatively, i.e., the type of the forms on
W_{i}
and
W_{j}

are opposite if
i
and
j
are adjacent.
Next suppose
Q
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
. There is a vertex, denoted
by
1
, which gives a vector representation of the corresponding Lie
algebra of type
\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{\ell}
. It is leftmost or rightmost one
depending on the convention.
We assign an
\varepsilon_{1}
-form on
W_{1}
according to the given type.
Then we assign
\varepsilon_{i}
-forms alternatively.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
G_{\mathbf{w}}=\prod_{i}\operatorname{GL}(W_{i})
. It acts on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

We introduce the corresponding group for
\sigma
version: Let
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
denote

\prod_{i:\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})=i}G_{\varepsilon_{i}}(W_{i})\times\prod_{\text{$\{i,j\}$ as below}}\operatorname{GL}(W_{i}),

where the product in the second factor runs over
unordered
pairs

\{i,j\}
with
\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})=j
, but
i\neq j
.

The notation is justified, as
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
is the fixed point subgroup
of
G_{\mathbf{w}}
with respect to an involution induced from
\sigma
:
it sends
g_{i}\in\operatorname{GL}(W_{i})
to
\varphi_{i}^{-1}\,\prescript{t}{}{g}_{j}^{-1}\,\varphi_{i}
with
j=\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})
.
If it is fixed by
\sigma
,
g_{j}
is determined by
g_{i}
, hence the factor in
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
is
\operatorname{GL}(W_{i})
.
We can choose
j
from the pair instead.

3(vii).
Orientation

The quiver varieties depend on the choice of the orientation, hence
the domain and target of
\sigma
are still different spaces. We fix
this issue in this subsection.

We choose an orientation
\Omega
according to
\star
and
\sigma^{\prime}

as follows.

Suppose that either (i)
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
contains a subdiagram of type A
of non-fixed edges,
or (ii)
Q
is of type
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{even}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
.
In either case, we choose a linear orientation on the subdiagram
or the entire diagram in case (ii).
For other parts, including the case
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star=\operatorname{id}
,
we choose an arbitrary orientation.

Under the transpose
t
, we change the orientation to the opposite
one. Then we change it further by
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
, as

S_{w_{0}}
does not change the orientation. Therefore the linear
orientation of the subdiagram of type A is preserved under
\sigma
.

On the other hand, the orientation in the
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
-fixed
part is still opposite. Therefore we compose
\sigma
with the multiplication of
-\varepsilon(h)

for edges
h
, not in the subdiagram. See (
3.16
).
We denote the composite also by
\sigma
for brevity. Thus we have defined
an automorphism
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

3(viii).
Categorical quotients

Let us calculate
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, the categorical quotient at the
level set
\mu=0
. Then
S_{w_{0}}
is the identity, hence the calculation is greatly simplified.
In particular, we define an involution
\sigma
on the symplectic vector space
\mathbf{M}

(see
[
Nak03
, §2(i)]
), which is used to define
{\mathfrak{M}}
as a symplectic quotient.

We put forms on
V_{i}
or
V_{i}\oplus V_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
, according to whether
\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})=i
or not, as in the previous subsection.
The rule is that the sign is the
opposite
of
W_{i}
or
W_{i}\oplus W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}
.
We have
\varphi_{V_{i}}\colon V_{i}\to V_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}^{*}
given by the form.
It satisfies
\prescript{t}{}{\varphi}_{V_{i}}=-\varepsilon_{i}\varphi_{V_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}}
.

Given a collection of linear maps
B
,
a
,
b
in
\mathbf{M}
, we define
a new collection
\sigma(B)
,
\sigma(a)
,
\sigma(b)
in
\mathbf{M}
by

(3.16)

\begin{gathered}\sigma(B)_{h}=\begin{cases}B^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}({\overline{h}}^{\star})}&\text{if $h$ in the subdiagram in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{subsec:orientation}}\\
-\varepsilon(h)B^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}({\overline{h}}^{\star})}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\\
\sigma(a)_{i}=-b^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})},\quad\sigma(b)_{i}=a^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}.\end{gathered}

This is related to the above definition by, for example,

a^{*}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}=\varphi_{i}^{-1}\circ\prescript{t}{}{a}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}\circ\varphi_{V_{i}}.

This formula is a consequence of the definition of the adjoint.
The choice of
\varphi_{V_{i}}
is irrelevant when we define
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{M}}
as it is a quotient space.
We have the induced involution
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, and
the projective morphism
\pi\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}},0)}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
intertwines
\sigma
.

Now let us show that
\sigma
is an involution.
Recall that
(C^{*})^{*}=C
if
C
is a linear map between vector spaces with
forms with the same type. On the other hand,
(C^{*})^{*}=-C
if forms are opposite types.
In the above formula, we use that
V_{i}
and
W_{i}
have the opposite form,
hence
\sigma^{2}(a)=a
,
\sigma^{2}(b)=b
.
As for
B_{h}
, we note
V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
and
V_{\operatorname{i}(h)}
have the same types
if
h
is in the subdiagram, and the opposite type otherwise.
Together with
\sigma^{\prime}({\overline{h}}^{\star})=\overline{h}
when

h
is in the subdiagram, we see
\sigma^{2}(B)_{h}=B_{h}
.

Since
\pi
is a birational morphism onto its image, we see that

\sigma^{2}=\operatorname{id}
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
as well.

This finishes the definition of the involution
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
. Let us denote
the fixed point subset by
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
. It is smooth
and symplectic if
\zeta
is generic (as in
[
Nak03
, Prop. 2.6]
),
as
\sigma
preserves the symplectic form by the construction.
If
\zeta
is not generic, we equip
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
with the fixed point subscheme
structure, i.e., the quotient by ideals generated by functions of forms
f-\sigma^{*}(f)
.

Following
[
Li19
]
, we call
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
a
\sigma
-quiver variety
.

By
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\circ S_{w_{0}}\circ\star\circ t
, we have

(3.17)

{\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{\prime}=-{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathbf{c}}\,{\mathcal{U}}_{\sigma^{\prime}(i)}^{\vee},\qquad{\mathcal{W}}_{i}^{\prime}={\mathcal{W}}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}^{\vee}

where
\vee
in the right hand side means the dual (virtual) vector bundle. Compare with the formula
(
3.14
).

3(ix).
The fixed point set as a symplectic
reduction

It is instructive to study the fixed point set
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
in
\mathbf{M}
before
going to the quotient space
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

(A).
Orthosymplectic quiver

The description of
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
is particularly simple when
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star=\operatorname{id}
.
Then
V_{i}
,
W_{i}
have
(-\varepsilon_{i})
,
\varepsilon_{i}
-forms, and the signs are alternating.
Following physics conventions, we draw the corresponding diagram as follows:

+
-
+
-
+
-
+
-
-
+
-
+
-
+
-
+

Here the circled nodes are
V_{i}
’s, and the rectangular nodes are
W_{i}
’s.
The
\pm
signs in circles and rectangles are the types of the forms on
V_{i}
and
W_{i}
.
For each edge in this quiver, we assign
B_{h}
, choosing
h
in the orientation.
We usually regard it as a vector in
V_{\operatorname{i}(h)}\otimes V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}

via
\varphi_{V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}}^{-1}\colon V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}^{*}\cong V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
given by the form on
V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
.
The opposite component
B_{\overline{h}}
is determined by
B_{h}
, as
we consider a point in the fixed point set
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
.
The same rule applies for edges between circled and rectangular nodes.
The group assigned to the vertex
i
is
G_{-\varepsilon_{i}}(V_{i})
, where

\varepsilon_{i}
is the type of the form on
V_{i}
.
The symplectic space
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
is the direct sum of tensor
products
V_{\operatorname{i}(h)}\otimes V_{\operatorname{o}(h)}
for edges
h
,
and
V_{i}\otimes W_{i}
.
It is a symplectic representation of
G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\prod G_{-\varepsilon_{i}}(V_{i})
.
This subgroup
G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}
is the fixed point subgroup with respect to an involution on
G_{\mathbf{v}}

defined by
(g_{i})\mapsto(\prescript{t}{}{g}_{i}^{-1})
as in
3.15
.
We have a natural residual action of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}=\prod G_{\varepsilon_{i}}(W_{i})

on the framing vector spaces.

The above diagram is an
orthosymplectic quiver
, appeared first in
[
KP82
]

(in type A).
It was used in order to realize nilpotent orbits in the classical Lie algebras
there.
It was also the main example in
[
Li19
]
.
The corresponding Coulomb branches will be studied in
[
FHN
]
.

(B).
Mixture of orthosymplectic and usual quivers

In type
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{odd}}
with
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
,
we only need to record the left half of the original quiver,
as the right half is determined by the left. This leads to the following diagram:

(3.18)

-

+

Here nodes without
\pm
signs are just vector spaces, and
we assign
\operatorname{Hom}(V_{\operatorname{o}(h)},V_{\operatorname{i}(h)})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(V_{\operatorname{i}(h)},V_{\operatorname{o}(h)})
as usual. The same
applies to the rightmost edge between

and
-
.
We assign
V_{i}\otimes W_{i}
to the rightmost vertical
edge between
-
and
+
.
The group assigned to the vertex is
\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
except
the rightmost one is
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(V_{i_{1}})
. Hence
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
is a symplectic representation
of
G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}=\prod_{i\neq i_{1}}\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})\times\operatorname{\rm Sp}(V_{i_{1}})
.
We have the residual action of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}=\prod_{i\neq i_{1}}\operatorname{GL}(W_{i})\times\operatorname{\rm O}(W_{i_{1}})
.
This example is the case when the type is
(+)
. If
the type is
(-)
, we exchange
+
and
-
in the quiver.
In type D,
\mathrm{E}_{6}
with
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star\neq\operatorname{id}
, we
also have a similar combination of the usual and orthosymplectic quivers.

(C).
Second exterior/symmetric power at the end of the quiver

Finally, we consider type
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{even}}
with
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
.
Let
\ell
be the number of vertices, and
\ell/2
and
\ell/2+1
be the middle vertices,
which are exchanged by
\star
.
We only need to record the left half of the original quiver as above,
but we have an additional contribution from the middle edge.
The diagram is as follows:

(3.19)

{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}

Here
{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}
means the second exterior power of the vector space
V_{\ell/2}

at the rightmost vertex, which was
\ell/2
in the original quiver.
We add its cotangent space
T^{*}{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}V_{\ell/2}={\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}V_{\ell/2}\oplus({\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}V_{\ell/2})^{*}
to
\mathbf{M}
for the usual quiver variety.
