This summand comes from the endomorphisms between
V_{\ell/2}
and
V_{\ell/2+1}
in the original quiver,
under the identification
V_{\ell/2+1}=V_{\ell/2}^{*}
.
Note that
T^{*}{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}V_{\ell/2}
is a symplectic representation of
\operatorname{GL}(V_{\ell/2})
.
This is the case when the type is
(+)
. For the type
(-)
, we replace
{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}
by
the second symmetric power
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}
.
Groups are
G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}=\prod\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
,
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}=\prod\operatorname{GL}(W_{i})
,
where
i
runs over the vertices in the above diagram.

Coulomb branches of gauge theories in (B,C) will be studied in
[
Nak25
]
. See
6.3
and
6.20
.
Just before submitting this manuscript to arXiv, we learn that there is a proposal
[
LWW25
]

to relate Coulomb branches of gauge theories associated with
(G^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{v}},\mathbf{M}^{\sigma})

to fixed point subschemes in generalized affine Grassmannian slices with respect to involutions.

Let us return back to the general case.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}
be the categorical quotient
of
\mu^{-1}(0)\cap\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
by the action of
G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}
.

Since the moment map for
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
is the restriction of
\mu
,
this quotient is regarded as a symplectic quotient of
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
.

By a generalization of the LeBryun-Procesi theorem
[
LBP90
]
in

[
Lus98
]
(with framing) and
[
Li19
]
(orthosymplectic),
we have a closed immersion

\iota\colon\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}).

See
[
Li19
, Prop. 9.2.1]
.

By the argument in
[
FHN
, §Fixed point set in the ordinary bow variety]
,
the closed immersion is a set theoretical bijection, whose restriction to the
regular locus consisting of
0
-stable orbits in
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}

is an isomorphism. Here the
0
-stability is defined with respect to the
action of the larger group
G_{\mathbf{v}}
(with parameter
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}=0
), and we take its intersection with

\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
.

Remark 3.21
.

Note that the reflection functor
S_{w_{0}}
has no effect on
W
. Therefore
we have a well-defined action of
G^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}
on
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
for
arbitrary
\zeta
.

3(x).
Unknown properties of cohomology/K-theory of
\sigma
-quiver varieties

One important properties of the equivariant
K
-theory of quiver varieties
is freeness over the equivariant
K
-theory of a point, established
in
[
Nak01a
, §7]
. See also
[
Nak01b
, Th. 3.4]
. It depends on the decomposition of the diagonal
of the quiver variety, found earlier in
[
Nak98
]
. Indeed, by using the
action of a maximal torus
A_{\mathbf{w}}
in
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
, we can reduce the problem to the case when

A_{\mathbf{w}}
is trivial, i.e.,
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}=\prod\operatorname{\rm O}(1)
. (Here we use
[
Nak01a
, §7]
to deal with
to deal with quiver varieties in factors of torus fixed points.)
Even in this special case, it is not clear that the decomposition of the diagonal
is available. Probably, it is not true.
Since we work on the cohomology groups, rather than
K
-theory, we can use Kaledin’s theorem
[
Kal06
]

to conclude that odd degree cohomology of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
vanishes. Therefore,
this difficulty disappears.

Another important property is the so-called Kirwan surjectivity, which says

H^{*}_{G}(\mathrm{pt})\to H^{*}({\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}))
is surjective. For quiver varieties,
this was proved by McGerty and Nevins
[
MN18
]
. However, it is not clear
whether this is still true for
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

4.
Stable envelopes for quiver varieties and
\sigma
-quiver varieties

We assume
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
,
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}>0
in this section. We drop
\zeta
from the notation
of quiver varieties and
\sigma
-quiver varieties, as
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
On the other hand, we keep
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})

for
\zeta=0
. We have projective morphisms
\pi\colon{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and
its restriction
\pi^{\sigma}\colon{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

It is also useful to introduce the notation
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{v}}{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})

and
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{w}})=\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}}{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, and similarly for
\sigma
-quiver varieties.

We have
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}
-action on
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
,
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and also on their
\zeta=0
versions by

3.2
. The morphisms
\pi
and
\pi^{\sigma}
are equivariant with respect to this action.

The inverse image
\pi^{-1}(0)
and
(\pi^{\sigma})^{-1}(0)
in
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
are denoted by
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
respectively.
It is known that
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is a lagrangian subvariety of
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
But it was observed that
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
may be smaller dimension by

[
Li19
, Rem. 6.2.2(3)]
.

Suppose
X
is either
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
or
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
Similarly
X_{0}
is either
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
or
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
In this notation, we drop
\sigma
from
\pi^{\sigma}
, hence

\pi\colon X\to X_{0}
.
We take a torus in
G_{\mathbf{w}}
or
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
, according to

X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
or
X={\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
. Denote it by
A
.
The choice of
A
will be explained below.

4(i).
Torus fixed points

For
X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, we choose
a basis of
W
, compatible with the decomposition
W=\bigoplus W_{i}
. Then we choose
a maximal torus
A_{\mathbf{w}}
of
G_{\mathbf{w}}
as the group of diagonal matrices.
The choice of the basis gives an isomorphism
A_{\mathbf{w}}\cong({\mathbb{C}}^{\times})^{n}
. Let
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})

be the coordinates of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{w}}
of
A_{\mathbf{w}}
given by this isomorphism.
The
a
-th factor
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
of
A_{\mathbf{w}}
acts on the scaling with weight
1

on a
1
-dimensional subspace

W^{a}\subset W
, corresponding to the
a
-th element in the basis.
We denote by
i_{a}
the vertex corresponding to
W^{a}
, i.e.,
W^{a}\subset W_{i_{a}}
.
Then the fixed point set
X^{A_{\mathbf{w}}}
is the disjoint union of

(4.1)

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1},{\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{2})\times\cdots\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{n},{\mathbf{w}}^{n}),

where
{\mathbf{w}}^{a}
is the dimension vector of
W^{a}
, hence the corresponding weight is
\varpi_{i_{a}}
by the definition of
i_{a}
.
We take the union over all possible choices of
{\mathbf{v}}^{a}
with constraint
{\mathbf{v}}=\sum_{a=1}^{n}{\mathbf{v}}^{a}
.
See
[
Nak01b
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Next consider the case
X={\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
. We choose a base of
W

compatible with the decomposition
W=\bigoplus W_{i}
as above, and
also compatible with forms as follows: the
a
-th factor
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
of
A_{\mathbf{w}}

acts on a basis element in
W_{i_{a}}
by weight
1
,
and on another basis element in
W_{\sigma^{\prime}({i_{a}}^{\star})}
by weight
-1
.
The value of the form between these two basis elements is nonzero, and
fixed by the
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action.
Other basis elements are fixed by this
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.
They are orthogonal to the above two basis elements with respect to the form,
hence
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
preserves the form.

We denote the
1
-dimensional
subspace spanned by the first basis element by
W^{a}
as before. Its dimension vector
is denoted by
{\mathbf{w}}^{a}
.
The second basis element gives another
1
-dimensional subspace,
whose dimension vector is
\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{a})=\sigma^{\prime}({{\mathbf{w}}^{a}}^{\star})
.
We have a coordinate system
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
of
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{w}}
as above.

Looking at the definition of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
in
3.15
, we see
an additional subspace
W^{0}
coming from vertices such that
G_{\varepsilon_{i}}(W_{i})=\operatorname{\rm O}(\mathrm{odd})
.
Each such a vertex
i
contributes a
1
-dimensional subspace
W^{0}_{i}
to
W^{0}
.
If there are no such vertices, we set
W^{0}=0
.
It is the weight
0
subspace of
W
with respect to
A_{\mathbf{w}}
-action. Let
{\mathbf{w}}^{0}
be its dimension vector.

Note also that
A_{\mathbf{w}}
is a maximal torus in the connected component
(G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma})^{0}
of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
.

The fixed point set
X^{A_{\mathbf{w}}}
is the disjoint union of

(4.2)

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1},{\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{2})\times\cdots\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{n},{\mathbf{w}}^{n})\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0}).

Note that the last factor is a
\sigma
-quiver variety, while all preceding factors are ordinary quiver varieties.
It is because
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{a},{\mathbf{w}}^{a})
appears through the embedding

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{a},{\mathbf{w}}^{a})\to{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{a}),{\mathbf{w}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{a})).

Here
\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{a})
,
\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{a})
are defined as in
3.13
. The embedding
is given by
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{a},{\mathbf{w}}^{a})\ni x\mapsto x\oplus\sigma(x)
, where the direct sum
is given by direct sum of linear maps. See
[
Li19
, Prop. 5.2.1]
for details.
7
7

7

The assumption
{\mathbf{w}}^{2}=a({\mathbf{w}}^{2})
in
[
Li19
]
, which corresponds to
{\mathbf{w}}^{a}=\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{a})
in our setting, is unnecessary.

When
{\mathbf{w}}^{0}=0
, we understand
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
is a point if
{\mathbf{v}}^{0}=0
,
and
\emptyset
otherwise.

Dimension vectors
{\mathbf{v}}^{a}
satisfy a constraint:

{\mathbf{v}}^{0}+\sum_{a=1}^{n}\left({\mathbf{v}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{a})\right)={\mathbf{v}}.

Note also
\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{0})={\mathbf{v}}^{0}
,
\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{0})={\mathbf{w}}^{0}
, as
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
does not
make sense otherwise.

Note that we place the factor
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
at the end of the product. It is because
we want to make the twisted Yangian as a
left
coideal subalgebra of the Yangian.
Equivariant cohomology of (
4.2
) will be a module of the twisted Yangian,
which is a tensor product of the equivariant cohomology of factors.
The factor
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
is a genuine
\sigma
-quiver variety without torus action, hence
not much is known about it.

Example 4.3
.

Consider type A quiver with
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
. For
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{even}}
, there is no
vertex
i
fixed by
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
. Therefore
W^{0}=0
, hence
{\mathbf{v}}^{0}={\mathbf{w}}^{0}=0
.
For
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, we have a vertex
i_{1}
in the middle of the quiver, fixed by
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star
.
We may have a
1
-dimensional
W^{0}
at the vertex
i_{1}
a priori, but
there is no
{\mathbf{v}}^{0}
for which
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
can be defined. Indeed,
we must have
\varpi_{i_{1}}={\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{v}}^{0}
from the equation
\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{0})={\mathbf{v}}^{0}
, but
there is no integral solution
{\mathbf{v}}^{0}
to this equation.

For the case of
\sigma^{\prime}=\star
, an example of
{\mathbf{w}}^{0}
is given by slices to
distinguished

nilpotent orbits. For example, we have the following.

Example 4.4
.

Consider the type
\mathrm{A}_{9}
quiver with
\sigma^{\prime}=\star
. We take the
type
(-)
. So we put orthogonal forms on
W_{i}
for even
i
.
We choose
{\mathbf{w}}=(0,0,0,1,0,1,0,0,0)
,
{\mathbf{v}}=(1,2,3,4,4,4,3,2,1)
,

{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}=(1,2,3,3,4,5,3,2,1)
). The
condition
\sigma({\mathbf{w}})={\mathbf{w}}
is automatically satisfied, and

\sigma({\mathbf{v}})={\mathbf{v}}
,
\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{\prime})={\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
are satisfied by our choice of
{\mathbf{v}}
,
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
.
We also choose
\zeta
so that
\sigma(\zeta)=\zeta
. Thus

G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}=\operatorname{\rm O}(1)\times\operatorname{\rm O}(1)
, hence
A_{\mathbf{w}}=\{1\}
.
By
[
Li19
, Th. 8.3.3]
,
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is the inverse image of the Slodowy
slice to the nilpotent orbit for the partition
(6,4)
in the
cotangent bundle of the flag variety of
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(10)
.
Similarly,
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{w}})
is the inverse image of the Slodowy
slice to the same nilpotent orbit in the cotangent bundle of the
9
-step isotropic flag variety
of type
(1^{3}2\,0^{2}1^{3})
, where the numbers are difference of dimensions of consecutive subspaces in the flag.
Note that entries of
(6,4)
have multiplicity
1
. Therefore they are distinguished nilpotent orbits.

Comparing
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
with
{\mathbf{v}}
, we see
{\mathbf{v}}_{4}
decreases by
1
while
{\mathbf{v}}_{6}
increases by
1
.
This suggests there should be a natural correspondence in the product

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{w}})
. Indeed, both are inverse images
of the
same
Slodowy slice, there is one from ambient spaces. In view of the construction below,
we want to interpret this correspondence as a matrix entry of an
R
-matrix.

4(ii).
Attracting sets

Let
\rho\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to A_{\mathbf{w}}
be a cocharacter. The
torus roots
are the
A_{\mathbf{w}}
-weights

\{\alpha\}
occurring in the normal bundle to
X^{A_{\mathbf{w}}}
. See
[
MO19
, Def. 3.2.1]
.
For
X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, they are contained in
\{u_{a}-u_{b}\mid a\neq b\}
.
For
X={\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, they are contained in
\{u_{a}-u_{b},u_{a}\}
.
Indeed,
u_{a}
appears in the normal bundle to

\prod_{b\neq a}{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{b},{\mathbf{w}}^{b})\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0}+{\mathbf{v}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{a}),{\mathbf{w}}^{0}+{\mathbf{w}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{a}))

unless this is equal to
X^{A_{\mathbf{w}}}
.

The root hyperplanes divide the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}_{{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbb{R}}}
of a maximal compact subgroup of
A_{\mathbf{w}}
into open chambers.
They are type A Weyl chambers for
X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, and type B/C Weyl chambers for
X={\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
For example
\mathfrak{C}=\{u_{1}>u_{2}>\cdots>u_{n}>0\}
is an example for the latter case.
When
G^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}
is an even orthogonal group, we should consider the Weyl chambers with respect to
the full
\operatorname{\rm O}
, instead of
\operatorname{\rm SO}
. Therefore, we get type B/C Weyl chambers.

For a cocharacter
\rho
, we replace
A_{\mathbf{w}}
by the Zariski closure

\overline{\rho({\mathbb{C}}^{\times})}
, and denote it by
A
. In other words,

A
is a subtorus of the maximal torus
A_{\mathbf{w}}
of
G_{\mathbf{w}}
or

G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
, and
\rho
is generic in
A
.

We define its attracting set by

X^{\rho\geq 0}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\left\{x\in X\,\middle|\,\lim_{t\to 0}\rho(t)x\text{ exists}\right\}.

It was introduced under the notation
\mathfrak{Z}
in
[
Nak01b
]
.
Similarly, we define the repelling set
X^{\rho\leq 0}
by the attracting set of
\rho^{-1}
.

It decomposes into the union of locally closed subsets

X^{\rho\geq 0}(F)\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\left\{x\in X\,\middle|\,\lim_{t\to 0}\rho(t)x\in F\right\},

according to the decomposition of
X^{A}
into
connected components
X^{A}=\bigsqcup F
.
For
X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and maximal
A=A_{\mathbf{w}}
, they are various choices of decompositions
{\mathbf{v}}^{a}
with

\sum{\mathbf{v}}^{a}={\mathbf{v}}
, as it is known that
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{a},{\mathbf{w}}^{a})
is connected.
For
X={\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, we do not know
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
is connected,
but we can still decompose
X^{A}
and
X^{\rho\geq 0}
according to the decomposition

{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}^{0}+\sum({\mathbf{v}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{a}))
anyway.

We have an order
\preceq
on the index set
\{F\}
of the decomposition so that

\bigcup_{F^{\prime}\preceq F}X^{\rho\geq 0}(F^{\prime})
is closed in
X
.

4(iii).
Polarization

Suppose
F
is a fixed point component of
X^{A}
.
Consider the equivariant Euler class
e(N_{F})
of normal bundle
N_{F}
to
F
in
X
. Let

e(N_{F})|_{H^{*}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})}
be its

H^{2\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})
-part with respect to the decomposition

H^{2\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F}_{A}(F)=\bigoplus_{i}H^{2i}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})\otimes H^{2\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F-2i}(F)
.
Since
X
has a symplectic form preserved by the
A
-action, weights appear as pairs

\alpha
,
-\alpha
in the normal bundle. Therefore,

(-1)^{\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F/2}e(N_{F})|_{H^{*}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})}

is a perfect square. A choice
\varepsilon
of a square root of this element is called a
polarization
:

\varepsilon^{2}=(-1)^{\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F/2}e(N_{F})|_{H^{*}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})}.

A choice of
\rho
gives a decomposition
N_{F}=N_{F}^{\rho>0}\oplus N_{F}^{\rho<0}
into
positive and negative parts with respect to
\rho
. Since
N_{F}^{\rho<0}
is the dual
of
N_{F}^{\rho>0}
with respect to the symplectic form, we have
e(N_{F})=(-1)^{\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F/2}e(N_{F}^{\rho<0})^{2}
.
We define the sign in
\pm e(N_{F}^{\rho<0})
by
\pm e(N_{F}^{\rho<0})_{H^{*}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})}=\varepsilon
.

For quiver varieties, there is a natural choice of the polarization given by the decomposition

T{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})=T^{1/2}\oplus(T^{1/2})^{\vee}
in the equivariant
K
-theory, where

T^{1/2}
is defined by a choice of an orientation of the quiver:

T^{1/2}=\sum_{h\in\Omega}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{V}_{\operatorname{o}(h)},\mathcal{V}_{\operatorname{i}(h)})-\sum_{i\in I}\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{V}_{i})+\sum_{i\in I}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{W}_{i},\mathcal{V}_{i}).

See
[
MO19
, §2.2.7]
.
For
\sigma
-quiver varieties, it is not clear how to define
T^{1/2}
. For example,

\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
in
section

3(ix)
(A)
is not a cotangent type
in general.
We will choose an ad hoc polarization for
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
in Examples in
sections

6
and
7
.

4(iv).
Stable envelopes

Fix
\rho
and
\varepsilon
as above.
Let
\mathbb{T}
be the product
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times A
.

The
A
-degree of a class in
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(F)
is the degree with respect
to
H^{*}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})={\mathbb{C}}[\mathfrak{a}]
in the isomorphism

H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(F)=H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}(F)\otimes H^{*}_{A}(\mathrm{pt})
.

Theorem 4.5

(
[
MO19
, Th. 3.3.4]
)
.

There exists a unique map of
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathrm{pt})
-modules

\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}\colon H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X^{A})\to H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X)

such that for any connected component
F
of
X^{A}
and any class

\gamma\in H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}/A}(F)
, it satisfies

(i)

\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}(\gamma)
is contained in the kernel
of the restriction homomorphism

H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X)\to H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X\setminus\bigcup_{F^{\prime}\preceq F}X^{\rho\geq 0}(F^{\prime}))
.

(ii)

The restriction of
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}(\gamma)
to
F
is equal to

\pm e(N_{F}^{\rho<0})\cup\gamma
, where the sign is determined by the polarization.

(iii)

The
A
-degree of the restriction of
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}(\gamma)
to
F^{\prime}
is less than

\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\text{\rm codim}}\nolimits F^{\prime}
for any
F^{\prime}\prec F
.

Main examples in
[
MO19
]
were quiver varieties, but the proof
was given for symplectic resolution, hence is applicable for

\sigma
-quiver varieties, as noted in
[
Li19
]
. Moreover,

\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}(\gamma)|_{F^{\prime}}
for
F^{\prime}\prec F
is divisible
by
\hbar
.

The stable envelope
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}
is induced from a lagrangian
cycle in
X\times X^{A}
supported on

(4.6)

(\pi\times\pi)^{-1}\left\{(x,y)\in X_{0}\times X^{A}_{0}\,\middle|\,\lim_{t\to 0}\rho(t)x=y\right\},

where the second
\pi
in
\pi\times\pi
is the restriction of
\pi
to
X^{A}
.
See
[
Nak13
, §3.3]
, where the above subvariety is denoted by
Z_{\mathfrak{T}}
.

4(v).
Induced polarization

One of fundamental properties of stable envelopes is a compatibility with respect to
the restriction to the subtorus
A^{\prime}\subset A
. This is guaranteed by the uniqueness
property of stable envelopes. See
[
MO19
, §3.6]
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a chamber and
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{C}
be a face of some codimension.
Let
A^{\prime}
be the subtorus of
A
spanned by
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
, and
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
be its Lie algebra.
We consider
X^{A}\subset X^{A^{\prime}}\subset X
. Consider a polarization
\varepsilon
of the normal bundle
N_{F}

of a component
F
of
X^{A}
in
X
. We factor it as
\varepsilon^{\prime}\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
into weights that are
0
and
nonzero on
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
respectively. Then
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is a polarization of the normal bundle

N^{\prime}_{F}
of
F
in
X^{A^{\prime}}
.

A connected component
F^{\prime}
of
X^{A^{\prime}}
contains
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
, …as connected components
of
(X^{A^{\prime}})^{A/A^{\prime}}=X^{A}
. We suppose
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
, …have polarization
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{2}
, …and decomposes them as
\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, …as above. We say

F^{\prime}
has the
induced polarization

\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
if
\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
\varepsilon_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, …are equal to
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
, when
they are restricted to
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
.

When all components of
X^{A^{\prime}}
have the induced polarization, the stable envelope factorizes as

\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}\circ\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}.

See
[
MO19
, Lemma 3.6.1]
.

Although it was not emphasized in
[
MO19
, §3.6]
, polarizations of
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
,
\cdots
must
be chosen consistent so that
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
is well-defined. This may not be possible in general.
See
section

6(iii)
.

4(vi).
Geometric construction of the
R
-matrix

By the localization theorem in equivariant cohomology,
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}
becomes
an isomorphism if we take tensor products with the fractional field

\operatorname{Frac}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathrm{pt})
of
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathrm{pt})
.

For two chambers
\mathfrak{C}
,
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

R_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathfrak{C}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{End}(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(X^{A}))\otimes\operatorname{Frac}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathrm{pt}).

Consider the case
X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
and
A
is a three dimensional
torus with coordinates
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
. We may divide the diagonal

{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
as it acts trivially on
X
. We have the chamber
structure divided by root hyperplanes
u_{a}-u_{b}=0
(
a\neq b
). Let
us denote the
R
-matrix from the chamber
\{u_{a}>u_{b}\}
to

\{u_{a}<u_{b}\}
by
R_{ab}(u_{a}-u_{b})
. It is the
R
-matrix defined by
the stable envelope for the subtorus generated by
u_{a}
,
u_{b}
,
if
X^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{a}=u_{b}}}
has the induced polarization in the sense of
section

4(v)
.

We have two ways to move from the chamber
\{u_{1}>u_{2}>u_{3}\}
to
the opposite chamber
\{u_{1}<u_{2}<u_{3}\}
by crossing root
hyperplanes. Composites of
R
-matrices are the same for either choice
by the definition of the
R
-matrix. It leads to the
Yang-Baxter
equation
:

(4.7)

R_{12}(u_{1}-u_{2})R_{13}(u_{1}-u_{3})R_{23}(u_{2}-u_{3})=R_{23}(u_{2}-u_{3})R_{13}(u_{1}-u_{3})R_{12}(u_{1}-u_{2}).

This is the fundamental observation in
[
MO19
, §4.1.9]
.

4(vii).
Maulik-Okounkov Yangian and extended Yangian

We assume the following hereafter.

Assumption 4.8
.

We have a polarization of components of
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})^{A_{\mathbf{w}}}
for all
{\mathbf{w}}
so that
all components of
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})^{A^{\prime}}
for any subtorus
A^{\prime}
from a face of a chamber
have the induced polarization.

In particular, the
R
-matrix
R_{F_{i},F_{j}}(u_{1}-u_{2})
is defined for
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i}))\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{j}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{j}))
and satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation.

The
R
-matrices, or rather
R
-linear operators in view of
2.13
,
defines the extend Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
, as its definition only requires the
R
-linear operators
satisfying the Yang-Baxter equation (
4.7
).
This is the Maulik-Okounkov Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
in
[
MO19
]
.

In fact, Varagnolo
[
Var00
]
constructed representations of
{\mathsf{Y}}

on the equivariant cohomology of quiver varieties
in the same way as the author did earlier in
[
Nak01a
]

for the equivariant
K
-theory, long before
[
MO19
]
.
Therefore it is natural to expect that
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
is
{\mathsf{X}}

when the quiver is of type ADE.

Theorem 4.9
.

Assume the quiver is of type ADE.

(1)
Maulik-Okounkov Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
is isomorphic to the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
.
The isomorphism is given by the identification of the
R
-matrix of
{\mathsf{Y}}
with the
geometric
R
-matrix in
section

4(vi)
under the identification
F_{i}=H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi^{i}))
in (
2.4
), reviewed below.

(2)
The core Yangian
\mathbb{Y}
, a subalgebra of
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
defined below, is equal to
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
under the
isomorphism of
(1)
.

(3)
The center of
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
is generated by
\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})

(
i\in I
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
)
,
where
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}
is the tautological bundle over
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
associated with the framing node
i
.

Indeed, slightly weaker statements
{\mathsf{Y}}\subset{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}

and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathrm{MO}}^{\mathrm{ext}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}

were shown by
McBreen in his thesis
[
McB13
]
.
Here
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathrm{MO}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is the extended Lie algebra constructed
from the geometric
R
-matrix as in
section

2(ix)
.
We will not use his argument, which is a calculation of some matrix coefficients of the geometric
R
-matrix.

Let us first recall the construction
[
Var00
]
briefly. It was given by
defining generators of
{\mathsf{Y}}
as operators on
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))
, and checking
the defining relations of
{\mathsf{Y}}
are satisfied. Here
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times A_{\mathbf{w}}
.

Among generators in the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
h_{i}
is just given by claiming
the direct summand
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}))
has weight
\mu
in (
3.1
).
The generators
x_{i,0}^{\pm}
is given by an correspondence in

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}+\alpha_{i},{\mathbf{w}})
, introduced in
[
Nak94b
, (10.4)]
.
See also
[
Nak98
, §5]
. Finally,
h_{i,r}
is given by
equivariant Chern classes of the complex
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
in (
3.9
).
More general
x_{i,r}^{\pm}
with
r>0
are given by commutator of
x_{i,0}^{\pm}
with
h_{i,r}
,
hence unnecessary to define separately, but they are given by powers of the first Chern class
of a natural line bundle over the correspondence.
See
[
Var00
, (4.2)]
for the detail.

Theorem 4.10

(
[
Var00
]
)
.

Operators
x_{i,r}^{\pm}
,
h_{i,r}
on
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))
satisfy the defining relations of

{\mathsf{Y}}
.

As we have mentioned
section

2(i)
, the
\ell
-highest weight vector is given by the
fundamental class of
{\mathfrak{M}}(0,{\mathbf{w}})=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
.

Structures of representations given by equivariant cohomology are parallel to those given by equivariant
K
-theory
studied in
[
Nak01a
]
.
The isomorphism (
2.4
) between
F_{i}
and
H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi^{i}))
follows
as both are irreducible
{\mathsf{Y}}
-modules, and Drinfeld polynomials are the same.

Another relevant result to us is about the tensor product of representations.

Let us decompose
{\mathbf{w}}
as
{\mathbf{w}}^{1}+{\mathbf{w}}^{2}
, and take the corresponding
cocharacter
\rho\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to A_{\mathbf{w}}
so that
{\mathbf{w}}^{1}
is weight
0
, and
{\mathbf{w}}^{2}
is weight
1
.
From the construction of
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module structure, it is clear that

H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))
, Borel-More homology of
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})
is also a
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module
so that the pushforward homomorphism with respect to the inclusion

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})
is a morphism of
{\mathsf{Y}}
-modules.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
Nak01b
, Th. 6.12]
)
.

There exists a unique isomorphism of
{\mathsf{Y}}
-modules

H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{1}))\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{2}))\cong H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})),

sending the tensor product of the
\ell
-highest weight vectors to the
\ell
-highest weight vector.

Here the
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module structure on the left hand side is given by the coproduct
\Delta
.
As remarked in
[
Nak01b
, Remark 6.14]
, the existence of the coproduct must be shown
independently from quiver varieties, and certain properties must be checked. A written proof
of the existence of the coproduct was given in
[
GNW18
]
, but it was known
to experts before. The necessary properties were checked even earlier in
[
CP91
, §2.8]
.

The uniqueness is an obvious consequence of the localization theorem in equivariant cohomology.
Namely, the isomorphism is the unique isomorphism of
{\mathsf{Y}}
-modules after tensoring with
the fractional field
\operatorname{Frac}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}(\mathrm{pt})
.
See
[
Nak01b
, Lemma 6.4]
. Therefore the point of the proof is to check that
the image of the left hand side is the equivariant cohomology of
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})

as a
non-localized
module.

The isomorphism with respect to the opposite coproduct is given by

H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\leq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))
instead. Therefore the
R
-matrix is
given via the isomorphism

H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))\otimes\operatorname{Frac}H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}(\mathrm{pt})\cong H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\leq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))\otimes\operatorname{Frac}H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}(\mathrm{pt}),

defined via the inclusions
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})
,
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\leq 0}({\mathbf{w}})\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})
.

Since the statement of
[
Nak01b
, Th. 6.12]
is about equivariant
K
-theory of
lagrangian subvarieties,
the isomorphism in
4.11
is replaced by

(4.12)

\Psi\colon H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}}}({\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}}^{1}))\otimes H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}({\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}}^{2}))\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}}}(\widetilde{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})),

where
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})
is the subvariety
\{x\in{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})\mid\lim_{t\to 0}\rho(t)x\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}}^{2})\}
.
We use the perfect pairing between corresponding modules (
[
Nak01a
, Th. 7.3.5]
,
[
Nak01b
, Th. 3.13]
) to get the above statement.

Next we prove the following:

Lemma 4.13
.

The isomorphism
\Psi
in (
4.12
) is given by the stable envelope in

4.5
.

Proof.

The property (i) of
4.5
is guaranteed as the image is contained
in
H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}}}(\widetilde{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))
.

The property (iii) is a consequence of the fact
that
\Delta
is cocommutative at
\hbar=0
. The
R
-matrix is the identity at
\hbar=0
,
and the stable envelope is a block Gauss decomposition of the
R
-matrix. See
[
MO19
, §4.9]
.

For the property (ii), we need to recall a part of the proof of
4.11
.

Consider a minimum component
F=F_{\min}
with respect to the order
\preceq
,
which is
{\mathfrak{M}}(0,{\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}^{2})
.
For
F_{\min}
,
the isomorphism is given by the Thom isomorphism of

X^{\rho\geq 0}(F)={\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathfrak{M}}(0,{\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}^{2}))
,
denoted by
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{1}
in
[
Nak01b
, (5.4)]
.
It is the total space of the vector bundle
N_{F_{\min}}^{\rho>0}
.
Therefore the restriction to
F_{\min}
is given by the multiplication
by the equivariant Euler class
e(N_{F_{\min}}^{\rho<0})
of
the normal bundle of
X^{\rho\geq 0}(F_{\min})
in
X
.
This implies the assertion for
H^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(0,{\mathbf{w}}^{1}))\otimes H^{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}^{2}))
.

For a cycle
\gamma
supported in a lagrangian of other components
F
, we use the proof of
[
Nak01b
, Prop. 6.3]
, which
turns out to be the argument in
[
Nak01a
, 12.3.2]
.
Let
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
be the subset of
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
defined in

[
Nak01b
, (2.11)]
or
[
Nak01a
, (5.4.1)]
. It is a closed subset
in an open subset
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;\leq n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})=\bigcup_{n^{\prime}\leq n}{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n^{\prime}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
Irreducible lagrangians in
F^{\prime}
intersects with
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
for some
k\in I
and
n>0

in their open dense subsets unless
{\mathbf{v}}=0
, and the complements are contained in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;>n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})=\bigcup_{n^{\prime}>n}{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n^{\prime}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
By the descending induction on
n
, we may assume that the property (ii) holds for
cycles
\gamma^{\prime}
whose images under
\Psi
are supported on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;>n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
, as
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;>n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})=\emptyset
for
n\geq\dim V_{k}
.

We have the Grassmann bundle structure
p\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;0}({\mathbf{v}}-n\alpha_{k},{\mathbf{w}})

(
[
Nak01b
, Prop. 2.13]
,
[
Nak01a
, Prop. 5.4.3]
or
[
Nak98
, Prop. 4.5]
).
By construction,
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}
and
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\rho\geq 0}
are
compatible with
p
, i.e., the inverse images of
p
of their intersection with
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;0}({\mathbf{v}}-n\alpha_{k},{\mathbf{w}})
are equal to the intersection with
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
Also the Grassmann bundle is the restriction of the correspondence used in the definition
of
x^{-}_{k,0}
in
[
Var00
]
.
(More precisely, we use the version which corresponds to the
n
-th divided power of
x_{k,0}^{-}
.)
Since (
4.12
) is constructed as a
{\mathsf{Y}}
-homomorphism, the image
\Psi(\gamma)

is the pull-back of
\Psi(\gamma^{\prime})
in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;0}({\mathbf{v}}-n\alpha_{k},{\mathbf{w}})
,
capped with Chern classes of tautological bundles of the Grassmann bundle.

By the induction on
{\mathbf{v}}
, we may assume that the property (ii) holds for
\gamma^{\prime}
,
as the assertion is trivial for
{\mathbf{v}}=0
.
Let
F^{\prime}
be the component containing
\gamma^{\prime}
.
The normal bundle
N^{\rho<0}(F)
of
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}(F)
in
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
contains
the pull-back of
N^{\rho<0}(F^{\prime})
by
p
as a subbundle by the compatibility
of
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}
with
p
, and the quotient

N^{\rho<0}(F)/p^{*}N^{\rho<0}(F^{\prime})
is
the normal bundle of the Grassmann bundle
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{k;\leq n}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
Therefore the property (ii) for
\Psi(\gamma^{\prime})
implies that for
\Psi(\gamma)
.

Here we did not care the polarization
\varepsilon
in the proof, but it is contained in the
definition of generators
x_{i,r}^{\pm}
in
[
Var00
]
.
∎

Thus the
R
-matrix defined by the stable envelope coincides with the

R
-matrix defined as the
{\mathsf{Y}}
-module. Therefore, we conclude That

(4.14)

{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}\cong{\mathsf{X}}.

Recall that the extended Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
was introduced
in
section

2(ix)
, from the expansion of the
R
-matrix. This is
the same definition of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}
in
[
MO19
, §5.3]
. Therefore,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}
is nothing but
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
.

In
[
MO19
, §6.1]
, a certain Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}^{\prime}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}
was
introduced. For finite type quivers, it is the subalgebra generated by
matrix elements of the normalized classical
r
-matrix

{\mathsf{r}}-\sum_{i,j}{\mathbf{C}}^{ij}{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\otimes{\mathbf{w}}_{j},

where
{\mathbf{C}}^{ij}
is the inverse of the Cartan matrix.
(Note that The Cartan matrix is invertible for finite type quivers.)
The diagonal part of the classical
r
-matrix is

\sum_{i}{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\otimes{\mathbf{v}}_{i}+{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\otimes{\mathbf{w}}_{i}-\sum_{j}{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}{\mathbf{v}}_{j}\otimes{\mathbf{v}}_{i}.

See
[
MO19
, (4.21)]
. Therefore
{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}^{\prime}
is nothing but the original Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

The
core Yangian

\mathbb{Y}
in
[
MO19
, §6.1]
is the subalgebra of
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
generated by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q}^{\prime}
and
the operators of cup products by
\operatorname{ch}_{k}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
for
k\geq 0
and
i\in I
.
(There is a typo in
[
MO19
, Th. 6.1.4]
:
\operatorname{ch}_{0}\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{i}}={\mathbf{w}}_{i}-\sum_{j}{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}{\mathbf{v}}_{j}

should be included.)
The normalized tautological bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is defined
by using the inverse of the
q
-analog of the Cartan matrix
{\mathbf{C}}^{ij}_{q}
.
See
[
MO19
, (6.3)]
.
Comparing (
3.9
) with
[
MO19
, (6.3)]
, we
find that
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is

\sum_{j}{\mathbf{C}}^{ij}_{q}{\mathbf{q}}{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
with
{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
defined in (
3.9
).
In other words, the core Yangian
\mathbb{Y}
, as the subalgebra
of
\prod_{\mathscr{H}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}})
, is generated by operators from
the original Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
.

By
2.20
, operators on
F_{i}
coming from the original Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}

are coming from the subalgebra
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
of
{\mathsf{X}}
.
The same is true for all
{\mathscr{H}}
in (
2.7
), as
\tilde{\Phi}
is a Hopf algebra
homomorphism. Since this holds for all
{\mathscr{H}}
simultaneously,
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
is the subalgebra
of
\prod_{\mathscr{H}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}})
consisting of operators coming from the original Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
.
Therefore the core Yangian
\mathbb{Y}
is the equal to
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}

as the subalgebra of
{\mathsf{X}}\cong{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
.

Finally, let us show that the center of
{\mathsf{X}}={\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
is generated by

\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})
. It is clear that
\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})
are central.
In the associated graded,
{\mathsf{X}}={\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
degenerates to
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u])
by
2.17
.
By
[
Mol07
, Lemma 1.7.4]
, the center of
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u])
is

{\mathbf{U}}(\mathfrak{z}_{I}[u])
. The latter is generated by coefficients of
\delta_{i}[u]=\sum_{k}\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})u^{k}

in
u
. Thus the center of
{\mathsf{X}}={\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
is generated by
\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})
.

The proof of
4.9
is complete.

4(viii).
Yangian representations on cohomology of quiver varieties

By the identification
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}\cong{\mathsf{X}}
, we have a representation
of
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
on
{\mathscr{H}}=F_{i_{1}}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{i_{2}}[u_{2}]\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}}[u_{n}]
.
The space
{\mathscr{H}}
is identified, via (
2.4
), with
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{1}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{1}}))\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{2}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{2}}))\otimes\dots\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{n}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{n}}))
.
By a straight forward generalization of
4.11
, this space is further
identified with
H_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times A_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))
, where
{\mathbf{w}}=\sum_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}}
and

\rho\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to A_{\mathbf{w}}
is an appropriate cocharacter.
By the construction of
4.10
,
pushforward homomorphisms with respect to the inclusion

{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}})\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}})\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})

H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}}))\to H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\rho\geq 0}({\mathbf{w}}))\to H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))

are homomorphisms of
{\mathsf{X}}
-modules.

This assertion can be also deduced directly from the definition of
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
. It is a consequence
of the factorization property of the stable envelopes (see
section

4(v)
and
[
MO19
, §4.2.4]
).
The
R
-matrix
R_{F[u],H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))}
for
F=F_{i}=H^{*}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i}))

and
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))
is given by the product of
R
-matrices, exactly as in (
2.8
)
under the
4.8
.
As the expansion of the
R
-matrix at
u=\infty
, coefficients of the
R
-matrix
define operators on
non-localized
equivariant homology groups.
This explains the assertion for
H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))
.

Moreover, in
[
Nak13
, §4 and Remark 4]
we view the stable envelope as
a class in
H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u}}_{*}(Z_{\mathcal{T}})
, where

Z_{\mathcal{T}}
is the subvariety in (
4.6
) with

A={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u}
,

X={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}+\varpi_{i})
,
X^{A}={\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})\times{\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i})
.
Therefore the
R_{F[u],H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}))}=\operatorname{Stab}_{-\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}
is given by
the convolution product class in

H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u}}(Z_{\mathcal{T}}^{\tau}\circ Z_{\mathcal{T}})
,
where the superscript
\tau
in
Z_{\mathcal{T}}^{\tau}
refers to a permutation of factors
X
and
X^{A}
.
See
[
MO19
, Th. 4.4.1]
for the assertion

\operatorname{Stab}_{-\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}^{-1}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\varepsilon}^{\tau}
.

The convolution product involves the pushforward homomorphism with respect to the projection
p_{13}\colon X^{A}\times X\times X^{A}\to X^{A}\times X^{A}
,
which is not proper, even after we restrict it to
p_{12}^{-1}(Z_{\mathcal{T},21})\cap p_{23}^{-1}(Z_{\mathcal{T}})
.
Therefore
p_{13*}
is defined by the localization. However,
p_{13}
becomes
proper after we restrict it the
A
-fixed points. Therefore, coefficients of
R
,
expanded at
u=\infty
, are
non-localized
classes in

H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u}}(Z_{\mathcal{T}}^{\tau}\circ Z_{\mathcal{T}})
.
Since
Z_{\mathcal{T}}^{\tau}\circ Z_{\mathcal{T}}\subset X^{A}\times_{X_{0}^{A}}X^{A}
,
the usual Steinberg type variety, coefficients of
R
preserves

H_{*}^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbf{w}}))
.

The stable envelopes used above are
A\times G_{\mathbf{w}}
-equivariant. Therefore,
we can replace
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}
-equivariant homology groups by
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times G_{\mathbf{w}}
-equivariant
ones.

4(ix).
Affine types

[
AGW23
]
generalized
2.5
to the case when
{\mathfrak{g}}
is an affine Lie algebra
except
A_{1}^{(1)}
and
A_{2}^{(2)}
.
The coproduct on the Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}
was defined in
[
GNW18
]
.

4.11
and
4.13
remain true in this case with the same proof.
Therefore the Maulik-Okounkov
R
-matrix in this case is also given by

[
AGW23
]
, once it is normalized so that it acts as the identity on
the tensor product of the
\ell
-highest weight vectors.
Also, the Maulik-Okounkov Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
is isomorphic to the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
,
which is defined by a straightforward modification of
section

2(v)
.
It is interesting to understand the core Yangian
\mathbb{Y}
in the way similar to
section

2(x)
.

5.
Twisted Yangian

After reviewing the stable envelope and the derivation of Yang-Baxter equations,
let us switch to the case of
X={\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

We keep the notation
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}
for
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times A_{\mathbf{w}}
from the previous section.
For usual quiver varieties,
A_{\mathbf{w}}
is a maximal torus of
G_{\mathbf{w}}=\prod_{i}\operatorname{GL}(W_{i})
.
For
\sigma
-quiver varieties,
A_{\mathbf{w}}
is a maximal torus of
(G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma})^{0}
,
the group introduced in
3.15
.

5(i).
Dual representation

This subsection is a preparation for the subsequent subsections.

Following
[
CP91
, §2.17]
, we define the left dual representation
\prescript{t}{}{V}
of a representation
V
by

(x\cdot f)(v)=f(S(y)\cdot v),\quad x\in{\mathsf{X}},f\in\prescript{t}{}{V},v\in V.

We also define the left dual for a representation
V
of
{\mathsf{Y}}
in the same way.

Proposition 5.1

(
[
CP91
, Prop. 3.1, 3.2]
)
.

Let
V
be an irreducible representation of
{\mathsf{Y}}
with Drinfeld polynomial
P_{i,V}(u)
.
Then the left dual representation
\prescript{t}{}{V}
is also irreducible, and its Drinfeld polynomial
P_{i,\prescript{t}{}{V}}
is
given by

P_{i,\prescript{t}{}{V}}(u)=P_{{i}^{\star},V}(u+\frac{\hbar}{4}c_{\mathfrak{g}}).

5(ii).
Reflection equation

Suppose
A
is two dimensional, and consider the decomposition (
4.2
) with

n=2
. We do not need to assume
{\mathbf{w}}^{a}
(
a=1,2
) is
1
-dimensional
here. We suppose
W
decomposes as

W^{0}\oplus W^{1}\oplus W^{-1}\oplus W^{2}\oplus W^{-2}

and
u_{a}
acts with weight
1
(resp.
-1
) on
W^{a}
(resp.
W^{-a}
) and trivially on
other summands. Moreover, pairs
W^{1}
and
W^{-1}
,
W^{2}
and
W^{-2}

are dual to each other with respect to the form on
W
, and
other pairs are orthogonal.
The fixed point set is the union of

(5.2)

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1},{\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{2})\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0}).

Root hyperplanes are
u_{1}-u_{2}=0
,
u_{1}+u_{2}=0
and
u_{a}=0

(
a=1,2
) in this case.
The fixed point sets with respect to the subtori defined by these roots are

(5.3)

\begin{gathered}{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1}+{\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{1}+{\mathbf{w}}^{2})\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0}),\quad{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{2}),{\mathbf{w}}^{1}+\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{2}))\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0}),\\
\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{b},{\mathbf{w}}^{b})\times{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0}+{\mathbf{v}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{a}),{\mathbf{w}}^{0}+{\mathbf{w}}^{a}+\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{a}))\end{gathered}

respectively, where
\{a,b\}=\{1,2\}
.

Assumption 5.4
.

We assume

•

(
5.3
) have the induced polarization in the sense of
section

4(v)
.

•

the polarizations of (
5.2
) in (
5.3
) are independent of connected components
the common factor
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}}^{0},{\mathbf{w}}^{0})
(in the first two cases), and

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{b},{\mathbf{w}}^{b})
(in the last case). When
{\mathbf{v}}^{0}
or
{\mathbf{v}}^{b}
could change, they are also independent of

{\mathbf{v}}^{0}
or
{\mathbf{v}}^{b}
.

It is customary to call the
R
-matrix from the chamber
\{u_{a}>0\}

to
\{u_{a}<0\}

K
-matrix
and denoted by
K(u_{a})
.
It is the
R
-matrix defined by the stable envelope for the subtorus generated by
u_{a}
.
On the other hand, we denote other
R
-matrices by
R(u_{1}-u_{2})
,

R(u_{1}+u_{2})
according to the root hyperplane
u_{1}-u_{2}=0
and

u_{1}+u_{2}=0
.
This abuse of notation is justified, as these
R
-matrices
are coming from ordinary quiver varieties

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1},{\mathbf{w}}^{1})\times{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{2})
with respect to polarizations
in
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1}+{\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{1}+{\mathbf{w}}^{2})
given by Assumption
5.4
.

The analog of the Yang-Baxter equation is the

reflection equation
:

(5.5)

K(u_{2})R(u_{1}+u_{2})K(u_{1})R(u_{1}-u_{2})=R(u_{1}-u_{2})K(u_{1})R(u_{1}+u_{2})K(u_{2}).

This is due to Li in
[
Li19
, Prop. 5.5.3]
.
By Assumption
5.4
,
K(u_{1})
acts by the identity on

H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{2},{\mathbf{w}}^{2}))
, and similarly for
K(u_{2})
,
R(u_{1}-u_{2})
and
R(u_{1}+u_{2})
appropriately.

Let us draw the corresponding chambers:

(5.6)

\begin{CD}\{u_{1}>u_{2}>0\}@>{K(u_{2})}>{}>\{u_{1}>0>u_{2},u_{1}+u_{2}>0\}\\
@V{R(u_{1}-u_{2})}V{}V@V{}V{R(u_{1}+u_{2})}V\\
\{u_{2}>u_{1}>0\}\{u_{1}>0>u_{2},u_{1}+u_{2}<0\}\\
@V{K(u_{1})}V{}V@V{}V{K(u_{1})}V\\
\{u_{2}>0>u_{1},u_{1}+u_{2}>0\}\{0>u_{1}>u_{2}\}\\
@V{R(u_{1}+u_{2})}V{}V@V{}V{R(u_{1}-u_{2})}V\\
\{u_{2}>0>u_{1},u_{1}+u_{2}<0\}@>{}>{K(u_{2})}>\{0>u_{2}>u_{1}\}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}\end{CD}

The reflection equation (
5.5
) is an equality in

\operatorname{End}\bigl(H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{1}))\otimes\allowbreak H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{2}))\otimes\allowbreak H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{0}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}^{0}))\bigr)\otimes\operatorname{Frac}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}}}(\mathrm{pt}).

However, we often want to understand
K
and
R
-matrices as

matrices
, rather than linear maps. See
2.13
.
It is a nontrivial task as
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{1}))
, …have no
preferred base.
We will do this computation in two examples in
sections

6
and
7
.

As a preliminary step, let us consider on which vector spaces
K
and

R
-matrices operate more carefully.
Let us introduce notations:

F_{1}[u_{1}]\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{1})),\quad F_{2}[u_{2}]\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}({\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}}^{2})),\quad{\mathfrak{H}}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{0}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}^{0})).

By the discussion around (
4.2
), we have two more natural spaces

F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u_{1}]\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{1}))),\quad F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{2}))).

Although
F_{a}[u_{a}]
and
F_{a}^{\sigma}[-u_{a}]
are isomorphic by
\sigma
, they
naturally appear when we interpret
R(u_{1}+u_{2})
as the
R
-matrix
for
usual
quiver varieties.

Indeed,
R(u_{1}+u_{2})
in the right hand side of (
5.5
) is
naturally interpreted as

R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})\in\operatorname{End}\left(F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\right)\otimes\operatorname{Frac}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{1}+{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}(\mathrm{pt}),

as the fixed point set with respect to the subtorus

\{u_{1}+u_{2}=0\}
is

{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}}^{1}+\sigma({\mathbf{v}}^{2}),{\mathbf{w}}^{1}+\sigma({\mathbf{w}}^{2}))
. This
consideration suggests to interpret
K(u_{2})
, the rightmost term in
the right hand side of (
5.5
) as

(5.7)

K_{F_{2}}(u_{2})\in\operatorname{Hom}(F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}},F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})\otimes\operatorname{Frac}H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{2}}}(\mathrm{pt}).

A further consideration leads us the following diagram:

(5.8)

\begin{CD}F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}@>{K_{F_{2}}(u_{2})_{23}}>{}>F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}\\
@V{R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u_{1}-u_{2})_{12}}V{}V@V{}V{R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})_{12}}V\\
F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}\\
@V{K_{F_{1}}(u_{1})_{13}}V{}V@V{}V{K_{F_{1}}(u_{1})_{13}}V\\
F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}\\
@V{R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})_{21}}V{}V@V{}V{R_{F_{2}^{\sigma},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}-u_{2})_{21}}V\\
F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}@>{}>{K_{F_{2}}(u_{2})_{23}}>F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-u_{2}]\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}\end{CD}

Here subscripts
12
,
13
,
23
indicate tensor factors in which
operators act.
Let us explain subscripts
21
in the last row. The
R
-matrix for

F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]
goes from the chamber

\{-u_{1}>u_{2}\}
to
\{-u_{1}<u_{2}\}
by convention for
R
of usual
quiver varieties in
section

4(vi)
.
It goes the opposite direction, compared with (
5.6
).
Also, the argument of the

R
-matrix is
-u_{1}-u_{2}
. This
R
-matrix is

R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}}(-u_{1}-u_{2})
, hence we replace it by

R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})_{21}
by the unitarity (
2.22
).
This explains the entry in the left column. For the right column,

R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-u_{1}+u_{2})
goes from
\{-u_{1}>-u_{2}\}
to

\{-u_{1}<-u_{2}\}
. It goes the opposite direction.
We use the unitarity (
2.22
) to change it to

R_{F_{2}^{\sigma},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}-u_{2})
.

Thus we arrive at the reflection equation, which says (
5.8
)
is commutative:

(5.9)

\begin{multlined}K_{F_{2}}(u_{2})R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})_{21}K_{F_{1}}(u_{1})R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u_{1}-u_{2})\qquad\\
\qquad=R_{F_{2}^{\sigma},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}-u_{2})_{21}K_{F_{1}}(u_{1})R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})K_{F_{2}}(u_{2}).\end{multlined}

Here we omit most of subscripts. The only exception is
21
which is essential.

This type of the reflection equation appeared in
[
Che84
,
Skl88
]
. See also
[
KS92
, §6.2]
containing
four

types of
R
-matrices. See
[
AV22
]
for a modern
reference.
8
8

8

The convention of the spectral parameter in the
R
-matrix
is different from ours. Their
R(z)
(trigonometric case) should be
compared with our
R(-u)
. The reflection equation in
[
AV22
, Theorem 5.1.2]

is different from (
5.9
) for spectral parameters in
R
-matrices, and also for the left vs right
coideals.

Remark 5.10
.

Suppose
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
.
By
5.1
,
F_{a}^{\sigma}[-u_{a}]
is isomorphic to
(\prescript{t}{}{F}_{a})[-u_{a}-\frac{\hbar}{4}c_{\mathfrak{g}}]

as a representation of
{\mathsf{Y}}
. Thus the
R
-matrix
R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})

is identified with
R_{F_{1},\prescript{t}{}{F}_{2}}(u_{1}+u_{2}+\frac{\hbar}{4}c_{\mathfrak{g}})
.

If
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\operatorname{id}
,
F_{a}^{\sigma}[-u_{a}]
is
(\prescript{t}{}{F}_{a})[-u_{a}-\frac{\hbar}{4}c_{\mathfrak{g}}]
, pull-backed
by the automorphism of
{\mathsf{Y}}
given by the diagram automorphism
\sigma^{\prime}
.

If
F_{a}
is
F_{i}
for
i\in I
and
{i}^{\star}=i
, then
F_{a}^{\sigma}=F_{i}
. This happens
for example when
Q
is of type A and
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\operatorname{id}
.

In either way, we can understand the reflection equation in terms of representations of
{\mathsf{Y}}
,
without invoking
\sigma
-quiver varieties.

5(iii).
Unitarity

The
K
-matrix
K(u)
satisfies the unitarity
K(u)K(-u)=\operatorname{id}
as
observed in
[
Li19
, Prop. 5.5.2]
.
In our framework of understanding the
K
-matrix as a rational homomorphism
from
F[u]\otimes\mathfrak{H}
to
F^{\sigma}[-u]\otimes\mathfrak{H}
as (
5.7
),
the unitarity says

K_{F^{\sigma}}(-u)K_{F}(u)=\operatorname{id}.

5(iv).
Definition

Recall (
2.7
), which has now a geometric construction via quiver varieties:

\begin{split}{\mathscr{H}}&=F_{i_{1}}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{i_{2}}[u_{2}]\otimes\dots\otimes F_{i_{n}}[u_{n}]\\
&=H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{1}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{1}}))\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{2}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{2}}))\otimes\dots\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{n}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{n}})).\end{split}

Choosing an auxiliary space
F
from
\{F_{i}\}
and

\mathfrak{H}=H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{0}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}^{0}))
as in
section

5(ii)
,
we consider

(5.11)

\begin{multlined}{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}(R_{F_{i_{1}},F^{\sigma}})(u+u_{1})_{21}\cdots(R_{F_{i_{n}},F^{\sigma}})(u+u_{n})_{21}\qquad\qquad\qquad\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad K_{F}(u)\,R_{F,F_{i_{n}}}(u-u_{n})\cdots R_{F,F_{i_{1}}}(u-u_{1}).\end{multlined}

For example, the composite of the vertical arrows in the left column of (
5.8
)
is an example of
{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)
.
We interpret
K_{F}(u)
as a rational operator in

\operatorname{Hom}(F[u]\otimes\mathfrak{H},F^{\sigma}[-u]\otimes\mathfrak{H})
as in
section

5(ii)
, and
acting by
\operatorname{id}
on the factor
{\mathscr{H}}
.
Then
{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)
is a rational operator in

\operatorname{Hom}(F[u]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H},F^{\sigma}[-u]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H}).

We expand it around
u=\infty
as for
{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)
in (
2.8
).
The constant term is
\sigma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}
.
This is the
R
-matrix from the chamber
\{u>u_{1}>u_{2}>\cdots>u_{n}>0>-u_{n}>\cdots>-u_{2}>-u_{1}\}
to the chamber
\{u_{1}>u_{2}>\cdots>u_{n}>0>-u_{n}>\cdots>-u_{2}>-u_{1}>u\}
.

Definition 5.12
.

We define the
extended twisted Yangian

{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
as the subalgebra of

\prod_{\mathfrak{H}}\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]}({\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H})

generated by coefficients of
\hbar u^{-N-1}
of matrix entries of
{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)-\sigma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}

for all
N\geq 0
and all auxiliary spaces
F
.

Remark 5.13
.

The definition of the extended twisted Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
is
due to Li
[
Li19
, §5.6]
. We slightly modify it by interpreting
linear operators in a different way, as explained in
section

5(ii)
.
We also add the additional factor over
\mathfrak{H}=H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{0}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}^{0}))
,
as it could potentially give interesting representations of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
, as
indicated in
4.4
.

Comparing (
5.11
) with (
2.8
), we get

(5.14)

{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)={\mathsf{T}}_{F^{\sigma}}(-u)^{-1}K_{F}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u),

where
{\mathsf{T}}_{F^{\sigma}}(-u)
is defined by (
2.8
) with
F
,
u
replaced by

F^{\sigma}
,
-u
respectively.

When
{\mathbf{w}}^{0}=0
, we have
\mathfrak{H}=\Bbbk
. Therefore we have a projection

{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}\to\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}})
.
By (
5.14
), the image of this projection is contained in the image
of the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
. As remarked in
section

2(vii)
,
the homomorphism of
{\mathsf{X}}
is injective. Therefore we have a homomorphism

(5.15)

{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}\to{\mathsf{X}}.

Remark 5.16
.

(1)
Even if
{\mathbf{w}}^{0}=0
,
K_{F}(u)-\sigma\in\frac{\hbar}{u}\operatorname{Hom}(F,F^{\sigma})[[u^{-1}]]
could be nonzero.
Indeed, we will see that
K_{F}(u)-\sigma
is zero when the type is
(+)
,
but nonzero when the type is
(-)
in
section

7
.
In particular, the homomorphism (
5.15
) depends on the chosen type.

(2) Viewing the image of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
in (
5.15
) as a subalgebra of
{\mathsf{X}}
,
the definition has similarity with one in
[
GR16
, Def. 3.1]
.

(3)
As in
4.9
(3), we expect the center
Z({\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}})

of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
is the polynomial ring in variables
\operatorname{ch}_{2k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})
from

i
in the first factor of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
,
and
\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{i})+\operatorname{ch}_{k}({\mathcal{W}}_{j}^{*})
from
\{i,j\}

in the second factor of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
in
3.15
.
Here we only consider even degree parts in the first case, as
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}\cong{\mathcal{W}}_{i}^{*}
.
We then expect that
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
decomposes as the tensor product

{\mathsf{Y}}^{\mathrm{tw}}\otimes Z({\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}})
, where
{\mathsf{Y}}^{\mathrm{tw}}

is the inverse image of
\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}_{R}
under the homomorphism (
5.15
).

(4) In view of
2.21
, we can choose and fix
i\in I
,
and consider only tensor products of
F_{i}[u]
to define the extended twisted Yangian.
Namely we define
{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
from a single
F_{i}
as in
2.21
, and
define
{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime\mathrm{tw}}
by the image of
{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}^{\sigma}}(-u)^{-1}K_{F_{i}}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{i}}(u)
.
We can even restrict
\mathfrak{H}
only to
{\mathbf{w}}^{0}
. This version of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime\mathrm{tw}}

is closer to the twisted Yangian defined in
[
GR16
]
.
See
6.21
and
7.4
below.

5(v).
Coideal property

The tensor product
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
of two spaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
,
{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
of form (
2.7
) is again
of the same form. The projection

\prod_{\mathfrak{H}}\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H})\to\prod_{\mathfrak{H}}\prod_{{\mathscr{H}}_{1},{\mathscr{H}}_{2}}\operatorname{End}({\mathscr{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathscr{H}}_{2}\otimes\mathfrak{H})

gives a coproduct

\Delta\colon{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}\to{\mathsf{X}}\otimes{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}

by sending
{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)
to
{\mathsf{T}}_{F^{\sigma}}(-u)^{-1}{\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)\otimes{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)
.
This is compatible with (
5.15
), i.e., (
5.15
) sends
this coproduct to the coproduct of the extended Yangian
{\mathsf{X}}
.
Although we do not know (
5.15
) is injective as we add factors

\mathfrak{H}
, the compatibility means that
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
is
a
left coideal subalgebra
of
{\mathsf{X}}
.

The coideal property implies that the category of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
-modules
is a
module category
over the monoidal category of
{\mathsf{X}}
-modules.

5(vi).
Twisted current algebra as the associated graded

Recall that
{\mathsf{X}}
has an ascending filtration defined in
2.12
.
The associated graded algebra is
{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u])
by
2.17
.

Viewing
{\mathsf{X}}
as
{\mathsf{X}}_{\mathrm{MO}}
by
4.9
, the involution
\sigma

on
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})
induces an involution on
{\mathsf{X}}
, and also
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u]
. Let us denote all of them by
\sigma
for simplicity.
Recall further that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is
{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus\mathfrak{z}_{I}
, and
\mathfrak{z}_{I}
is spanned
by
\delta_{i}
,
i\in I
.

Lemma 5.17
.

(1)
The involution
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is given by

\sigma(h_{i})=-h_{\sigma^{\prime}(i)},\quad\sigma(\delta_{i})=\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})},\quad\sigma(e_{i})=\pm f_{\sigma^{\prime}(i)},\quad\sigma(f_{i})=\pm e_{\sigma^{\prime}(i)}.

(2)
The involution
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u]
is given by

\sigma(xu^{k})=\sigma(x)(-u)^{k}
for
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
.

Proof.

(1) By the formula of
\sigma(\mu)
in (
3.14
), the formula for
\sigma(h_{i})
follows,
as
h_{i}
is
\langle h_{i},\mu\rangle=\operatorname{rank}{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
on
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
with
\mu={\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{v}}
.
On the other hand,
\delta_{i}
counts dimension of
W_{i}
, hence the formula of
\sigma(\lambda)
in
(
3.14
) implies the formula for
\sigma(\delta_{i})
.

Elements
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
are generators of root subspaces of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, which is
1
-dimensional.
Considering their weights, we have the above formula up to nonzero multiplicative constants. We have
natural integral structures on homology groups, and
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
are integral generators. Therefore
constants must be
\pm 1
. Being
\sigma
an involution,
\pm
must be the same for
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
.

(2) It is enough to check the assertion for
x=h_{i}
,
\delta_{i}
,
k=1
. Then
h_{i}u
,
\delta_{i}u
are
given by the first Chern classes of
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}
respectively. Since
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}
are
replaced by
-{\mathcal{U}}_{\sigma^{\prime}(i)}^{\vee}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{\sigma^{\prime}({i}^{\star})}^{\vee}
under
\sigma
by (
3.17
),
we have the assertion.
∎

Remark 5.18
.

From the computation of
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, we can compute which type of symmetric pair

({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sigma})
in
[
Li19
, Table 1]
appears from the
\sigma
-quiver variety. More precisely,

\Gamma
and
a
there are the Dynkin diagram of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star

respectively.

Let us introduce a filtration
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}_{\leq 0}\subset{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}_{\leq 1}\subset\cdots
on
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
from the filtration on
{\mathsf{X}}
via
the homomorphism (
5.15
).
As in
[
GR16
, Prop. 3.3]
, we have the following:

Proposition 5.19
.

The image of the associated graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}

under (
5.15
) is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra

{\mathbf{U}}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u]^{\sigma})
of the
\sigma
-twisted current algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}}[u]^{\sigma}
.

Corollary 5.20

(cf.
[
GR16
, Cor. 3.3]
)
.

There is an isomorphism between
{\mathbf{U}}(({\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{ext}})^{\sigma})

and the image of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}_{\leq 0}
under (
5.15
).

5(vii).
Representations on cohomology groups of
\sigma
-quiver varieties

By its definition,
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
has a representation

{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H}=H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{1}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{1}}))\otimes\cdots\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\varpi_{i_{n}}}}({\mathfrak{M}}(\varpi_{i_{n}}))\otimes H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{{\mathbf{w}}^{0}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}^{0}))
.
As in
4.11
, it is isomorphic to
H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})^{\rho\geq 0})
,
where
{\mathbf{w}}=\sum_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}}+\sigma(\varpi_{i_{k}})+{\mathbf{w}}^{0}
and

\rho\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to A_{\mathbf{w}}
is an appropriate character.
As in
section

4(viii)
, we have the following:

Theorem 5.21
.

Pushforward homomorphisms with respect to the inclusion

{\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})^{\rho\geq 0}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})

H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))\to H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})^{\rho\geq 0})\to H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))

are homomorphisms of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
-modules. The leftmost and rightmost ones, after
replacing
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}
by
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}
, are also
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
-modules,
and forgetting homomorphisms
H^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{\hbar}\times G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}}({\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})\text{ or }{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))\to H^{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}})\text{ or }{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))

are homomorphisms of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
-modules.

5(viii).
Three additional
RTT
relations

The
RTT
relations for
F_{1}[u]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-v]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}
,

F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u]\otimes F_{2}[v]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}
,

F_{1}^{\sigma}[-u]\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}[-v]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}
are

(5.22)

\displaystyle R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u+v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-v)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}}(u)R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u+v),

(5.23)

\displaystyle R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}}(-u-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}}(v)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-u)R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}}(-u-v),

(5.24)

\displaystyle R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-u+v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-v)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-u)R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(-u+v).

Indeed, (
5.23
) can be deduced from (
5.22
) as follows.
Conjugating (
5.22
) by
(12)
and exchanging names of
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
,
we get

R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u+v)_{21}{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-v)={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}}(u)R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u+v)_{21}.

By the unitarity (
2.22
), we have

R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u+v)_{21}=R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}}(-u-v)^{-1}
, hence

{\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-v)R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}}(-u-v)=R_{F_{1}^{\sigma},F_{2}}(-u-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{1}^{\sigma}}(-v){\mathsf{T}}_{F_{2}}(u).

By exchanging
u
,
v
, we get (
5.23
).

5(ix).
Reflection algebra

By the construction of the
R
-matrix from the stable envelope,
{\mathsf{S}}(u)
apparently
satisfies the reflection equation

(5.25)

\begin{multlined}{\mathsf{S}}_{F_{2}}(u_{2})R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2})_{21}{\mathsf{S}}_{F_{1}}(u_{1})R_{F_{1},F_{2}}(u_{1}-u_{2})\qquad\qquad\qquad\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad=R_{F_{2}^{\sigma},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}-u_{2})_{21}{\mathsf{S}}_{F_{1}}(u_{1})R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u_{1}+u_{2}){\mathsf{S}}_{F_{2}}(u_{2}),\end{multlined}

as (rational) operator from
F_{1}[u_{1}]\otimes F_{2}[u_{2}]\otimes{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H}

to
F_{1}^{\sigma}(-u_{1})\otimes F_{2}^{\sigma}(-u_{2})\otimes{\mathscr{H}}\otimes\mathfrak{H}
.

Let us formally derive the reflection equation
from the basic one (
5.9
) and the
RTT
relations,
though the argument is well-known.
Namely we show the following lemma, then (
5.25
) follows by induction.

Lemma 5.26
.

If
\{{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u)\}
is a solution of the reflection equation (
5.25
), then

{\mathsf{T}}_{F^{\sigma}}(-u)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{F}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{F}(u)

is also a solution of the reflection equation.

Proof.

Let us introduce short-hands notations:

\displaystyle R^{\bullet\sigma}(u+v)=R_{F_{1},F_{2}^{\sigma}}(u+v),R^{\sigma\bullet}_{21}(u+v)=R_{F_{2},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u+v)_{21},

\displaystyle R^{\sigma\sigma}_{21}(u-v)=R_{F_{2}^{\sigma},F_{1}^{\sigma}}(u-v)_{21},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{a}(u)={\mathsf{S}}_{F_{a}}(u),{\mathsf{T}}_{a}^{\sigma}(-u)={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{a}}(u),{\mathsf{T}}_{a}(u)={\mathsf{T}}_{F_{a}}(u)\quad(a=1,2).

We need to show

(5.27)

\begin{multlined}R_{21}^{\sigma\sigma}(u-v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u)R^{\bullet\sigma}(u+v){\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v)\\
={\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v)R^{\sigma\bullet}_{21}(u+v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u)R(u-v).\end{multlined}

We change the left hand side by first applying (
5.22
)
to the middle part
{\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u)R^{\bullet\sigma}(u+v){\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}
,
second using

{\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}={\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u)
,

{\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v)={\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u)
, then using
RTT
relation (
5.24
). The result is

{\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}R^{\sigma\sigma}_{21}(u-v){\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u)R^{\bullet\sigma}(u+v){\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v).

We apply (
5.25
) to the middle to get

{\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v)R^{\bullet\sigma}_{21}(u+v){\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u)R(u-v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v).

We apply the
RTT
relation (
2.9
) to the last part

R(u-v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v)
, and then use

{\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v)={\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}
. We get

{\mathsf{T}}_{2}^{\sigma}(-v)^{-1}{\mathsf{S}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}^{\sigma}(-u)^{-1}R^{\bullet\sigma}_{21}(u+v){\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{T}}_{1}(u)R(u-v).

We apply
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u){\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v)={\mathsf{T}}_{2}(v){\mathsf{S}}_{1}(u)
and (
5.23
)
to this expression. The final result is the right hand side of
(
5.27
).
∎

Following
[
GR16
]
, we define the extended reflection algebra.

Definition 5.28
.

The
extended reflection algebra

{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{ref}}
is the algebra
generated by
\{s^{(r)}_{i;ab}\mid i\in I,a,b=1,\dots,\dim F_{i},r\geq 1\}
, subject to the relations (
5.25
). Here

S_{F_{i}}(u)=\sigma+\left(\sum_{r\geq 1}s^{(r)}_{i;ab}u^{-r-1}\right)_{a,b}

is regarded as an element of
\operatorname{Hom}(F_{i},F_{i}^{\sigma})\otimes{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{ref}}
under
the bases of
F_{i}
,
F_{i}^{\sigma}
.

When
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
, this is introduced in
[
Mol07
, §2.13]
under
the name of
extended twisted Yangian
. The terminology is
not consistent with ours, as our extended twisted Yangian is called just
twisted Yangian in
[
Mol07
]
.

Since we have checked the reflection equation (
5.25
) both geometric and
formal ways, we have a surjective homomorphism
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{ref}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
.

The kernel of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{ref}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
has been
understood in many examples, at least when
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
is a version
in
5.16
(4), e.g.,
[
Mol07
, Th. 2.13.4]
for Olshanski
twisted Yangian,

[
Mol07
, §2.16, Examples 4-6]
for reflection equation algebras,
9
9

9

{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{ref}}
,
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
are denoted by

\mathrm{X}({\mathfrak{g}}_{N})
,
\mathrm{Y}({\mathfrak{g}}_{N})
in
[
Mol07
, Th. 2.13.4]

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(N,l)
,
\mathcal{B}(N,l)
in
[
Mol07
, §2.16, Examples 4-6]

respectively.

and

[
GR16
, Th. 5.2]
for twisted Yangian
for symmetric pairs of type B,C,D.
But we do not pursue this direction here.

6.
Examples – instanton moduli spaces

In this section we give examples of involutions on quiver varieties,
which give moduli spaces of
\operatorname{\rm SO}
and
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons on ALE spaces of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
.
We also discuss type D and
\mathrm{E}_{6}
briefly.
We compute the
K
-matrices of these examples. From the computation,
we can see that the twisted Yangian is the
twisted Yangian
{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathfrak{o}_{\ell})
of Olshanski for
\operatorname{\rm SO}
-instantons.
(See
[
Mol07
, Chapter 2]
for the definition.)
On the other hand, we cannot choose an induced polarization for
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
-instantons,
except the case
\ell=2
.

As a byproduct, we compute their Betti numbers of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
in terms of Young tableaux
in these examples.

6(i).
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
-instanton moduli spaces

We consider type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
quiver variety and name linear maps as follows:

(6.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\ell-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\ell-\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}}

We also set
V_{0}=W_{1}
,
V_{\ell}=W_{\ell-1}
for notational convention.
The involution
\star
is given by
{i}^{\star}=\ell-i
, as indicated in
the middle broken line. We take
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
.
We choose the linear orientation so that
C_{3/2}
,
C_{5/2}
, …,

C_{\ell-3/2}
are in the orientation. We put the
(-)
sign on
C_{\ell-1/2}

so that the defining equation has a uniform form.

We assume
\dim V_{0}=\dim V_{1}=\dots=\dim V_{\ell}
. We set this
number as
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
. Let

\mathbf{M}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell}\operatorname{Hom}(V_{i-1},V_{i})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(V_{i},V_{i-1}),\qquad G\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell-1}\operatorname{GL}(V_{i}).

Thus the quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is the symplectic reduction of
\mathbf{M}
by
G

at the moment map level
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
with respect to the stability condition
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
.
It is not so important, but we use the following convention for
\zeta
. The moment map equations are

C_{i-\frac{1}{2}}D_{i-\frac{1}{2}}-\zeta_{i-\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}=D_{i+\frac{1}{2}}C_{i+\frac{1}{2}}-\zeta_{i+\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}\quad\text{for $i=1,\dots,\ell-1$}.

Therefore we have
\ell
parameters
\zeta_{\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}
, …,
\zeta_{\ell-\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}
.
However, only differences
\zeta_{\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}-\zeta_{\frac{3}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}
, …,

\zeta_{\ell-\frac{3}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}-\zeta_{\ell-\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}
matter. Thus we only have
\ell-1
independent parameters.
In this convention, the reflection functor
S_{i}
for a simple reflection at
i
exchanges

\zeta_{i-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\zeta_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
, and leaves other parameters fixed.

As in
section

3
, we define an involution
\sigma=S_{w_{0}}\circ\star\circ t
on
\mathbf{M}
.
Since
{\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{v}}=0
for our choice of dimension vectors, we have
w_{0}\ast{\mathbf{v}}^{*}={\mathbf{v}}
, hence
the target space of
\sigma
has the same dimension vectors as the source space.
Next we choose
(-)
as a type, hence we put a symplectic form
(\ ,\ )
on

W_{1}\oplus W_{\ell-1}
such that

W_{1}
and
W_{\ell-1}
are lagrangian subspaces. (Hence
W_{1}
to
W_{\ell-1}
are dual to each other.)
Then
W_{\ell-1}^{*}\oplus W_{1}^{*}\cong W_{1}\oplus W_{\ell-1}
. Therefore we can identify
the source and target spaces of
\sigma
as a variety. Therefore
\sigma
is regarded as an involution
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

Concretely,
\sigma
