changes, in the categorical quotient

{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}
, the linear maps as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\ell-1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell-1}^{*}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\ell-1}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{C}_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{D}_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{C}_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{D}_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{C}_{\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{D}_{\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{t}{}{C}_{\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\prescript{t}{}{D}_{\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{{\ell-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{{\ell-1}}
are isomorphisms induced by the symplectic form on
W_{1}\oplus W_{\ell-1}
.
Hence they satisfy
\prescript{t}{}{\varphi}_{1}=-\varphi_{{\ell-1}}
.

The
\sigma
-quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}
is the
moduli space of framed
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1})
-instantons on an ALE space of
type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
with appropriate Chern class and boundary
condition, as reviewed in
appendix

A
.

Example 6.2
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=1
. Then
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is an ALE space of type

\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
with period
\zeta
. The involution
\sigma
is trivial
in this case. Let us give two proofs of this statement.

The first one is computational. We have

\pi\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{(0,\zeta)}
, which is a resolution of
singularities
[
Kro89
]
. It is enough to check the assertion for

{\mathfrak{M}}_{(0,\zeta)}
. The coordinate ring of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{(0,\zeta)}
is
generated by

x\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\dots C_{\frac{1}{2}},\quad y\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}D_{\frac{1}{2}}\dots D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}},\quad z\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}D_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\frac{1}{2}}

with relation

xy=(z+\zeta_{\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}})\dots(z+\zeta_{\ell-\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}})
. Note that
x
,
y
,
z
are mapped to

x
,
y
,

C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}=z+\zeta_{\ell-\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}-\zeta_{\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}
.
In view of the definition
[
Lus00
, §1.1]
, these are sent to

x
,
y
,
z
by the reflection functor.

We have an alternative, more conceptual explanation as follows: By

[
KN90
]
,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is the moduli space of framed

\operatorname{\rm U}(2)
-instantons with fixed Chern class and boundary condition at

\infty
. In this example, the first Chern class
c_{1}
is zero, and
a self-dual boundary condition. Therefore an instanton is
automatically isomorphic to its dual.

\operatorname{\rm SU}(2)
-instantons are automatically

\operatorname{\rm Sp}(2)
-instantons as well, since
\operatorname{\rm SU}(2)\cong\operatorname{\rm Sp}(2)
.

Thus we have
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}={\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is an ALE space of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
with period
\zeta
in this case.

Remark 6.3

(Relation to Coulomb branches)
.

Consider the following quiver gauge theory of type
\mathrm{C}_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
:

(6.4)

1
2
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{w}}_{1}\!-\!1
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
\ell

By
[
dCA18
]
, the Coulomb branch of this theory is a symmetric bow variety. By
the choice of the above dimension vectors, it is nothing but the
categorical quotient
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G^{\sigma}
in this case.
Here

G^{\sigma}=\prod_{a=1}^{\ell/2-1}\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})\times\operatorname{\rm O}(V_{\ell/2})\quad\text{if $\ell$ is even},\qquad\prod_{a=1}^{(\ell-1)/2}\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})\quad\text{if $\ell$ is odd}.

Indeed, the bow diagram is

0
\vphantom{j^{X}}\bm{\times}
1
\vphantom{j^{X}}\bm{\times}
2
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}\!-\!1
\vphantom{j^{X}}\bm{\times}
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
\vphantom{j^{X}}\bm{\medcirc}
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
\vphantom{j^{X}}\bm{\medcirc}
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}

where we only draw the left side of the symmetry axis. The triangle
parts give
T^{*}\operatorname{GL}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})
, hence it means that we only consider the
two-way part, and do not take the reduction by
\operatorname{GL}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})
. It is
nothing but (
6.1
), and the involution coincides with one
used to define
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}
.

We can put deformation parameters
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
for
\operatorname{GL}
-factors, but the number of parameters
obtained in this way is at most
\lfloor(\ell-1)/2\rfloor
. On the other hand,
the number of parameters given by the flavor symmetry
[
BFN18
, 3(viii)]
is

\ell-1
. This number of parameters is achieved in the definition of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
above.
Indeed, we will show that
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is the deformed Coulomb branch in a separate publication.

Being the deformed Coulomb branch of a quiver gauge theory of type
\mathrm{C}_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
,
it is Beilinson-Drinfeld affine Grassmannian slice of type
\mathrm{C}_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
by

[
BFN19
, §3(v)]
. Its cohomology is related to a representation
of the Langlands dual Lie algebra, i.e.,
\mathfrak{so}(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
by geometric Satake correspondence.
Since we have
\ell
-dimensional framing at the rightmost vertex, the representation in question
is the tensor product of
\ell
-copies of the spin representation.
We expect that the twisted Yangian and
\mathfrak{so}(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
commute,
like in orthogonal skew Howe duality

[
Wen20
,
Abo22
,
BK24
]
.
This is because
\mathfrak{so}(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
should be defined on

\bm{\times}
parts in the bow diagram as in
[
Nak23
]

while
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
is defined on
\bm{\medcirc}
parts.

This remark is a motivation to study this example of
\sigma
-quiver varieties. Indeed, the computation in
the next subsection was done some years ago.

Remark 6.5
.

The categorical quotient for (
6.1
) is (
3.18
) with
+
,
-
swapped
when
\ell
is even,
or (
3.19
) with
{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}
replaced by
\operatorname{Sym}^{2}
when
\ell
is odd.
Since the
S
-duality
is expected to be the
duality
, namely the
S
-dual of the
S
-dual is the
original space, we expect the
S
-dual of (
3.18
,
3.19
) as
the rightmost part of (
6.4
). However, this is a delicate point,
as there is no consensus about the definition of Higgs branch of a
non-symmetric

quiver gauge theory, as in (
6.4
). See
[
Nak25
]
.

6(ii).
Torus action

We have an induced action of
G_{\mathbf{w}}^{\sigma}=\operatorname{GL}(W_{1})
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}

by
3.21
.
Note that
g\in\operatorname{GL}(W_{1})
acts by
\prescript{t}{}{g}^{-1}
on
W_{\ell-1}
via the isomorphism

W_{\ell-1}\cong W_{1}^{*}
.
It preserves the symplectic form, and the moment map is given by

\mu([C,D])=-D_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\frac{1}{2}}+\zeta_{\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}.

The introduction of the shift
\zeta_{\frac{1}{2},{\mathbb{C}}}
is a matter of the convention to match
with other parts of moment maps.

Let us fix a decomposition
W_{1}=W_{1}(1)\oplus\dots\oplus W_{1}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})

into
1
-dimension subspaces. We have the corresponding decomposition

W_{\ell-1}=W_{\ell-1}(-1)\oplus\dots\oplus W_{\ell-1}(-{\mathbf{w}}_{1})
so that

W_{\ell-1}(k)\cong W_{1}(-k)^{*}
under
W_{\ell-1}\cong W_{1}^{*}
.
We have the maximal torus
A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
of
\operatorname{GL}(W_{1})
preserving the
above decomposition.

Let us consider a fixed point
x
with respect to the

A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
-action. Analysis of fixed points in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is
explained in
section

4(i)
.
If
W_{1}(k)
is the eigenspace for an eigenvalue
t_{k}
,
W_{\ell-1}(-k)
is
for
t_{k}^{-1}
. In particular, all eigenvalues in
W_{0}\oplus W_{\ell-1}

are distinct, as
t_{k}\neq\pm 1
for a generic element in
A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
.
Therefore, at a fixed point
x=[(C,D)]
, vector spaces

V_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,\ell-1)
decomposes as

V_{i}=V_{i}(1)\oplus\dots\oplus V_{i}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})\oplus V_{i}(-1)\oplus\dots\oplus V_{i}(-{\mathbf{w}}_{1})

so that
C
,
D
sends
V_{i}(k)
to
V_{i\pm 1}(k)
. Let us denote
components for
k
by
C(k)
,
D(k)
.
Then
x_{k}\overset{\operatorname{\scriptstyle def.}}{=}[(C(k),D(k))]
gives a point of the quiver variety
with dimension vectors
(\dim V_{1}(k),\dots,\dim V_{\ell-1}(k))
,

(\dim W_{1}(k),0,\dots,0,\dim W_{\ell-1}(k))
.
For example, if
k>0
, the data look like

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}(k)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell-1}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{1}{2}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{1}{2}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{3}{2}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{3}{2}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}(k)=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}(k)=0}

as
W_{\ell-1}(k)=0
. This is a point in the quiver variety whose
framing dimension vector is
(1,0,\dots,0)
. It is well-known
that the corresponding quiver variety is nonempty if and only if

(6.6)

\dim V_{1}(k)=\dots=\dim V_{i}(k)=1,\quad\dim V_{i+1}(k)=\dots=\dim V_{\ell-1}(k)=0

for some
i=0,1,\dots,\ell-1
. Moreover it is a single point if it
is nonempty.
See e.g.,
[
Nak94a
, Lemma 5.1]
for a geometric proof.
In terms of a
\operatorname{\rm U}(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1})
-instanton moduli space of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
, a
fixed point corresponds to a direct sum of line bundles
(
\operatorname{\rm U}(1)
-instantons) compatible with the framing.
Alternatively,
we note that this set of quiver varieties realizes the vector representation
{\mathbb{C}}^{\ell}
of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{sl}}_{\ell}

so that the quiver variety for (
6.6
) gives a weight space. Since all weights
are in Weyl group orbits of the highest weight, the corresponding quiver varieties
are points.

If the fixed point
x
is in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
, the decomposition is
preserved under
\sigma
. More precisely
x
decomposes as
x_{k}

(
k=-{\mathbf{w}}_{1},\dots,-1,1,\dots,{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
), and the summand
x_{k}
is
mapped to
x_{-k}
under
\sigma
. Therefore if the summand for

k>0
satisfies (
6.6
) for
i
, the corresponding summand for

-k
satisfies

(6.7)

\begin{multlined}\dim V_{1}(-k)=\dots=\dim V_{i}(-k)=0,\\
\dim V_{i+1}(-k)=\dots=\dim V_{\ell-1}(-k)=1.\end{multlined}

If these are satisfied,
x_{k}
is sent to
x_{-k}
indeed. In fact,
taking transpose, the diagram automorphism and the reflection functor
are defined more generally for quiver varieties with generic
parameter. Since relevant quiver varieties are single points, we only
need to check that dimension vectors above are exchanged by

\sigma
. This check is trivial.

In this way, a fixed point assigns an integer
i=0,\dots,\ell-1
for
each
k=1,\dots,{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
. Therefore we have
\ell^{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
fixed points.

Following
[
Nak94a
]
, we assign row-increasing Young tableau
T

to a fixed point.
An each row corresponds to the summand
x_{k}
.
In order to avoid confusion, let us call the row for
x_{k}

the

k
-th row
. Then we put
(-{\mathbf{w}}_{1})
-th row at the top,
(-{\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
-th
row one below, and so on. After putting
(-1)
-st row, we put the
1
-st
row next below,
2
-nd row next, and so on. So the numbering of rows is
increasing if we go below.
The length of a row is equal to the numbering of the framed vector
space, which is
1
-dimensional.
Thus the
k
-th row with
k>0
(resp.
k<0
) has length

1
(resp.
\ell-1
).
Each node of
T
is numbered by integers between
1
and
\ell
.
In each row, the sequence of numbers is strictly increasing.
A number
i
appears in the
k
-th row if and only if

\begin{cases}\dim V_{i-1}(k)-\dim V_{i}(k)=0&\text{if $i\leq$ the length of the row},\\
\dim V_{i-1}(k)-\dim V_{i}(k)=1&\text{if $i>$ the length of the row}.\end{cases}

See
[
Nak94a
, p.120]
. In our case, for
k>0
, the unique node
is numbered by
(i+1)
when
i
is given by (
6.6
) for
V(k)
.
On the other hand, the
(-k)
-th row have nodes
1
, …,

\ell
, excluding
(i+1)
when

i
is given by (
6.7
) for
V(-k)
.
The sum
\sum_{k}\dim V_{i}(k)
is
\dim V_{i}
, hence is fixed. This
condition appears as a constraint

(6.8)

the cardinarity of the nodes with
i
is
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
.

Row increasing Young tableaux
T
with (
6.8
) is bijective to
torus fixed points in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
(
[
Nak94a
, Lem. 5.9]
).

Since
i
for (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) are the same when a
fixed point is in
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
, we have an additional constraint

(6.9)

integers appearing the
k
-th row are those which do
not

appear in
(-k)
-th row.

Here is an example:

-2

5

4

-1

5

4

1

3

2

1

with
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=2
,
\ell=5
. The numbering of rows is written in the
left of the first row.

It is clear that these tableaux are determined by entries of
k
-th
rows with
k>0
. Therefore we have
\ell^{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
fixed points.
We will record entries for
k<0
in order to express the formula of
Betti numbers by using results in
[
Nak94a
]
.

Let
T(k,a)
be the number assigned to the node at the
k
-th row,
the
a
-th column.
For a given pair
k
,
a
, we consider the following conditions on
l\neq k

(
[
Nak94a
, Def. 5.10]
):

(6.10)

•

T(l,a)<T(k,a)
when
l<k
.

•

T(l,a+1)<T(k,a)
when
l>k
, where we understand
this inequality is not satisfied if there is no node at
(l,a+1)
.

•

T(k,a)
does not appear in the
l
-th row in either cases.

For the above tableau, the node
T(1,1)=3
satisfies this condition
for
l=-1
, but not other
l=-2
,
2
.

In practice, if
k>0>l
, this condition is satisfied if and only
if
T(k,1)
does not appear in the
l
-th row and
T(k,1)\neq 1
.

Let us define the
charge

l(T)
by

l(T)=\#\{(k,l,a)\mid\text{\eqref{eq:28} is satisfied}\}.

The Poincaré polynomial of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is equal to

\sum_{T}t^{2l(T)}
by
[
Nak94a
, Th. 5.15]
. Here the sum is
over row increasing Young tableaux
T
with (
6.8
).

For a tableau
T
with (
6.9
) we define
l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)
by

(6.11)

\displaystyle l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)=\#\{

\displaystyle(k,-k,a)\mid\text{\eqref{eq:28} is satisfied}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\#\{(k,l,a)\mid\text{$k\neq-l$ and \eqref{eq:28} is satisfied}\}.

Note that (
6.10
) is satisfied for
(k,l,a)
if and only if
it is so for
(-l,-k,a)
. Therefore this is an integer.

Theorem 6.12
.

(1)
The
\sigma
-quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
has dimension
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
.

(2)
The Poincaré polynomial of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is

\sum_{T:\eqref{eq:27}}t^{2l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)}.

In particular,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is connected.

Proof.

By the hyper-Kähler rotation, the dimension and homology groups
are independent of
\zeta
. Therefore we may assume
\zeta
is of a
form
(\zeta_{{\mathbb{R}}},0)
.

Let us first introduce a
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
given by
multiplication of
t^{2}
on
C_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
for
i\neq 0
,
\ell-1
and
of
t
on
C_{\frac{1}{2}}
,
D_{\frac{1}{2}}
,
C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}
,
D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}

for
t\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.
This is the choice of the
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action in
[
Nak94a
, §5]

with a modification by
t^{-1}
on all
\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
(
i\neq 0,\ell
).
The modification does not change the action on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
as it
is in
G
.
It commutes with
\sigma
on the categorical quotient
{\mathfrak{M}}_{0}
.
Since
\pi
is a birational map onto its image, the same is true
for
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
.
Hence we have an induced
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
. We perturb it by an action of a maximal torus

A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
in
\operatorname{GL}(V_{0})
consisting of diagonal matrices as

\rho\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\ni t\mapsto(t^{N},t^{\varepsilon_{1}},\dots,t^{\varepsilon_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\times A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}

with
N\gg\varepsilon_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}>\dots>\varepsilon_{1}>0
. Then

({\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma})^{\rho({\mathbb{C}}^{\times})}=({\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\times A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}}
.
This action is the restriction of the action used in
[
Nak94a
, §5]
.
Moreover
\lambda(t)[(C,D)]
stays in a compact subset of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}

as
t\to 0
, hence converges to a fixed point. Therefore any
connected component of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
contains a fixed
point. Thus it is enough to show that the dimension of the tangent
space at a fixed point is
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
.

Let us consider the three term complex
[
Nak98
, (3.11)]
,
describing the tangent space at
[(C,D)]
. (The
0
-th and second
cohomology vanish.)
According to the decomposition
(C,D)=\bigoplus(C(k),D(k))
, the
complex decomposes as
\bigoplus_{k,l}\mathcal{C}(k,l)

(cf.
[
Nak98
, (6.1)]
). The summand
\mathcal{C}(k,l)
computes

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(x_{k},x_{l})
,
the extension between
x_{k}
and
x_{l}
for framed vector bundles.
See
[
Nak01b
, Remark 3.16]
.
The involution
\sigma
maps

\mathcal{C}(k,l)
to
\mathcal{C}(-l,-k)
. In fact,
x_{k}
is mapped
to
x_{-k}
, and the order of the extension is exchanged as
\sigma

involves transpose.

Let us give the dimension of the first cohomology of

\mathcal{C}(k,l)
. When
k>0
, let
i(k)
be the number
i
in
(
6.6
). Then

\dim H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $kl>0$ and $i(|k|)=i(|l|)$},\\
1&\text{if $kl>0$ and $i(|k|)\neq i(|l|)$},\\
1&\text{if $kl<0$ and $i(|k|)=i(|l|)$},\\
0&\text{if $kl<0$ and $i(|k|)\neq i(|l|)$}.\end{cases}

This is checked by a direct calculation for the complex.
Alternatively, we observe that
\dim H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))=\dim H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(l,k))

by the duality given by the symplectic form as in
6.13
(2) below,
hence
\dim H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
is half of the dimension of the quiver variety
containing the direct sum
x_{k}\oplus x_{l}
. The dimension of the relevant quiver variety
can be computed as
0
or
2
by the formula

{\mathbf{v}}\cdot(2{\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{v}})
mentioned in
section

3(i)
.

Note that
\mathcal{C}(k,-k)
is mapped to itself by
\sigma
,
Therefore we need to determine the action of
\sigma
on

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
. Note that

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-k,k))
gives the
tangent space for the special case
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=1
, corresponding to a
point
x_{k}\oplus x_{-k}
. We know that the involution is trivial in
the case
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=1
by
6.2
. Therefore
\sigma=\operatorname{id}
. Thus
\dim H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))^{\sigma}=1
.
For other cases,
\mathcal{C}(k,l)
and

\mathcal{C}(-l,-k)
are different components
in the tangent space
\bigoplus_{k,l}H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
.
Hence
\left(H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-l,-k))\right)^{\sigma}\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
.
From these, as well as the formula above, we see that

\bigoplus_{k,l}H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))^{\sigma}
is

{\mathbf{w}}_{1}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
-dimensional, as claimed.
Indeed, the total dimension
\sum H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
is
2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}^{2}
,
which is
\dim{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
. Among pairs
(k,l)
, we have
\dim H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))=1
for all
k
, which gives
2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
-dimensional contributions in total.
Other pairs come with
(k,l)
and
(-l,-k)
(
k\neq l
), hence contribute
as
(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}^{2}-2{\mathbf{w}}_{1})/2={\mathbf{w}}_{1}^{2}-{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
. Thus we get

2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}+{\mathbf{w}}_{1}^{2}-{\mathbf{w}}_{1}={\mathbf{w}}_{1}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}+1)
.

Let us turn to (3). The charge
l(T)
is the Morse index of the
moment map associated with the
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action above at the
fixed point corresponding to
T
(
[
Nak94a
, Lemma 5.11]
). It
is also equal to the sum of dimensions of negative weight subspaces
of the tangent space at the fixed point. The tangent space
decomposes as
\bigoplus_{k,l}H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
as above, and
the contribution of
\mathcal{C}(k,l)
to the index is equal to the
number of
a
such that (
6.10
) is satisfied.

If
k+l=0
,
\sigma
is
\operatorname{id}
on
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
. Therefore
the
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))^{\sigma}=H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
, and the
contribution to the index is the same as for
l(T)
. If
k+l\neq 0
,
the contribution is half, as
\mathcal{C}(k,l)
and

\mathcal{C}(-l,-k)
are swapped by
\sigma
. Thus we have the
formula for
l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)
.

Let us note that
T
has
l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)=0
if and only if the
k
-th row
with
k>0
(resp.
k<0
) has node
1
(resp.
2
,
3
, …,

\ell
). Since
T
is unique,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is connected.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

(1)
The above proof used
6.2
to show that
\sigma
is identity
on
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
. Since the second argument in
6.2

just used
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(2)\cong\operatorname{\rm SL}(2)
, the formula (
6.11
) remains valid
for tableaux of more general shapes, in other words, for arbitrary
framing dimension vectors
{\mathbf{w}}
.

(2) Under the symplectic structure on the tangent space
\bigoplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
,
subspaces
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
and
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(l,k))
are dual to each other, as
they are
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(x_{k},x_{l})
and
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(x_{l},x_{k})
.
(Small remark:
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,k))=0
, hence we may assume
k\neq l
, so

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))
and
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(l,k))
are different, possibly zero,
components.))
The isomorphism

(H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-l,-k)))^{\sigma}\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,l))

appeared during the proof come with the ‘partner’,

(H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(l,k))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-k,-l)))^{\sigma}\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(l,k))
,
hence the
\sigma
-fixed subspace is a symplectic subspace. This checks that

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is symplectic in a naive approach.

6(iii).
K
-matrix

We assume
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
and
\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}>0
for all
i
. We drop
\zeta
from the notation
in quiver varieties.

Let us take
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=2
and two dimensional torus
A=A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
in
\operatorname{GL}(W_{1})
. Let us denote the
coordinates of the Lie algebra of the torus by
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
.

(A).
Wall
u_{1}=u_{2}

We first consider the
R
-matrix, moving from the chamber
\{u_{1}>u_{2}\}
to

\{u_{1}<u_{2}\}
.

We may first take the fixed point set with respect to the diagonal
\Delta{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
given by
u_{1}=u_{2}
.
In this case,
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
is a usual quiver variety of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
with
the framing dimension vector
(2,0,\dots,0)
. The dimension vector
{\mathbf{v}}
is of the form

(2,2,\dots,2,1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0)
, i.e., it is empty otherwise. Moreover, it is the cotangent bundle of the
\ell
-step flags in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, with
dimensions of subspaces specified in this manner. Therefore

T^{*}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
if
1
actually appears in the middle, and a point otherwise.
In order to parametrize fixed points
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{A}
, we can concentrate on the lower part of
the Young tableau:

(6.14)

1

s

2

t

By the above discussion, the corresponding fixed point in contained in a single point component of

({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
if and only if
s=t
.
It is contained in a component of the form
T^{*}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
if and only if
s\neq t
. In that case,
there is another fixed point with the same tableau, but with
s
and
t
exchanged.

The normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{A}
in
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
is

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,-1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,-2))
,
which is
2
-dimensional if
s\neq t
, and
0
-dimensional if
s=t
.
We choose the polarization in the fiber direction of
T^{*}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
for
s\neq t
. Concretely,
it is
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))
if
s<t
, and
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))
if
s>t
.

The normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
in
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
is

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,1))
. Summands
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-2))
,

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,1))
vanish if
s\neq t
, and are
1
-dimensional if
s=t
.
Other four summands are always
1
-dimensional.
We do not fix the polarization of this normal bundle at this stage. We assume that it is
an induced one. Therefore the choice must be consistent for the points corresponding
to
(s,t)
and
(t,s)
.

The computation of the
R
-matrix was done in
[
MO19
, Ex. 4.1.2]
. It gives Yang’s
R
-matrix

R(u)=1-Pu^{-1}
with
P=\sum_{ij}e_{ij}\otimes e_{ji}
up to a normalization factor.
For example, if
\ell=2
, and under the base

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 1

,

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 2

,

\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle 1

,

\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle 2

,

the
R
-matrix is given by

R(u_{1}-u_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{u_{1}-u_{2}}{u_{1}-u_{2}+\hbar}&\frac{\hbar}{u_{1}-u_{2}+\hbar}&0\\
0&\frac{\hbar}{u_{1}-u_{2}+\hbar}&\frac{u_{1}-u_{2}}{u_{1}-u_{2}+\hbar}&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

The sign of
\hbar
is opposite, as our action scales
the cotangent fiber by
{\mathbf{q}}^{2}=\exp(\hbar)
instead of
\exp(-\hbar)
.

This computation is compatible with the identification of the Maulik-Okounkov Yangian with
{\mathsf{Y}}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{gl}}_{\ell})
in this case,
hence the
R
-matrix is the intertwiner for
{\mathbb{C}}^{\ell}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{\ell}
with the coproduct
\Delta
and the opposite
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
.

If
s
does not appear in the
(-2)
-nd row, the dimension vector is
(1,\dots,1)
. Hence
it belongs to a component with
{\mathbf{v}}=(1,\dots,1)
. There are
\ell
such tableaux, given by
the choice
s=1,2,\dots,\ell
. Other tableaux are contained in a single point component of

({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta^{\prime}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
.
Therefore, we can concentrate on the tableaux in the first case.

Let us name the fixed point corresponding to the tableaux with
s=i
by
p_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,\ell
).
It is easy to compute weights of tangent spaces at
p_{i}
:

\displaystyle p_{1}:(-u+\frac{\ell\hbar}{2},u+\frac{(2-\ell)\hbar}{2}),\quad p_{2}:(-u+\frac{(\ell-2)\hbar}{2},u+\frac{(4-\ell)\hbar}{2}),\quad\dots,

\displaystyle\dots,\quad p_{\ell}:(-u+\frac{(2-\ell)\hbar}{2},u+\frac{\ell\hbar}{2}),

where
u=u_{1}+u_{2}
.
There are
(\ell-1)

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s in the exceptional divisor,
\Sigma_{1}
, …,
\Sigma_{\ell-1}
:

\Sigma_{i}
contains
p_{i}
and
p_{i+1}
. There is an additional noncompact divisor
F_{\infty}
passing
through
p_{\ell}
. It is the attracting direction, and the weight of its normal bundle is

-u+\frac{(2-\ell)\hbar}{2}
.

The normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{A}
in
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta^{\prime}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
is

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,1))\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))
,
which is
2
-dimensional if
s
does not appear in the
(-2)
-nd row, and
0
-dimensional otherwise.
We choose the polarization in the repelling direction.
Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{\ell})=[F_{\infty}],\quad\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{\ell-1})=[F_{\infty}]+[\Sigma_{\ell-1}],\dots,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{1})=[F_{\infty}]+[\Sigma_{\ell-1}]+\dots+[\Sigma_{1}].

We have the similar formula for the opposite chamber
-\mathfrak{C}=\{u<0\}
.

The normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{\Delta^{\prime}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}
in
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
is

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-2))\oplus H^{2}(\mathcal{C}(-2,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))
.
We do not fix the polarization at this stage. We assume that it is an induced one.
Therefore the choice must be consistent for points
p_{1}
, …,
p_{\ell}
.

The
(\ell\times\ell)
-entries of the
R
-matrix corresponding to has the following form:

(6.16)

\frac{1}{u+\frac{\ell\hbar}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}u+\frac{(\ell-2)\hbar}{2}&-\hbar&-\hbar&\dots\\
-\hbar&u+\frac{(\ell-2)\hbar}{2}&-\hbar&\dots\\
-\hbar&-\hbar&\ddots&\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}=\operatorname{id}-\frac{\hbar}{u+\frac{\ell\hbar}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&\dots\\
1&1&1&\dots\\
1&1&\ddots&\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}.

It is different from the
R(u_{1}-u_{2})
above in
\ell=2
by sign in off-diagonal entries.
It is because of the choice of the polarization is different.

When we write all the
(\ell^{2}\times\ell^{2})
entries of the
R
-matrix, rows and columns
are indexed by fixed points
({\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma})^{A}
.
Concretely we return back
to Young tableaux (
6.14
), though
(
6.15
) is natural in view of geometry.
The above
(\ell\times\ell)
-entries are
those for
s=t=1,\dots,\ell
.
For example, if
\ell=2
, we have

R^{\bullet\sigma}(u_{1}+u_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{u_{1}+u_{2}}{u_{1}+u_{2}+\hbar}&0&0&\frac{-\hbar}{u_{1}+u_{2}+\hbar}\\
0&1&0&0\\
0&0&1&0\\
\frac{-\hbar}{u_{1}+u_{2}+\hbar}&0&0&\frac{u_{1}+u_{2}}{u_{1}+u_{2}+\hbar}\end{pmatrix}

Up to the shift
u\mapsto u+{\ell\hbar}/2
, this coincides with

R^{t}(-u_{1}-u_{2})=1+Q(u_{1}+u_{2})^{-1}
with
Q=\sum_{ij}e_{ij}\otimes e_{ij}

with

,
which appears in the reflection equation for the twisted Yangian
in
[
Mol07
, §2.2]
.
Note that

is the
R
-matrix for the vector representation
of
\mathrm{Y}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{gl}}_{N})
and its left dual representation, say by the first displayed
equation in the proof of
[
Mol07
, Prop. 1.9.8]
.
The shift
\ell\hbar/2
of the spectral parameter is explained by the
difference of our convention in
5.10
.

(C).
Wall
u_{1}=0

Finally consider the
K
-matrix, moving from the chamber
\{u_{1}>0\}
to
\{u_{1}<0\}
.
The computation is the same for the

-matrix
\{u_{2}>0\}\to\{u_{2}<0\}
, just
by exchanging
u_{1}
and

.

We take the fixed point set with respect to the second
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}
given by

,
which is the union of
\ell
copies of ALE spaces of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
.
We have
\ell
copies from the fixed points with respect to
u_{2}
, which is indexed by
the entry
t
in the
2
-nd row.
We can focus on the
(-1)
-st and
1
-st rows of the Young tableaux, but the
(-1)
-st row
is determined by the
1
-st row anyway. There are
\ell
tableaux for
t=1,\dots,\ell
.
Since the geometry is the same as for
\{u_{1}+u_{2}>0\}\to\{u_{1}+u_{2}<0\}
,
we do not need to repeat the computation. We replace

by

in (
6.16
).
Note that the
K
-matrix has the size
\ell\times\ell
, hence there are no other entries.
We choose the polarization as above, i.e., in the repelling direction of the normal bundle
of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{A}
in
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
, which is

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,1))
.

The analysis of the wall
u_{2}=0
is the same.

We still need to consider a polarization of
the normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
in
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
, which is

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))
.
The summands
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))
, and

are
1
-dimensional if

s\neq t
, and
0
-dimensional if
s=t
.
Their weights, restricted to
\operatorname{Lie}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}
, are
u_{2}
and
-u_{2}
respectively.
On the other hand, the summands

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))
,

are
0
-dimensional if
s\neq t
,
and
1
-dimensional if
s=t
.
Their weights, restricted to
\operatorname{Lie}{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}
, are
u_{2}
and
-u_{2}
respectively.
If the polarization of the normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
in
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}

would satisfy Assumption
5.4
, it must
be constant on connected components of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
.
Note that the polarization of factors
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,2))
are already
fixed, when we discussed the normal bundle of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{A}
in
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{1}}}
.
By the second part of Assumption
5.4
, this part of the polarization
must be independent of
s
.
Hence the polarization in the remaining factor must be either
u_{2}
or
-u_{2}
on each component,
i.e., on each fixed
t
.
Since both
s\neq t
and
s=t
cases
appear in each component, we should choose the polarization as

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))
or
the opposite
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))
on each
t
.

For the wall
\{u_{2}=0\}
, an induced polarization of the normal bundle
of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{1}}}
in
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
would be either

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,-2))\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))
or

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-2,1))\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))
on each component.

These conditions are not compatible with our choice of the polarization
for the wall
\{u_{1}=u_{2}\}
above, as we do not have distinction
between cases
s<t
,
s>t
.
Even if we would change the polarization for the wall
\{u_{1}=u_{2}\}
,
we could not choose induced polarizations simultaneously for

\{u_{1}=0\}
and
\{u_{2}=0\}
walls.
Indeed, suppose we
choose
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))
for the component
of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
given by
t=1
.
In particular, we need to choose
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))
at
s=1
, and

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))
at
s\neq 1
.
Both have weight
-u_{2}
on the wall
\{u_{1}=0\}
, but
-u_{1}-u_{2}

and
u_{1}-u_{2}
on the whole Lie algebra of
A
.
Then considering the component of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{1}}}
,
i.e., moving
t
fixing
s
,
we must choose

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,-1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))
for
s=1
, and

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))
for
s\neq 1
.
In particular, we need to choose

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))
at
s=1
,
t\neq 1
, and

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))
at
s\neq 1
,
t\neq s
.
Now we move
s
from
s=1
,
t\neq 1
fixing
t
. We have to choose

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(-1,2))
for all the components
t\neq 1
.
Thus the choice cannot be consistent on
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(1,2))
vs
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(2,1))

for
s\neq 1
,
t\neq 1
,
s\neq t
.

There is an exceptional case
\ell=2
, as there is no pair
s
,
t
with
\neq 1
,
s\neq t
. Indeed, one define
an induced polarization by the above argument. We can also see a well-defined polarization since

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
is a cotangent bundle when
\ell=2
.
Indeed, for
\ell=2
, the diagram automorphism
\sigma^{\prime}
is trivial, and
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
is an example
discussed in
section

7
. We will prove that the equivariant cohomology
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})
is
a representation of Molev-Ragoucy reflection equation algebra
\mathcal{B}(2,1)
.
The reflection equation algebra is a quotient of the extended reflection equation algebra

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(2,1)

[
Mol07
, §2.16, Example 6]
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(2,1)

is isomorphic to the extended twisted Yangian
\mathrm{X}(\mathfrak{o}_{2})
in the sense of

[
Mol07
, Def. 2.13.1]
by
[
Mol07
, §2.16, Example 6]
. Indeed, one of the defining
relations of
\mathrm{Y}(\mathfrak{o}_{2})
,
[
Mol07
, (2.6)]
, is
not
satisfied
in our case, while the unitarity, which gives the quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(2,1)\to\mathcal{B}(2,1)
,
is satisfied.

For
\ell>2
, we observe that
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
is an ALE space of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
for
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=1
.
Therefore
H_{2}({\mathfrak{L}}^{\sigma})
is
(\ell-1)
-dimensional. We have no non-trivial
(\ell-1)
-dimensional
representation of
\mathfrak{o}_{\ell}
. This is an obstruction to choose an induced polarization,
and well-defined
{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathfrak{o}_{\ell})
representation on
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))
.

6(iv).
\operatorname{\rm SO}
-instanton moduli spaces

The same argument can be applied to type
(+)
case, where we
put an
orthogonal
form on
W_{1}\oplus W_{\ell-1}
. The
\sigma
-quiver variety

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is the moduli space of framed

\operatorname{\rm SO}(2{\mathbf{w}}_{1})
-instantons on an ALE space of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
in
that case. The action of
\sigma
on
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
is

-1
, as we multiply the isomorphism
W_{\ell-1}\cong W_{1}^{*}
by
-1
.
Hence there is no contribution from
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
, and the
dimension of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}-1)
. The torus

A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
fixed points are parametrized by
T
with
(
6.9
). We have
\ell^{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
fixed points in total.

The charge
l_{\operatorname{\rm SO}}(T)
is given
only
by the second term of

l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)
in (
6.11
) because we do not have contribution from

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
. Namely,

(6.17)

l_{\operatorname{\rm SO}}(T)=\frac{1}{2}\#\{(k,l,a)\mid k\neq-l\text{ and \eqref{eq:28} is satisfied}\}.

By this difference, we have more
T
with
l_{\operatorname{\rm SO}}(T)=0
. Therefore
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is
not
connected. Let us
study several examples:

Example 6.18
.

(1) Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=1
, there are
\ell
isolated points. If

\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
is generic, they are points

(x,y,z)=(0,0,-\zeta_{a,{\mathbb{C}}})
(
a=1/2,\dots,\ell-1/2
) in

xy=(z+\zeta_{1/2,{\mathbb{C}}})\dots(z+\zeta_{\ell-1/2,{\mathbb{C}}})
in

6.2
: the involution is
(x,y,z)\mapsto(-x,-y,z)
. Therefore we have isolated fixed points

(x,y,z)=(0,0,-\zeta_{a,{\mathbb{C}}})
(
a=\frac{1}{2},\dots,\ell-\frac{1}{2}
).
Like in the explanation in
6.2
,
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
in this case is
the moduli space of
\operatorname{\rm SO}(2)
-instantons with fixed Chern class and boundary condition at
\infty
.
Since
\operatorname{\rm SO}(2)\cong\operatorname{\rm U}(1)
, it is the moduli space of line bundles. But there is at most
one line bundle with given Chern class and boundary condition at
\infty
.
Therefore
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is a finite set of points. This is a geometric explanation of
the above description.

(2) Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=2
. Let us check that there is one component,
which is an ALE space of type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
, and

\frac{1}{2}\ell(\ell-1)
other components, which are ALE spaces of type

\mathrm{A}_{1}
:
Since the charge
l_{\operatorname{\rm SO}}(T)
of
T
in this case is only the second term in

l_{\operatorname{\rm Sp}}(T)
of (
6.11
), tableaux
T
with
l_{\operatorname{\rm SO}}(T)=0
are

(6.19)

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad(s>t)

which have index
1
.

The first type of a fixed point is connected to the first one in
(
6.19
). This can be checked as follows. We consider the sum of
data for the
1
-st and
(-2)
-nd rows only, as a quiver variety with
dimension vectors
{{\mathbf{w}}}=(1,0,0,1)
,

{{\mathbf{v}}}=(0,0,0,0)
. This quiver variety is an ALE space of
type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
. The fixed points are classified by the same data,
just taking the
1
-st and
(-2)
-nd rows only.
We add the image of data under
\sigma
to see that those fixed points
are connected.

In the same manner, the second type of a fixed point sits in the same
connected component of the second one in (
6.19
). We take the
sum of the
1
-st and
2
-nd rows in this case. The component is an ALE
space of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}
.

In fact, these description give the whole
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
, not only
fixed points, as we know
\dim{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}=2
, and we cover all
fixed points. In particular, for the second type of components,

C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\dots C_{\frac{1}{2}}
and

D_{\frac{1}{2}}\dots D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}
vanish. In fact, when we first
take the sum of
1
-st and
2
-nd, these vanish as there is no
V_{\ell}

in that component. Since a point is the direct sum of this plus its
image under
\sigma
, the assertion follows.

On the other hand, for the first type of components,

C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\dots C_{\frac{1}{2}}
and

D_{\frac{1}{2}}\dots D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}
do
not
vanish generically.

(3) Suppose
\ell=2
. Consider
\zeta
with
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
, but

\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
is a standard one. Then
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}
is the cotangent
bundle of the Grassmannian of
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
-dimensional subspaces in

{\mathbb{C}}^{2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
. The
\sigma
-quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
is the
cotangent bundle of the Grassmannian of maximal isotropic subspaces,
which is known to have
two
connected components. We have two
Young tableaux with
l_{\operatorname{\rm SO}}(T)=0
: (a)
T(k,1)=1
for
k>0
,

=2
for
k<0
and (b)
T(k,1)=1
for
k>1
or
k=-1
,

=2
for
k<-1
or
k=1
.
More generally, connected components of fixed points can be
distinguished by the parity of the number of
1
in
T(k,1)
for

k>0
: if
T(k,1)=T(k^{\prime},1)
for
k>k^{\prime}>0
, we use the analysis
in the above example (2) to conclude that it is contained in the same
connected component of the fixed point for
T^{\prime}
, obtained from
T
by
replacing
T(k,1)
,
T(k^{\prime},1)
by
1
. Repeating this procedure, we arrive at
either
T
of (a) or (b).
The number of
A_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
-fixed points in each component is

2^{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}-1}
:
T(k,1)
for
k=1,\dots,{\mathbf{w}}_{1}-1
is either
1
or

2
. The final entry
T({\mathbf{w}}_{1},1)
is determined by the parity of the
number of
1
in
T(k,1)
in total.

Remark 6.20
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
is even.
Naively
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G^{\sigma}
is
expected
to be the Coulomb
branch of the following type D quiver theory:

1
2
\scriptstyle 2n\!-\!2
\scriptstyle n\!-\!1
n
\ell

where
n={\mathbf{w}}_{1}/2
. Indeed, de Campos Affonso studied
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G^{\sigma}
during
preparing his master’s thesis
[
dCA18
]
, and found that it is
not
irreducible.
This is seen from the above computation of connected components of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
.
The Coulomb branch of the above quiver theory is given by the irreducible component
corresponding to
\prescript{\circ}{}{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
, where
C_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\dots C_{\frac{1}{2}}

and
D_{\frac{1}{2}}\dots D_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}
are invertible generically, as in (2).
We identify the connected component of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
with the deformed Coulomb branch
in a separate publication.
Like in
6.3
, we expect the cohomology of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}
has commuting
actions of twisted Yangian and
\mathfrak{so}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})
.

As in
6.5
, the categorical quotient
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/\!\!/\!\!/G^{\sigma}
is
(
3.18
) when
\ell
is even,
or (
3.19
) when
\ell
is odd.
In this case, there is a different delicate point from
6.5
,
as
G^{\sigma}
is disconnected when
\ell
is even. See
[
Nak25
]
.

6(v).
K
-matrix for type (+)

Let us first suppose
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=2
as in
section

6(iii)
.

Computation of the
R
-matrix for type
(+)
is the same as one in
type
(-)
. It is because we are only concerned with the usual quiver
varieties, and the computation does not depend on the choice of type.

The computation of the
K
-matrix becomes trivial, as the fixed point satisfies

({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
are isolated points in this case. Therefore the corresponding

K
-matrix is the identity matrix.

A crucial difference is about the choice of a polarization.
In type
(-)
, we cannot choose a polarization, which gives an
induced
polarization on
the walls
\{u_{1}=0\}
,
\{u_{2}=0\}
.
This issue disappears in type
(+)
.

Indeed, consider
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
. The entry
t
in the

2
-nd row is fixed on each component of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
. In type
(-)
,
we cannot fix the entry
s
in the
1
-st row, as each component is the type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
ALE
space. However, in type
(+)
,
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma})^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
consists
of isolated points, we can fix
s
as well. In particular, any choice of a polarization
is automatically an induced one. We choose a polarization at walls

\{u_{1}=u_{2}\}
,
\{u_{1}+u_{2}=0\}
as in type
(-)
, and it works
for the remaining walls
\{u_{1}=0\}
,
\{u_{2}=0\}
.

This argument works for
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}\geq 2
as well.
Let us denote the corresponding
\sigma
-quiver variety by
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})
.
Here we fix a decomposition

W=W^{0}\oplus W^{1}\oplus W^{-1}\oplus W^{2}\oplus W^{-2}
and consider
two dimensional torus
A
acting on
W^{1}
,
W^{-1}
,
W^{2}
,
W^{-2}
as
above, and trivially on
W^{0}
.
Connected components of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))^{A}
are divided according to

s
,
t=1,\dots,\ell
according to summands for
W^{1}
,
W^{2}
respectively as above. Together
with connected components of the remaining factor for
W^{0}
, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}-2)
, we have

\pi_{0}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1})^{A})\cong\{(s,t)\mid s,t=1,\dots,\ell\}\times\pi_{0}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}-2))
.
In the fixed point set
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}))^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}}
with respect to the smaller torus
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}
, we fix the entry
t
.
Thus
\pi_{0}(({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}))^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{u_{2}}})\cong\{t=1,\dots,\ell\}\times\pi_{0}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}-1))
.
The normal bundle of a component of
({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}))^{A}
has an additional summands

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(a,0))
,
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(0,a))
for
a=1,2
, but it is irrelevant to
the issue of choosing a well-defined induced polarization: the only issue was
choices between
u_{1}-u_{2}
vs
-u_{1}-u_{2}
.
Then we only need to care summands
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(a,b))
for
a,b=\pm 1
,
\pm 2
.
But they already appeared in the case
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=2
: we choose
arbitrary polarization as above.

Theorem 6.21
.

The equivariant cohomology
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}_{1}))
is a
representation of Olshanski twisted Yangian
\mathrm{Y}(\mathfrak{o}_{\ell})
.

Remark 6.22
.

It is natural to ask which
\sigma
-quiver varieties give rise representations
of the twisted Yangian
\mathrm{Y}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sp}}_{2\ell})
.
As in
6.3
, one should look at Howe duality,
involving
\mathrm{Y}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sp}}_{2\ell})
, or its variant, the coideal subalgebra
{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}_{q}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sp}}_{2\ell})
.
The author could not find it, but
[
ST19
]
provides one in which
the quantum group and the coideal subalgebra are exchanged.
The dual algebra is
{\mathbf{U}}_{q}(\operatorname{\mathfrak{sp}}_{2{\mathbf{w}}_{1}})
, hence we should look at the
Coulomb branch of a quiver gauge theory of type
\mathrm{B}_{{\mathbf{w}}_{1}}
.
This Coulomb branch was studied by de Campos Affonso during preparing his
master’s thesis
[
dCA18
]
. For a certain choice of dimension vectors,
it is a
\sigma
-quiver variety of type
\mathrm{A}_{2\ell-1}

as in (
6.1
), but additional
1
-dimensional framing at the middle vertex.
Like in
4.3
, there is no integral solution
{\mathbf{v}}
of
\mu={\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{v}}=0
.
It means that we need to consider a singular
\sigma
-quiver variety.

6(vi).
Type D and
\mathrm{E}_{6}

Consider moduli spaces of
\operatorname{\rm SO}
-instantons on an ALE space of other types.
In the above argument for the case of type A, it was important that

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
for
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}=1
is a finite set of points. It is because

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}
is the moduli space of
\operatorname{\rm SO}(2)
-instantons. See
6.18
(1).
This explanation remains valid for other types. Concretely, we take
a vertex
i
of the Dynkin diagram, which corresponds to a minuscule weight.
Concretely
i
is the leftmost vertex of type D, or one in two tails of
type D, or the leftmost/rightmost vertex of type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
. We then choose

{\mathbf{w}}
such that the corresponding weight
\lambda
is
\varpi_{i}+\varpi_{{i}^{\star}}
.
We then choose
{\mathbf{v}}
so that the corresponding
\mu
is
0
. We consider the
corresponding
\sigma
-quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
with
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
.
In
[
Li19
, Table 1]
referred in
5.18
, it
corresponds to
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sigma})=(\mathfrak{so}_{2\ell},\mathfrak{\operatorname{\mathfrak{so}}}_{\ell}\oplus\mathfrak{so}_{\ell})
in Satake type DI, and
(E_{6},\mathfrak{sp}_{4})
in Satake type EI.

We can choose polarizations which are induced on
\{u_{1}=0\}
,
\{u_{2}=0\}
walls as above,
hence get a representation of
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
on
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}))
,
where
{\mathbf{w}}
corresponds to
\lambda=N(\varpi_{i}+\varpi_{{i}^{\star}})
for
N=1,2,\dots
.
(
N
here corresponds to
{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
in the above subsections.)

In this case, the
K
-matrix is the identity matrix, as in
section

6(v)
.
The
R
-matrix is the one for the
\ell
-fundamental representation of
{\mathsf{Y}}
of
the underlying Dynkin diagram, i.e., of type D or
\mathrm{E}_{6}
.
As in
section

6(iii)
, we need to compute it in terms of the fixed point
basis, but we leave it as an exercise for the reader.

After the computation, one should compare
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}
for
type D with
i=
the leftmost vertex with the twisted Yangian
in
[
GR16
]
.
Note that twisted Yangian in
[
GR16
]
is classified into two types,
the first and second kinds, according to the
K
-matrix,

\mathcal{G}(u)
in their notation. For type D with
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sigma})=(\mathfrak{so}_{2\ell},\mathfrak{\operatorname{\mathfrak{so}}}_{\ell}\oplus\mathfrak{so}_{\ell})
, it is of the first kind, i.e.,
\mathcal{G}(u)
is a constant matrix
with respect to
u
.
This is consistent with our
K
-matrix, which is the identity matrix.

7.
Examples – partial flags

Let us continue examples of involutions in type
\mathrm{A}_{\ell-1}
, but now
with
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\star=\operatorname{id}
. Therefore the diagram for

\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/G^{\sigma}
in
section

3(ix)
is an
orthosymplectic one. Let us further assume that framing vector spaces
appear only at the leftmost vertex. This class of examples studied in

[
Li19
, §6]
. We will use same techniques as in

section

6
to compute the
K
-matrix explicitly, and show that the
twisted Yangian is the
reflection equation algebra
in

[
Mol07
, §2.16, Example 4]
.

7(i).
Partial flags

Let us label linear maps as in (
6.1
), but without framing at
the rightmost vertex:

(7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\ell-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{1}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\ell-\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{5}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\frac{1}{2}}}

We drop the index
1
from
W_{1}
in this section.
Let us set
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}={\mathbf{w}}
,
{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}=0
for convention.
Our convention for parameters
\zeta
is the same as in
section

6
.

Among the assumption
\sigma(\zeta)=\zeta
,
\sigma({\mathbf{v}})={\mathbf{v}}
,

\sigma({\mathbf{w}})={\mathbf{w}}
, the last one is automatic. (See

3.13
.) The first one means

\zeta_{3/2}=\zeta_{\ell-3/2}
,
\zeta_{5/2}=\zeta_{\ell-5/2}
,
…. Note that we can achieve this condition without violating the
genericity condition. However, the number of deformation parameters is
about half. The middle condition means

(7.2)

{\mathbf{v}}_{0}-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell},\quad{\mathbf{v}}_{1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{2}={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-2}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1},\quad\dots

In other words,
{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}_{i}+{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-i}
for all
i=1,\dots,\ell-1
.

Depending on the type
(+)
,
(-)
, we assign an
\varepsilon
-form on

W
. Thus the involution
\sigma
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is defined.

Let us concentrate on the case
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}=0
, but

\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}>0
. Hence
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is smooth.

The whole quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
was described in

[
Nak94b
, §7]
. It is the cotangent bundle of a (partial) flag
variety of
\operatorname{GL}(W)
. It is the space of pairs

(x\in\operatorname{End}(W),S_{1}\supset S_{2}\supset\dots\supset S_{\ell-1}\supset 0)

satisfying conditions

x(S_{i})\subset S_{i+1}

where
\dim S_{i}={\mathbf{v}}_{i}
. The
\operatorname{GL}(W)
-action is the natural one.
The involution was computed in
[
Li19
, §6]
, and the

\sigma
-quiver variety is the cotangent bundle of the partial flag
variety of classical type. More precisely, it is the space of pairs

x\in\operatorname{End}(W)
and flags
W\supset S_{1}\supset S_{2}\supset\dots\supset S_{\ell-1}\supset 0
as above, with further conditions

x^{*}=-x,\quad S_{i}^{\perp}=S_{\ell-i},

where
*
and
\perp
are defined by the chosen
\varepsilon
-form on
W
.
The
G_{\varepsilon}(W)
-action in
3.21
is identified
with the standard one on the cotangent bundle of the partial flag variety.

The categorical quotient
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}
in

3.20
is an orthosymplectic quiver variety, studied by

[
KP82
]
. If the dominance condition

{\mathbf{v}}_{0}-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\geq{\mathbf{v}}_{1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\geq\dots\geq{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-2}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}\geq{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}

is satisfied,
\mathbf{M}^{\sigma}/G_{\mathbf{v}}^{\sigma}
is isomorphic to the closure
of nilpotent
G_{\varepsilon}(W)
-orbits in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})
corresponding to the
partition given by the above sequence of integers.
Unfortunately the dominance condition and (
7.1
) are
compatible only when

{\mathbf{v}}_{0}-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-2}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}
.
Indeed, the image of
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
under the projective
morphism
\pi
is the nilpotent orbit corresponding to the partition,
which is obtained from

{\mathbf{v}}_{0}-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
,
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, …
{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-2}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
,

{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}
by sorting them in descending order.

Example 7.3
.

As in
section

6
, it is important to understand examples with
small
{\mathbf{w}}
. Let us study
{\mathbf{w}}=1
,
2
, or
3
.

(1) Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}=1
. Only type
(+)
is possible. By the condition
(
7.2
), the only possibility is the case
\ell
is odd and

{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell-1)/2}=1
,

0={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell+1)/2}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
. Indeed,

{\mathbf{w}}-{\mathbf{v}}_{1}
,
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, …,
{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
are
0
or
1
,
the total sum is
1
, and they appear in pairs by (
7.2
).
Thus it is the only possibility.
The corresponding
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is a single point.
Note also the whole quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is a single point in this case.

(2) Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}=2
and the type is
(+)
. We have a decomposition

W=W(1)\oplus W(-1)
as before by the eigenvalue of

t\in G_{\varepsilon}^{0}(W)=\operatorname{\rm SO}(2)={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. There are only two

1
-dimensional isotropic subspaces, namely
W(1)
and
W(-1)
. The
linear map
x\in\operatorname{End}(W)
is automatically
0
as it is in

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\varepsilon}(W)
and nilpotent. Thus
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is either
empty, a single point or two points. It is a single point if and
only if
\ell
is odd and
{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell-1)/2}=2
,

0={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell+1)/2}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
.
It is two points if and only if

{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{i-1}=2
,

1={\mathbf{v}}_{i}={\mathbf{v}}_{i+1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-i}
,

0={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell+1-i}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
for some

i\leq\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor
.
The whole quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is also a single point
in the case
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is a single point,
and
T^{*}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in the case
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is two points.

(3) Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}=2
and the type is
(-)
. Contrary to the case (2)
above, arbitrary
1
-dimensional subspace is lagrangian in the
symplectic vector space
W
. The condition
x^{*}=-x
simply says

x
is trace-free, but it is automatically satisfied if
x
is
nilpotent. The possible dimension vectors giving nonempty

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
are the same as the case (2), but we get

T^{*}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in the case when
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is a two point above.
The whole quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is the same as
{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
,
in other words,
\sigma
is a trivial involution.

(4) Suppose
{\mathbf{w}}=3
and the type is
(+)
. As in the case (1),

\ell
is odd and
{\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell-1)/2}-{\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell+1)/2}
is either

3
or
1
. In the former case,

{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell-1)/2}=3
,

0={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell+1)/2}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
. The corresponding

{\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is a point. The whole quiver variety

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is also a point in this case.

In the latter case,

3={\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{i-1}
,

2={\mathbf{v}}_{i}={\mathbf{v}}_{i+1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell-1)/2}
,

1={\mathbf{v}}_{(\ell+1)/2}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-i}
,

0={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell+1-i}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\ell-1}
for some

i\leq(\ell-1)/2
. We get
T^{*}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in this case.
The whole quiver variety
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is the cotangent bundle
of the full flag variety for
{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
.

7(ii).
Torus action

As in

section

6(ii)
, we decompose
W
into
1
-dimensional spaces:

W=W(1)\oplus W(-1)\oplus W(2)\oplus W(-2)\oplus\dots.

The final part of
\dots
is
W({\mathbf{w}}/2)\oplus W(-{\mathbf{w}}/2)
if
{\mathbf{w}}
is
even. If
{\mathbf{w}}
is odd, we continue until

W(({\mathbf{w}}-1)/2)\oplus W(-({\mathbf{w}}-1)/2)
and add one more summand
W(0)
. We
then require
W(k)\cong W(-k)^{*}
under the
\varepsilon
-form, which remains to be true for
k=0
.

Let us take a maximal torus
A_{\mathbf{w}}
of
G_{\varepsilon}(W)
preserving the above
decomposition. Note that
A_{\mathbf{w}}
is
{\mathbf{w}}/2
-dimensional
if
{\mathbf{w}}
is even, while
({\mathbf{w}}-1)/2
dimensional if
{\mathbf{w}}
is odd.

The torus fixed points are parametrized by Young tableaux as
before. The differences are followings:

•

All rows have length
1
,

•

The cardinarity of the nodes with
i
is
{\mathbf{v}}_{i-1}-{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
.

•

If the entry of
k
-th row is
s
, the entry of
(-k)
-th row is

\ell+1-s
. In particular, the only possible entry of the
0
-th row is

s=(\ell+1)/2
.

For example,

-2

5

-1

4

1

2

2

1

-2

5

-1

4

0

3

1

2

2

1

for
{\mathbf{w}}=4
,
\ell=5
and
{\mathbf{w}}=5
,
\ell=5
respectively.

Thanks to
7.3
, we can compute
\sigma
on the complex

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
, and it is the identity map for
(-)
-type,
and
-\operatorname{id}
for
(+)
-type.
In case both entries of
k
and
-k
-th rows are
(\ell+1)/2
,

H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(k,-k))
is
0
anyway, hence it does not matter
which sign we take.

7(iii).
K
-matrix

Let us compute the
K
-matrix in this example.

Since
{\mathfrak{M}}({\mathbf{w}})
is the cotangent bundle of a partial flag variety,
we can choose a polarization as the cotangent directions.
It is automatically an induced polarization.

We consider the case
{\mathbf{w}}=4
or
5
(type
(+)
only). We take a maximal torus
A_{\mathbf{w}}

in
G_{\varepsilon}(W)
, which is two dimensional torus in all cases.
The Young tableaux are

-2

s_{2}

-1

s_{1}

0

\scriptstyle{\frac{\ell+1}{2}}

1

t_{1}

2

t_{2}

with
s_{1}+t_{1}=s_{2}+t_{2}=\ell+1
when
{\mathbf{w}}=5
. If
{\mathbf{w}}=4
, we omit
the
0
-th row. In particular, the number of fixed points is always
\ell^{2}
.

The
R
-matrix for
u_{1}=u_{2}
is the same as the one in

section

6(iii)
(A)
, as we only need to consider
the same quiver varieties.
Let us decide to write the
K
-matrix as a matrix with rows and columns
indexed by

\scriptstyle t_{1}

\scriptstyle t_{2}

as before.

The
R
-matrix for
u_{1}=-u_{2}
is also the same as the one in

section

6(iii)
(A)
with
u_{1}-u_{2}
replaced by
u_{1}+u_{2}
,
as the geometry is the same.
There is no shift of spectral parameter unlike
section

6(iii)
(B)
.
See
5.10
.
However, there is one caveat: the natural index set is for

\scriptstyle s_{1}

\scriptstyle t_{2}

.
We want to write the
R
-matrix as the same matrix with
R(u_{1}+u_{2})
,

(1+P\hbar(u_{1}+u_{2})^{-1})(1+\hbar(u_{1}+u_{2})^{-1})
.
Then we will write the
K
-matrix from a vector space with basis indexed
by

\scriptstyle t_{1}

to
another
vector space with basis indexed by

\scriptstyle s_{1}

.

In order to illustrate how
K
-matrix looks like, let us
consider the case
{\mathbf{w}}=2
of type
(+)
. Then all varieties involved
are points, hence entries of the
K
-matrix is either
0
or
1
.
By the above discussion, the
K
-matrix is the anti-identity matrix:

[formula]

[formula]

1

\dots

\ell

1

0

\dots

1

\vdots

\vdots

\iddots

\vdots

\ell

1

\dots

0

Note that this matrix is conjugate to
\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,-1,\dots,-1)
,
where
1
appears
\lceil\frac{\ell}{2}\rceil
times and
-1
appears

\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\rfloor
times.
Thus the extended twisted Yangian, more precisely the version with
fixed
i=1
in
2.21
, is the
Molev-Ragoucy reflection equation algebra
\mathcal{B}(\ell,\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\rfloor)
.
(See
[
Mol07
, §2.16, Example 4]
and
[
MR02
]
.)

If
{\mathbf{w}}=2
with type
(-)
, or
{\mathbf{w}}=3
with type
(+)
,
the
K
-matrix has nontrivial
(i,j)
entries if
j=i
or
j=\ell+1-i

except
i=j=(\ell+1)/2
. Those nontrivial entries are the same as
the ones in
section

6(iii)
(A)
with
u_{1}-u_{2}
replaced by

2u_{1}
.
The reflection equation algebra is alternatively defined by
the reflection equation and the unitary condition. The latter holds
thanks to
section

5(iii)
. Therefore the extended twisted Yangian,
the version with fixed
i=1
in
5.16
(4), is again the reflection equation algebra.
Nevertheless the associated embedding
{\mathsf{X}}^{\mathrm{tw}}\to{\mathsf{X}}
is different
from the one for
{\mathbf{w}}=2
with type
(+)
above.

From these analysis, we get the following for general
{\mathbf{w}}
.

Theorem 7.4
.

The equivariant cohomology
H^{*}_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{w}}}({\mathfrak{M}}^{\sigma}({\mathbf{w}}))
is a representation
of the Molev-Ragoucy reflection equation algebra
\mathcal{B}(\ell,\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}\rfloor)
.

Appendix A
ADHM description of instantons on ALE spaces for classical groups

When a gauge group is a unitary group, instantons on ALE spaces have a
description in terms of quiver representations
[
KN90
]
. It is a
modification of ADHM description
[
ADHM78
]
of instantons on

S^{4}
. (It followed more closely the presentation in
[
DK90
]
.)
The latter has a version for
\operatorname{\rm SO}/\operatorname{\rm Sp}
gauge groups, as an

\operatorname{\rm SO}/\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instanton can be considered as a unitary instanton
A

together with an involutive isomorphism between the dual instanton and
the original instanton
A^{*}\cong A
. More precisely we take ADHM
description for both
A
and
A^{*}
, and assign an isomorphism between
two descriptions. This was explained already in
[
ADHM78
]
, and has
been well-known in gauge theory context. See
[
Buc86
]
for
instantons on
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}P}^{2}
. It is discussed e.g. in

[
Cho16
]
in the presentation in
[
DK90
]
.

When
[
KN90
]
was written, description of
\operatorname{\rm SO}/\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons on
ALE spaces was
not
known, as the description of the dual
instanton
A^{*}
was not given.
It was mentioned in the third paragraph of
[
KN90
, p. 266]
.
More precisely, the description in

[
KN90
]
involves the parameter
\zeta
for the level of the
hyperkähler moment map, and we are forced to change
\zeta
to

-\zeta
when we describe
A^{*}
.
Thus we need an isomorphism from description for
\zeta
to one for

-\zeta
. Such an isomorphism was found later as the reflection functor
S_{w_{0}}

corresponding to the longest element
w_{0}
in the finite Weyl group

[
Nak03
, §9]
.

However, the ADHM description of
\operatorname{\rm SO}/\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons on ALE spaces was

not
discussed in
[
Nak03
, §9]
, as the motivation there was different.
Let us explain it in this appendix.
No new input other than
[
KN90
,
Nak03
]
is necessary, so we just
state the result without a proof. It is also good to look at

[
Nak16
, App. A.4]
where ADHM description of

\operatorname{\rm SO}/\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}/\Gamma
is explained.
It can be considered as a degenerate case of the discussion below
where the reflection functor
S_{w_{0}}
becomes the identity.

A(i).
ADHM description of dual instantons

Let
(I,H)
be the McKay quiver for
\Gamma
, namely
I
is the set of
isomorphism classes of irreducible representations of
\Gamma
, and

H
is the set of arrows where we draw
a_{ij}
arrows from
i
to
j

for
a_{ij}=\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\rho_{i},\rho_{j}\otimes Q)
, where
Q
is
the
2
-dimensional representation of
\Gamma
given by the inclusion

\Gamma\subset\operatorname{\rm SU}(2)
.
Contrary to the assumption in
section

3
, our quiver is of affine type.
However, the finite type quiver was obtained by removing the
0
-th vertex corresponding to the trivial
representation
\rho_{0}
in
section

3(ii)
, hence the explanation
in
section

3
is still valid.
We choose an orientation
\Omega
of
H
,
which is a division
H=\Omega\sqcup\overline{\Omega}
, where

\overline{h}
is the arrow with the opposite direction to
h
.
We choose it as in
section

3(vii)
, where we choose a cyclic orientation,
instead of a linear orientation, in affine type A.

We take
\zeta=(\zeta_{i})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{3})^{I}
, the data for an ALE space

X_{\zeta}
asymptotic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}/\Gamma
at infinity. It sits in the
level
0
hyperplane

0=\zeta\cdot\delta=\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}\dim\rho_{i}
, where
\delta
is
the positive primitive imaginary root of the corresponding affine Lie
algebra.

Let us take an
\operatorname{\rm U}(n)
framed instanton
A
on
X_{\zeta}
. We have the
corresponding ADHM description
[
KN90
]
. Namely the quiver variety

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is the moduli space of framed
instantons on
X_{\zeta}
. Here the superscript “reg” means that we take
the open subset of the quiver variety consisting of free orbits of the
gauge group
\prod_{i\in I}\operatorname{\rm U}(V_{i})
.
Furthermore, we can replace the hyperKähler quotient by the complex symplectic
quotient with the stability condition given by
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
, hence the quiver
variety is one discussed in
section

3
.
(
B_{h}
was denoted by
B_{i,j}
, and
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
were denoted by

i_{k}
,
j_{k}
in
[
KN90
]
.)

The description of
[
KN90
]
uses the tautological bundle

{\mathscr{R}}
, which decomposes as

\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\mathscr{R}}_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}^{*}
.
Reflection functors in
[
Nak03
]
are understood as
isomorphisms between different descriptions of instanton moduli spaces
for different choices of
{\mathscr{R}}
. In particular, the reflection
functor
S_{w_{0}}
for the longest element
w_{0}
composed with the
diagram automorphism
\star

*\circ S_{w_{0}}=S_{w_{0}}\circ*\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{w_{0}{\zeta}^{\star}}^{\mathrm{reg}}(w_{0}\star{{\mathbf{v}}}^{\star},{{\mathbf{w}}}^{\star})

corresponds to the dual bundle

{\mathscr{R}}^{*}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\mathscr{R}}_{i}^{*}\otimes\rho_{i}
as
shown in
[
Nak03
, 9(iii)]
.
Note that
S_{w_{0}}
and
\star
do
not
touch the vertex
0

corresponding to the trivial representation, hence the component

{\mathbf{v}}_{0}
,
{\mathbf{w}}_{0}
are unchanged.

The dual instanton
A^{*}
is given by the transpose
t
in
section

3(iii)

t\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{-\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}).

Recall that
{\mathfrak{M}}_{-\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is associated with
W^{*}
.

Composing two isomorphisms, we get

S_{w_{0}}\circ\star\circ t\colon{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}(w_{0}\star{{\mathbf{v}}}^{\star},{{\mathbf{w}}}^{\star}),

where we have used
-w_{0}{\zeta}^{\star}=\zeta
.

A(ii).
\operatorname{\rm SO}/\operatorname{\rm Sp}
instantons

Let us take
\operatorname{\rm SO}(n)
or
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(n)
framed instanton
A
on

X_{\zeta}
. We regard it as a
\operatorname{\rm U}(n)
framed instanton
A
together
with an isomorphism
A\cong A^{*}
compatible with the framing.
Since framed instantons have no nontrivial automorphisms, the
isomorphism
A\cong A^{*}
is unique if it exists. Hence moduli spaces
of
\operatorname{\rm SO}(n)/\operatorname{\rm Sp}(n)
framed instantons are fixed point loci in
moduli spaces of
\operatorname{\rm U}(n)
instantons with respect to the involution
given by
A\mapsto A^{*}
. Here moduli spaces are constructed by a gauge
theoretic method as in
[
Nak90
]
.

Recall that the framing of
A
is an approximate isomorphism of
A

and a flat connection on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}/\Gamma
at infinity. The flat
connection corresponds to a
\Gamma
-module

\bigoplus_{i}W_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}
.
Its dual representation is

\bigoplus W_{i}^{*}\otimes\rho_{{i}^{\star}}=\bigoplus W_{{i}^{\star}}^{*}\otimes\rho_{i}
.
We choose an isomorphism
W_{i}\cong W^{*}_{{i}^{\star}}
as in
section

3(vi)
.
Here we choose
\sigma^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
, and determine
\varepsilon_{i}
as in
section

3(vi)
,
according to a choice of type
(+)
or
(-)
.
We choose
(+)
if we want to describe
\operatorname{\rm SO}
-instantons, and
(-)
for
\operatorname{\rm Sp}
-instantons.
The rule for the choice of
\varepsilon_{i}
is determined so that the isomorphism

\bigoplus W_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}\cong\bigoplus W_{i}^{*}\otimes\rho_{{i}^{\star}}
is given by
an orthogonal form in type
(+)
or a symplectic form in type
(-)
.
Thus we fix an isomorphism of the flat connection and its dual in advance.

Being an
\operatorname{\rm SO}(n)
or
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(n)
instanton, a
\operatorname{\rm U}(n)
instanton must have
vanishing first Chern class. Hence we have
w_{0}\star{{\mathbf{v}}}^{\star}={\mathbf{v}}
by
[
KN90
, §9]
.
in the last paragraph of
section

A(i)
.

Now
\sigma=S_{w_{0}}\circ\star\circ t
is defined as an involution on

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

Theorem A.1
.

A moduli space of
\operatorname{\rm SO}(n)
or
\operatorname{\rm Sp}(n)
framed instantons on

X_{\zeta}
is isomorphic to the fixed point locus

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})^{\sigma}
as a hyperkähler manifold.

Dimension vectors
{\mathbf{v}}
,
{\mathbf{w}}
are determined by the second Chern
class and the framing considered as
\operatorname{\rm U}(n)
instantons.

A(iii).
Extension to partial compactification

Recall that
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is defined as
the open subset consisting of free
\prod\operatorname{\rm U}(V_{i})
-orbits. We have a
larger space
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
by dropping the freeness
condition. It is Uhlenbeck’s partial compactification of the moduli
space of framed instantons on
X_{\zeta}
.

The isomorphism
\sigma
extends to

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
as a homeomorphism. This is because the
reflection functor is defined on the larger space, and
t
,
*

clearly extend. It is also clear in the gauge theoretic construction
of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
If we use algebro-geometric construction of

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
via geometric invariant theory, we can
endow
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
with a structure of a
quasiprojective variety. Then the extension of

\sigma
is an involution on a variety.

Note that
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
has another
partial compactification as a moduli space of framed torsion free
sheaves on
X_{\zeta}
. The corresponding ADHM description is given as
follows. (See
[
Nak07
]
for detail.) We take an algebro-geometric
description of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
. We
decompose the parameter
\zeta
to complex and real parts
\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
,

\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
, impose the complex moment map equation involving only

\zeta_{\mathbb{C}}
. We have the group action of
\prod\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
on the
solution space. We take the quotient of the
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
-semistable
locus by the S-equivalence relation. Thus

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\cong H^{\mathrm{ss}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}},\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}/\!\sim,

where
H^{\mathrm{ss}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}},\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}
is the open subset of

\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
-semistable points in the solution space of the complex
moment map equation. See
[
Nak03
, Prop. 2.11]
.

Recall that
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
lives on the level 0 hyperplane

\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\delta=0
. Then we take
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
near

\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
with
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}\cdot\delta<0
. Then we define

{\mathfrak{M}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{C}},\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime})}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
as the quotient of the

\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
-semistable (equivalently
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
-stable) locus by
the action of
\prod\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})
.
Then
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
-semistability implies
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}
-semistability by
our choice, that is
H^{\mathrm{ss}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime},\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}\subset H^{\mathrm{ss}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}},\zeta_{\mathbb{C}})}
. It induces a morphism

{\mathfrak{M}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{C}},\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime})}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}),

which is an isomorphism on

{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
. Therefore

{\mathfrak{M}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{C}},\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime})}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is another partial
compactification of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{reg}}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.

Note that
-w_{0}(\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime})^{*}\neq\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
as they live in the
opposite side of the level 0 hyperplane
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\delta=0
.
Therefore
\sigma
does not define an involution
on
{\mathfrak{M}}_{(\zeta_{\mathbb{C}},\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime})}({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
.
